Stably diffeomorphic manifolds and the realisation of modified surgery obstructions
Anthony Conway, Diarmuid Crowley, Mark Powell, Joerg Sixt

Abstract
. For every k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 we construct infinitely many 4 ​ k 4k -dimensional manifolds that are all stably diffeomorphic but pairwise not homotopy equivalent. Each of these manifolds has hyperbolic intersection form and is stably parallelisable. In fact we construct infinitely many such infinite sets. To achieve this we prove a realisation result for appropriate subsets of Kreck’s modified surgery monoid ℓ 2 ​ q + 1 ​ ( ℤ ​ [ π ] ) \ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]) , analogous to Wall’s realisation of the odd-dimensional surgery obstruction L L -group L 2 ​ q + 1 s ​ ( ℤ ⁡ [ π ] ) L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]) .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 57R65, 57R67.

1.
Introduction

Let
W_{g}:=\mathbin{\#}_{g}(S^{q}\times S^{q})
be the
g
-fold connected sum of
S^{q}\times S^{q}
with itself.
Two compact, connected, smooth
2q
-manifolds
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
with the same Euler characteristic are
stably diffeomorphic
if there exists a nonnegative integer
g
and a diffeomorphism

M_{0}\mathbin{\#}W_{g}\to M_{1}\mathbin{\#}W_{g}.

We write
M_{0}\cong_{\text{st}}M_{1}
.
Note that
S^{q}\times S^{q}
admits an orientation reversing diffeomorphism for every
q
, using the antipodal map on one factor for
q
even, and switching the factors for
q
odd. Hence the same
is true of
W_{g}
and it follows that when the
M_{i}
are orientable the diffeomorphism type of the
connected sum does not depend on a choice of orientations.

An effective procedure for classifying
2q
-dimensional manifolds
M
,
pioneered by Hambleton and Kreck in dimension
4

[
HK88a
,
HK88b
,
HK93b
,
HK93a
]
and systematised by Kreck
[
Kre99
]
in dimensions
2q\geq 4
, is to first classify manifolds up to stable diffeomorphism and then for a fixed manifold
M
to understand its
stable class
, which is the set of diffeomorphism classes

\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(M):=\{N\mid N\cong_{\text{st}}M\}/\text{diffeomorphism}.

The literature on stably diffeomorphic manifolds contains work on the stable classification of 4-manifolds, that is enumerating and detecting the possible stable classes, including
[
CHR95
,
Spa03
,
KLPT17
,
HH19
,
KPT20
]
.
There is also work showing that, under additional assumptions, a stable diffeomorphism type contains a unique diffeomorphism class
[
HKT09
,
CS11
,
Kha17
]
.
In addition Davis gave conditions under which the homotopy type of a
4
-manifold
M
contains a unique stable class
[
Dav05
]
.

In this paper, for
k\geq 2
we present new general methods to construct stably diffeomorphic manifolds, examples of
4k
-manifolds with infinite stable classes, and new invariants which distinguish stably diffeomorphic manifolds.
Before stating our main results we briefly review the history of the stable class.

If
w_{1}=w_{1}(M)
is the orientation character of
M
, then classical surgery theory
provides an action of the surgery obstruction group

L^{s}_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)],w_{1})
on
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(M)
. This action can give rise to
infinite stable classes, such as for
L\times S^{1}
, where
L
is a
(2q{-}1)
-dimensional lens space
[
Wei99
]
, and for
\mathbb{RP}^{2n}\mathbin{\#}\mathbb{RP}^{2n}

[
BDK07
]
.
However the action of
L^{s}_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)],w_{1})
necessarily preserves the homotopy type of
M
,
and in the simply-connected case
L_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z})
is trivial.
We will investigate the stable class of
M
beyond the action of the classical

L
-group and so we define the
homotopy stable class
of
M
,

\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h}(M_{0})=\{M_{1}\mid M_{1}\cong_{\text{st}}M_{0}\}/\text{homotopy equivalence},

which is the set of homotopy types within the stable class of
M_{0}
, and is thus a quotient of
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(M_{0})
.

As Kreck and Schafer point out
[
KS84
, I]
,
examples of smooth
(4k{-}1)
-connected
8k
-manifolds
M
with arbitrarily large homotopy class
have been implicit in the literature since Wall’s classification of these manifolds up to
the action of exotic spheres
[
Wal62
]
. These examples are distinguished by
their intersection forms
\lambda^{\mathbb{Z}}_{M}\colon H_{2k}(M;\mathbb{Z})\times H_{2k}(M;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}
.
Kreck and Schafer themselves provided examples of
4k
-manifolds
M
with nontrivial
finite fundamental groups where the homotopy stable class of
M
contained distinct
elements with isometric intersection forms and even isometric equivariant intersection forms.

In
[
CCPS21
]
we gave the first examples of simply-connected
4k
-manifolds, for
k\geq 2
, with hyperbolic intersection form and arbitrarily large stable class. In contrast to Kreck and Schafer, we exhibited these examples in infinitely many stable classes. However as far as we know all of these stable classes are finite. While this paper is a sequel to
[
CCPS21
]
, extending the results there to infinite homotopy stable classes, the two papers
can be read independently.

1.1.
Manifolds with infinite homotopy stable class

Our main theorem asserts the existence, for every
k\geq 2
, of infinitely many mutually non stably diffeomorphic
4k
-manifolds,
each of which has
infinite homotopy stable class
.
We start with relatively simple manifolds: given
i>0
we set
p=p(i):=-i(2k)!
and
q=q(i):=1-4p^{2}
,
and take the simply-connected
4k
-manifold
N_{pq,1}
,
from
[
CCPS21
, Theorem 1.1]
, which is reviewed in Section
9.1
.
As
i
varies, our starting manifolds are the connected sums

M_{i}:=N_{pq,1}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}).

All of the
4k
-manifolds we construct, like the examples of
[
KS84
]
,
have isomorphic
intersection forms and isomorphic equivariant intersection forms.
They are also all
stably parallelisable

because
N_{pq,1}
is stably parallelisable, the connected sum of a manifold with a stably parallelisable manifold is stably parallelisable if and only if the original manifold is stably parallelisable. Thus each
M_{i}
and any manifold stably diffeomorphic to it is stably parallelisable.
It follows that one cannot use stable characteristic classes to distinguish these manifolds.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix an integer
k\geq 2
. There is a closed, connected oriented, stably parallelisable, smooth
4k
-manifold
M
with

\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
|\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h}(M)|=\infty
.
Indeed, there are infinitely many stable diffeomorphism classes of such manifolds,

\{[M_{i}]_{\rm st}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
such that each
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h}(M_{i})
contains an infinite subset
\{M^{j}_{i}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
, where
M^{0}_{i}=M_{i}
and
each
M^{j}_{i}
has hyperbolic intersection form and hyperbolic equivariant intersection form.

Moreover,
M_{i}^{j}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}\cong M_{i}^{j^{\prime}}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}
for all pairs
\{j,j^{\prime}\}
,
but
M_{i}^{j}
is homotopy equivalent to a connected sum
N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
with
N
a simply-connected

4k
-manifold if and only if
j=0
.

To construct the manifolds
M_{i}^{j}
,
we generalise the surgery procedure underlying
the Wall Realisation of classical surgery obstructions in
L_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M_{i})])\cong L_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])

to the setting of modified surgery; see Theorem
1.3
below.
When applied to
M_{i}
this procedure constructs new elements of the stable class of
M_{i}
,
which are related to
M_{i}
by a bordism with
a given modified surgery obstruction lying in a certain subset of the modified surgery monoid

\ell_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
.
We prove Theorem
1.1
by finding infinitely many elements of

\ell_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
and proving that their realisation produces manifolds
M_{i}^{j}
spanning infinitely many homotopy types.

To distinguish the manifolds
M_{i}^{j}
, we identify
\pi_{1}(M_{i}^{j})=\mathbb{Z}

and then compactly supported cohomology of
the universal cover of
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-module given by

H^{*}(M_{i}^{j};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
for
*=2,\dots,2k{-}2,2k{+}2,\dots,4k-2
, with

H^{2k}(M_{i}^{j};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]^{2}
and all odd degree cohomology trivial.
Moreover, for any generator
z\in H^{2}(M_{i}^{j};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
, the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-module generated
by
z^{k}\in H^{2k}(M_{i}^{j};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]^{2}
is a summand.
The homotopy invariant we use arises by considering the primitive submodule
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]z^{k}\subset H^{2k}(M_{i}^{j};\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}])
up to the isometries of the hyperbolic intersection pairing and automorphisms of
\pi_{1}(M_{i}^{j})
;
see Remark
9.9
.

1.2.
Realising modified surgery obstructions

Since our realisation results apply generally,
we expand our discussion to consider smooth manifolds of dimension

2q
, for
q\geq 3
, recall some classical surgery theory, and explain the tools we have developed in terms of the analogy between classical and modified surgery. We will also mention when our results extend to dimension
2q=4
, in the topological category.

One aim of surgery theory is to decide whether two compact, smooth
n
-dimensional manifolds
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are diffeomorphic.
To describe the surgery program in the briefest terms, assume that
\partial M_{0}\cong\partial M_{1}
,
that
f\colon M_{0}\to M_{1}
is a homotopy equivalence, and that
(W^{n{+}1};M_{0},M_{1})

is an oriented cobordism rel. boundary.
Classical surgery, as developed by Browder, Novikov, Sullivan, and Wall, associates an obstruction
\sigma(F)\in L_{n+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M_{0})])
to a degree one normal bordism
(F,\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits,f)\colon(W;M_{0},M_{1})\to(M_{0}\times[0,1];M_{0},M_{0})
.
This obstruction vanishes if, and for
n{+}1\geq 5
only if,
(W,F)
is normally bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism. If this holds, then for
n\geq 5
, by the
s
-cobordism theorem,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are diffeomorphic.

If
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are
2q
-dimensional, then the aforementioned surgery obstruction is (an equivalence class of) a
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic formation, i.e. a triple
((P,\psi);F,G)
, where
(P,\psi)
is a
(-1)^{q}
-quadratic form, and
F,G
are lagrangians of
(P,\psi)
.
A classical result in surgery theory is
Wall Realisation

[
Wal99
, Theorem 6.5]
.
We restrict to the oriented case here, since we shall do so throughout this article.

Theorem 1.2

(Wall Realisation)
.

Let
M_{0}
be a compact, connected, oriented, smooth
2q
-dimensional manifold with
q\geq 3
.
Given
x\in L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M_{0})])
, there exists a degree one normal bordism rel. boundary

(W^{2q+1};M_{0},M_{1})\to(M_{0}\times[0,1];M_{0}\times\{0\},M_{0}\times\{1\})

whose surgery obstruction equals
x
.

The same holds for
q=2
for topological manifolds if
\pi_{1}(M_{0})
is a good group
[
FQ90
]
.
In Theorem
1.3
below, we present
an analogue of Theorem
1.2
for
Kreck’s
modified surgery theory
[
Kre99
]
.
The starting point of modified surgery theory is to weaken the homotopy theoretic
assumption that
M_{0}^{2q}
and
M_{1}^{2q}
are homotopy equivalent
and to strengthen the bundle-theoretic input as follows.
First we fix a fibration
\xi\colon B\to BO
. A
normal
j
-smoothing
over
(B,\xi)

is a pair
(M,\overline{\nu})
, where
M
is a compact, smooth manifold,
and
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is a
(j{+}1)
-connected map that lifts the classifying map

\nu_{M}\colon M\to BO
of the stable normal bundle.

We assume that
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are connected, oriented, and admit normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings into
(B,\xi)
,
which together bound a normal
(B,\xi)
-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
.
By
[
Kre99
, Theorem 4]
there is a
modified surgery obstruction

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]),

where
\pi:=\pi_{1}(B)
and
the
\ell
-monoid

\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
consists of certain equivalences classes of quasi-formations.
As we recall in Section
2
, a
quasi-formation
is a triple
((P,\psi);F,V)
,
where for
\varepsilon=(-1)^{q}
,
(P,\psi)
is an
\varepsilon
-quadratic form over
the ring
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
,
F
is a lagrangian of
(P,\psi)

and
V\subseteq P
is a half rank direct summand. The
induced form
of a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
is the

\epsilon
-quadratic form
(V,\theta)
obtained by restricting
\psi
to
V
.

When
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=[(P,\psi);F,V]
, we now explain how the induced form
(V,\theta)

relates to the topology of
(W,\overline{\nu})
.
The kernel group
K\pi_{q}(M_{0}):=\ker\bigl(\overline{\nu}_{0*}\colon\pi_{q}(M_{0})\to\pi_{q}(B)\bigr)

of the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
, is a finitely
generated
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module, and intersections and self-intersections endow it
with the structure of a quadratic form
(K\pi_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
, called the
Wall form
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
If
S
is the finitely generated free
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
-module on a set of generators for
K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
,
then the
0
-stabilised Wall form
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is

[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}:=[S,\mu]_{0},

where
\mu
is the
\varepsilon
-quadratic form induced on
S
and
[S,\mu]_{0}
indicates the
0
-stable isometry
class of a form, as defined in Section
2.3
.
By
[
Kre99
, Proposition 8]
,
[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}
is well-defined and
[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}=[V,\theta]_{0}
.

We can now state our realisation result for modified surgery obstructions, which is the second
main result of this paper; (see Theorem
3.10
for a more detailed version
of Theorem
1.3
).

Theorem 1.3

(Realisation of modified surgery obstructions)
.

For
q\geq 3
, let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing and
let
x=((P,\psi);F,V)

be a quasi-formation such that
[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}=[V,\theta]_{0}
.
Then there exists a normal
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
with

\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and surgery obstruction

[\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})]=[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]).

Again, the same result holds for
q=2
, provided we are in the topological category and
\pi
is a good group.

Remark 1.4
.

The condition
[V,\theta]_{0}=[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}
in Theorem
1.3
cannot be avoided:
by
[
Kre99
, Proposition 8]
, it is a necessary condition for a quasi-formation
to be realised under the hypotheses of the theorem.
Theorem
1.3
is in this sense the best possible realisation result.

Given a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
whose
0
-stabilised induced form is isomorphic to the
0
-stabilised Wall form of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
, Theorem
3.10
produces another
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})=\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})
.
Since
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
are normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings over

(B,\xi)
,
[
Kre99
, Corollary 3]
ensures that
M_{0}
and

M_{1}
are stably diffeomorphic.

With
q=2k
, Theorem
1.3
gives rise to a procedure to realise elements of
\ell_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])\cong\ell_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M_{0})])
by a bordism based on a
4k
-manifold
M_{0}
, as alluded to after the statement of Theorem
1.1
. The manifold at the other end of such a bordism is an element in
the same stable class as
M_{0}
.

Remark 1.5
.

Theorem
1.3
can be re-stated as follows.
We define a
(2q{+}1)
-dimensional modified surgery problem

(W,\xi)
to be a normal

(B,\xi)
-bordism from a fixed
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
to some
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})
and let

\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0}):=\bigl\{\,[W,\overline{\nu}]\,\mid\,\text{$\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})$,
$\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})$ is a $(q{-}1)$-smoothing}\bigr\}

be the set of rel. boundary bordism classes of

(2q{+}1)
-dimensional modified surgery problems over
(B,\xi)
with
incoming boundary
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and outgoing boundary a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.
We have seen that there is a well-defined surgery obstruction map

\displaystyle\Theta\colon\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

\displaystyle\to\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})

\displaystyle[W,\overline{\nu}]

\displaystyle\mapsto\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}),

where
\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
is the subset of elements
[(P,\psi);F,V]
,
which satisfy
[V,\theta]_{0}=[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}
.
Theorem
1.3
states that the surgery obstruction map
\Theta
is onto.

The analogous statement for Wall Realisation also says that the
surgery obstruction is injective, in the appropriate sense, and this allows one to
formulate Wall Realisation as an action of the group
L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])
on an appropriate
structure set of
M
.
Calculations of the fourth author lead us to expect that
\Theta
is not in general injective
and this limits our current ability to use modified surgery to provide upper bounds
on the stable class. However, we expect for an appropriate refinement of the
\ell
-monoid,
that a refined surgery obstruction can be defined for which the surgery obstruction map
is injective and we plan to pursue this in further work.

1.3.
Boundary isomorphisms and primitive embeddings

Now we describe the algebraic structures we use to distinguish the manifolds of Theorem
1.1
,
as well as their relevance to modified surgery theory.
First, we return to the
\ell
-monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, and recall a structural result from
[
CS11
]
.
Given a quasi-formation
((P,\psi);F,V)
over a ring
R
with involution, there are induced
\epsilon
-quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\perp},\theta^{\perp})
, where
\theta
is the restriction of
\psi
to
V
, and
\theta^{\perp}
is the restriction of
\psi
to the orthogonal complement
V^{\perp}
of
V\subseteq P
.
Under favourable conditions (see e.g.
[
CS11
, Lemma 6.7]
),
given
\epsilon
-quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
,
[
CS11
, Theorem 5.11]
fits the set
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})
of quasi-formations whose induced forms are (
0
-stably)
v
and
v^{\prime}
into an “exact sequence” (of sets):

(1)

L_{2q+1}^{s}(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \delta\penalty\ }\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \kappa\penalty\ }L_{2q}^{s}(R).

This means that fibres of
\delta
equal orbits under the action
\rho
, and
\operatorname{Im}(\delta)=\kappa^{-1}(0)
.
We focus on the
boundary isomorphism set

\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.
Briefly,
\partial v
and
\partial v^{\prime}
denote (split)
boundary formations
associated to
v
and
v^{\prime}
, and
\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
consists of
equivalence classes
of stable isomorphisms between
\partial v
and
\partial v^{\prime}
; we refer to Section
4.2
for details.
The map
\kappa
assigns to
a stable isomorphism
f\colon\partial v\to\partial v^{\prime}
the element of
L_{2q}^{s}(R)
represented by the
union

v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
,
which is a nonsingular
\epsilon
-quadratic form whose definition is recalled in Section
4.3
.

Next, we describe the relevance of the exact sequence displayed in (
1
) to modified surgery theory.
In classical surgery theory, if a surgery obstruction
\sigma(F)
is trivial in
L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M_{0})])
, then the degree one normal map
F
is normally bordant to a homotopy equivalence.
In modified surgery theory, if a modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
is “elementary”, then the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism
[
Kre99
, Theorem 3]
.
A quasi-formation is
elementary
if it is stably isomorphic (after adding trivial formations
(H_{\epsilon}(F);F,F^{*})
, with
H_{\epsilon}(F)
the standard hyperbolic form on
F\oplus F^{*}
) to a quasi-formation of the form
(H_{(-1)^{q}}(P),P,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\phi\end{smallmatrix}\right)(P))
for some homomorphism
\phi\colon P\to P^{*}
.

We point out that elementary quasi-formations are not generally trivial.
Indeed for every quadratic form
v
, by
[
CS11
, Corollary 5.3(ii)]
there is a unique elementary element of
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v)

(and if
[v]_{0}\neq[v^{\prime}]_{0}
, then no element of
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})
is elementary).
Since the induced form of the trivial formation is the zero form, for
v
not the zero form these elementary elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v)
cannot be trivial.
The exactness of (
1
) thus entails that
[x]
is elementary modulo the action of
L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
, represented by the dashed arrow labelled
\rho
in (
1
), if and only if
\delta([x])=1
; see
[
CS11
, Corollary 5.12]
.

When
v=v^{\prime}
, the boundary isomorphism set becomes the boundary automorphism set

\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v):=\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v)
and the exact sequence (
1
)
shows that computing
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)
is crucial for determining whether quasi-formations are elementary.
One of the important algebraic innovations of this paper is the discovery of quadratic forms with infinite boundary automorphism
sets in Theorem
5.8
.
The other algebraic innovation in this paper is to give a new definition of
\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
in terms
of
2
-sided primitive embeddings
, as we now explain.

Given
\epsilon
-quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
and a nonsingular
\epsilon
-quadratic form
m
,
a 2-sided primitive embedding is a pair

(2)

v\xhookrightarrow{\penalty\ j\penalty\ }m\xhookleftarrow{\penalty\ j^{\prime}\penalty\ }v^{\prime}

of split isometric injections such that
j(v)^{\perp}=j^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
and we define
\operatorname{bPrim}(v,v^{\prime})
to be the set of all isomorphism classes of 2-sided primitive embeddings
involving
v
and
v^{\prime}
.
Given an equivalence class of boundary isomorphisms
[f]\in\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
, the union
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
comes with maps

v\hookrightarrow(v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime})\hookleftarrow-v^{\prime}
which form a 2-sided primitive embedding.
We thus obtain a map

(3)

\Pr\colon\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits(v,-v^{\prime}),\quad[f]\mapsto[v\hookrightarrow(v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime})\hookleftarrow-v^{\prime}],

and we prove the following result
(see Theorem
6.12
for a more detailed statement).

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms. The map
\Pr
of (
3
) is a bijection.

Theorem
1.6
provides a new point of view
on the boundary isomorphism set, which in certain cases
allows us to chart the effect of realising modified surgery obstructions
in Theorem
1.3
on the algebraic invariants of the manifolds involved.
Next we outline how 2-sided primitive embeddings and boundary isomorphisms are used
in the construction of the manifolds of Theorem
1.1
.

The first step is carried out in Section
7
, where for
q=2k
we associate a
boundary isomorphism
\delta_{\overline{\nu}}
to every “even, split-free” normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
(M^{4k},\overline{\nu})
.
Under some technical assumptions, Theorem
7.7
provides a realisation result for these boundary isomorphisms, allowing us to change
\delta_{\overline{\nu}}
by
\delta([x])
for certain
[x]\in\ell_{4k+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
and
\delta
the map (
1
).

Next we take the
4k
-manifolds
\{M_{1},M_{2},\ldots\}
introduced in
Section
1.1
, which are pairwise not stably diffeomorphic.
They are built by taking
N_{pq,1}
which we constructed in
[
CCPS21
]

and which we recall in Proposition
9.3
.
As mentioned above, this paper can be read independently of
[
CCPS21
]
,
provided one accepts the properties of the manifolds
N_{pq,1}
.
Recalling that
p=p(i)=-i(2k)!
and
q=q(i)=1-4p^{2}
,
we define

M_{i}:=N_{pq,i}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}).

Then for each
i
we use our realisation result on boundary isomorphisms,
Theorem
7.7
, to construct sets of normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothings

\{(M_{i}^{j},\overline{\nu}_{i}^{j})\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
with distinct associated boundary isomorphisms

\{\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{i}^{j}}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
, but which are all stably diffeomorphic to one another.
Section
8
shows that for these manifolds the
\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{i}^{j}}
are invariants of the homotopy type, and so
for fixed
i
the manifolds in the
\{M_{i}^{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
are pairwise homotopically inequivalent.
The proof once again goes via
2
-sided primitive embeddings.

1.4.
Extended symmetric forms

To close the introduction we define a natural invariant of oriented normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothings,
which in the relatively simple cases we consider associates a primitive embedding to a normal smoothing.

Let
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing with
q\geq 2
, where as above
M
is a closed, connected, smooth, oriented
2q
-dimensional manifold.
Since
\overline{\nu}
is a
q
-equivalence, we use
the map induced by
\overline{\nu}
to identify the fundamental groups of
M
and
B

and set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
.
For simplicity, we assume that
q=2k
is even.
The
extended symmetric form
of
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is the triple

\overline{\lambda}(M,\overline{\nu}):=(H_{2k}(M;\Lambda),\lambda_{M},\overline{\nu}_{*}),

where

\lambda_{M}\colon H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\times H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\to\Lambda

is the equivariant intersection form of the manifold
M
and

\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(B;\Lambda)
is the map induced by
\overline{\nu}
.

More generally, fix a unital ring with involution
R
.
An
extended symmetric form over
R
is a triple

(H,\lambda,\nu)
, where
\lambda\colon H\times H\to R
is a sesquilinear form
and
\nu\colon H\to Q
is an
R
-module homomorphism.
For example, for any
Q
the standard hyperbolic form
(H_{g},\lambda_{g})
on
\Lambda^{2g}
and the zero homomorphism
H_{g}\to Q
define the extended symmetric form
(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0)
.
Two extended symmetric forms
(H,\lambda,\nu)
and
(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
are
isometric
if there is an isometry
f\colon(H,\lambda)\to(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime})
and an isomorphism
h\colon Q\to Q^{\prime}
such that
\nu^{\prime}f=h\nu
.
If
H
and
H^{\prime}
have the same rank, a
stable isometry
is an isometry

(H,\lambda,\nu)\oplus(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0)\xrightarrow{\cong}(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\oplus(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0)

for some
g\geq 0
. In this case we write
(H,\lambda,\nu)\cong_{\text{st}}(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.

We define the
stable class
of an extended symmetric form
(H,\lambda,\nu)
to be the set of isometry classes of extended symmetric forms over
\Lambda
that become isometric to

(H,\lambda,\nu)
after stabilisation with
(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0)
for some
g\geq 0
:

\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(H,\lambda,\nu):=\{(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\mid(H^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\cong_{\text{st}}(H,\lambda,\nu)\}/\text{isometry}.

If we stabilise
(M,\overline{\nu})
by adding
(W_{g},\overline{\nu}_{g})
, where
\overline{\nu}_{g}
is the trivial
B
-structure on
W_{g}
, then the effect on the extended symmetric form is to stabilise
by
(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0)
:

\overline{\lambda}(M\mathbin{\#}W_{g},\overline{\nu}\mathbin{\#}\overline{\nu}_{g})=\overline{\lambda}(M,\overline{\nu})\oplus(H_{g},\lambda_{g},0).

Next we introduce some simplifying assumptions on the normal smoothings, which will all be satisfied for the examples
we build in Section
9
for the proof of Theorems
1.1
.
Recall that there is a homotopy functor
P_{2k-1}(-)
sending a
CW
-complex to a model for its

(2k{-}1)
st Postnikov type obtained by attaching cells of dimension
2k{+}1
and higher. We make the following
assumptions on
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
:

(i)

The
\Lambda
-modules
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
,
H_{2k}(B;\Lambda)
, and
H^{2k}(B;\Lambda)
are finitely generated and free.

(ii)

The intersection form on
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
is even.

(iii)

There is a map
B\to P_{2k-1}(M)

inducing an isomorphism

H_{2k}(B;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
,
such that the composition
M\xrightarrow{\overline{\nu}}

B\to P_{2k-1}(M)
agrees with the
Postnikov slice
M\to P_{2k-1}(M)
.

Since the equivariant intersection form
\lambda_{M}
uses the orientation of
M
, we introduce
oriented versions of the stable class and homotopy stable class:
write
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h,+}(M)
for the set of oriented manifolds stably diffeomorphic to
M
modulo orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms and modulo orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences respectively, and equipped with an identification of their fundamental groups with
\pi
.
Assumption (iii) above ensures that there is a map

\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M,\overline{\nu})),

where we replace
H_{2k}(B;\Lambda)
with
H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
in the rôle of
Q
.
Since orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences induce an isometry of intersection forms
and since
P_{2k-1}(-)
is functorial,
\overline{\lambda}
factors through the forgetful map to
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h,+}(M)
. Moreover Wall Realisation produces homotopy equivalent, stably diffeomorphic manifolds with the same normal
(2k{-}1)
-type, so we obtain a factorisation:

\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M)/L_{4k+1}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h,+}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M,\overline{\nu})).

We have the following refinement of Theorem
1.1
, which can be compared with
[
BCD+20
, Problem 11]
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Fix
k\geq 2
. For the
4k
-dimensional manifolds
\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, constructed in Section
9
for the proof of Theorem
1.1
,
the composition

\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M_{i})/L_{4k+1}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h,+}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))

is surjective and has infinite image. Moreover
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\mathbb{Z})=\{\pm 1\}
is finite, so after further identifying two extended symmetric forms related by changing the identification of the fundamental group with
\mathbb{Z}
, the image remains infinite.

We conjecture that the map

\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(M_{i})/L_{4k+1}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))

of Theorem
1.7
is also injective.
All of the manifolds we construct to realise this infinite image have isometric intersection forms, so in other words forgetting
\nu\colon H\to Q
yields isomorphic (non-extended) symmetric forms.
We give the proof of Theorem
1.7
in Section
10
.
The proof that the image is infinite follows by relating extended symmetric forms and 2-sided primitive embeddings,
which uses assumptions (i) and (ii) above.
Since in Section
9
we use 2-sided primitive embeddings to distinguish homotopy types of manifolds in the same stable class,
in Section
10
we use this to show that the image of

\overline{\lambda}
is infinite.

The extended intersection form also recognises when the manifolds

M_{i}^{j}
are homotopy equivalent to a connected sum
N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
,
for a simply-connected
4k
-manifold
N
. If
M_{i}^{j}\simeq N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
, then
the extended intersection form of
M_{i}^{j}
will be extended over the inclusions of
rings
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
in the obvious way,
but using Example
6.8
, we deduce that this happens
if and only if
j=0
.
This situation
is analogous to a situation discovered by Hambleton and Teichner in
[
HT97
]
,
where they found a closed oriented topological

4
-manifold
X
with
\pi_{1}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}
,
whose equivariant intersection form is not extended over
\iota
.

Organisation

In Section
2
, we
recall the algebra of quadratic forms and formations we will need.
Section
3
reviews some aspects of modified surgery and proves Theorem
1.3
on the realisation of suitable elements of
\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)])
.
In Section
4
,
we review the definitions of the boundary isomorphism set and the boundary automorphism set
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits
.
Section
5
, exhibits quadratic forms over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
with infinite
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits
.
In Section
6
, we introduce 2-sided primitive embeddings and prove Theorem
1.6
, relating boundary automorphisms and
2
-sided primitive embeddings.
In Section
7
, we associate a boundary isomorphism to a normal smoothing and prove a second realisation result in Theorem
7.7
.
Section
8
interprets primitive embeddings as a homotopy invariant of manifolds in the same stable class.
In Section
9
, we combine realisation, the algebraic theory of boundary automorphisms and primitive embeddings, and the resulting homotopy invariant, to prove Theorem
1.1
.
Finally, Section
10
discusses extended symmetric forms and proves Theorem
1.7
.

Conventions

Throughout the article, manifolds are by default assumed to be compact, oriented, and connected.

The symbol
\Lambda
will always denote a unital ring with involution. In topological settings

\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
, the group ring of a fundamental group.
If
M
is a left
\Lambda
-module then

M^{*}
or
\overline{M}
denotes the dual of
M
,
\mathrm{Hom}(M,\Lambda)
,
which is given a left
R
-module structure using the involution on
R

in the usual way.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism in a category
\mathcal{C}
, and let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a contravariant functor. Assume that
F(f)
is invertible. We shall often write the induced morphism in
\mathcal{D}
as
f^{*}=F(f)
, and denote its inverse by
f^{-*}:=(f^{*})^{-1}=F(f)^{-1}
.

2.
Forms and formations

In this section, we review some background material on forms and formations.
From now on, we fix a positive integer
q\geq 2
, we set
\varepsilon=(-1)^{q}
and let
R
be a weakly finite unital ring with an involution
x\mapsto\overline{x}
.
We refer to
[
Coh77
, p.143]
for the definition of a weakly finite ring, but simply note that finitely generated modules over such rings have a well defined rank
[
Coh77
, Proposition 4.6]
.
We abbreviate “finitely generated” by “f.g.” throughout.
In Section
2.1
, we review forms and formations and in Section
2.2
we review quasi-formations and some of the structure of the
\ell
-monoids.

2.1.
Forms and formations

In this subsection, we briefly review forms and formations and fix our notational conventions.
References include
[
Ran02
,
CS11
,
Wal99
]
.

Let
\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)
be the reduced
K
-group of
R
.
The involution on
R
induces an involution
\ast\colon\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)\to\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)

and we fix
Z\subseteq\widetilde{K}_{1}(R)
a
\ast
-invariant subgroup.
In topological applications,
R=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
will be the integral group
ring of a group
\pi
and

Z=\{g\mid[g]\in\pi\}\subseteq\penalty\ \widetilde{K}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
.
An
s
-basis
for a stably free f.g. left
R
-module
M
is a basis of some f.g. free left module
M\oplus\penalty\ R^{m}
.
A
based module
is a stably f.g. free left module together with an equivalence class of
s
-bases, where two bases are
equivalent
if the change of basis matrix represents an element in
Z
.
An isomorphism
f
of based modules is
simple
if, with respect to some
s
-bases, the torsion of
f\oplus\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
lies in
Z
.

A basis of
M
induces a dual basis on the dual module
M^{*}
and
for
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, we consider the involution

\displaystyle\varepsilon T\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,M^{*})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(M,M^{*})

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto(x\mapsto(y\mapsto\varepsilon\overline{\phi(y)(x)})).

We then set
Q_{\varepsilon}(M):=\mathop{\rm coker}\nolimits(1-\varepsilon T)
.

Definition 2.1
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
is a pair
(M,\psi)
, where
M
is a based module and
\psi\in Q_{\varepsilon}(M)
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic form is
nonsingular
(resp.
simple
) if its
symmetrization

(1+\varepsilon T)\psi
is an isomorphism (resp. a simple isomorphism).
An
isometry

h\colon(M,\psi)\to(M^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
between quadratic forms consists of an isomorphism
h\colon M\to M^{\prime}
such that
h^{*}\theta^{\prime}h=\theta\in Q_{\varepsilon}(M)
.
Unless stated otherwise, we shall assume that all isometries are simple.
For a based module
L
, the standard
hyperbolic

\varepsilon
-quadratic form is defined as follows:

H_{\varepsilon}(L)=\left(L\oplus L^{*},\left[\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right]\right).

The module
L^{*}
is based using the dual basis, and using this we have a canonial basis for
L\oplus L^{*}
,
with respect to which
(1+\varepsilon T)\psi
is the simple isomorphism

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
\varepsilon&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
A form
(M,\psi)
is called
hyperbolic
if it is isometric to
H_{\varepsilon}(L)
for some
L
.

A
sublagrangian
of an
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
(M,\psi)
is a direct summand
j\colon L\hookrightarrow M
which is based, and satisfies
j^{*}\psi j=0\in Q_{\varepsilon}(L)
.
A sublagrangian
j\colon L\hookrightarrow M
satisfies
j(L)\subseteq L^{\perp}
, where the (unbased) module
L^{\perp}=\ker(j^{*}(1+\varepsilon T)\psi)
is called the
annihilator
of
j\colon L\hookrightarrow M
.
A sublagrangian
j\colon L\hookrightarrow M
is a
lagrangian
if it satisfies
L=L^{\perp}
, as unbased modules.
Setting
\phi=(1+\varepsilon T)\psi
, observe that a lagrangian gives rise to an exact sequence

(4)

0\to L\xrightarrow{\penalty\ \penalty\ j\penalty\ \penalty\ }M\xrightarrow{\penalty\ j^{*}\phi\penalty\ }L^{*}\to 0.

A lagrangian
L
for
(M,\psi)
is
simple
if the
s
-basis of
M
is represented by a basis such that the exact sequence in (
4
) is based exact.
The second main definition of this subsection is the following.

Definition 2.2
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation
is a triple
((M,\psi);F,G)
, where
(M,\psi)
is a simple
\varepsilon
-quadratic form and
F,G
are simple lagrangians of
(M,\psi)
.

In the literature, what we call a formation is often referred to as a nonsingular formation.
For concision, we drop this adjective.
Finally, note that if
((M,\psi);F,G)
is a formation, then
(M,\psi)
is a
nonsingular
quadratic form;
in fact
(M,\psi)
is hyperbolic
[
Ran02
, Proposition 12.3]
.

2.2.
Quasi-formations and the
\ell
-monoid.

In this subsection, we describe quasi-formations and the
\ell
-monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
. Recall that
\varepsilon=(-1)^{q}
.
The monoid
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
was first defined by Kreck
[
Kre99
]
.
The definition we present here is a reformulation from
[
CS11
]
.

Definition 2.3
.

An
\varepsilon
-quadratic
quasi-formation
is a triple
x=((M,\psi);F,V)
, where
(M,\psi)
is a simple
\varepsilon
-quadratic form,
F
is a simple lagrangian of
(M,\psi)
, and
V
is a half rank simple direct summand of
M
. A quasi-formation
x
is called
elementary
if the canonical map

F\oplus V\to M
is a simple isomorphism.

If
x=((M,\psi);F,V)
is a quasi-formation in which
V
is a simple lagrangian of
(M,\psi)
, then
x
is a formation.
In particular, all formations are quasi-formations and a
trivial (quasi)-formation
is a formation of type
(H_{\varepsilon}(P);P,P^{*})
, for some f.g. stably free
R
-module
P
.
Two quasi-formations
((M,\psi);F,V)
and
((M^{\prime},\psi^{\prime});F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
are
isomorphic
if there is an isometry
h\colon(M,\psi)\to(M^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
such that
h(F)=F^{\prime}
and
h(V)=\penalty\ V^{\prime}
, and such that the induced isomorphisms
F\xrightarrow{\cong}F^{\prime}
and
V\xrightarrow{\cong}V^{\prime}
are simple.
Next, two quasi-formations
((M,\psi);F,V)
and
((M^{\prime},\psi^{\prime});F^{\prime},V^{\prime})
are
stably isomorphic
if they become isomorphic after the addition of trivial formations.
We use
\cong
to denote the isomorphism relation and
\cong_{s}
to denote the stable isomorphism relation.

Definition 2.4
.

The
\ell
-monoid

\ell_{2q+1}(R)=(\{[x]\},\oplus)
is the unital abelian monoid of stable isomorphism classes of
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formations modulo the further relation generated by

((M,\psi);F,G)\oplus((M,\psi);G,V)\sim((M,\psi);F,V),

where
F
and
G
are both simple lagrangians. Addition is by direct sum and the zero element is the class of the trivial formations.

The
elementary submonoid

\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}(R)\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R)
is the submonoid
of elements represented by elementary quasi-formations.

Note that Kreck’s original definition of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
does not involve quasi-formations
[
Kre99
, page 733]
: it makes use of classes of pairs
(H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{k}),V)
, where
V\subseteq\Lambda^{2k}
is a based free direct summand of rank
k
.
The equivalence between Kreck’s definition and Definition
2.4
was proven in
[
CS11
, Lemma 3.11]
.
Finally, if one is working up to isomorphism of quasi-formations, then without loss of generality one can write quasi-formations as
(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,V)
.

2.3.
0
-stabilised quadratic forms and the structure of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)

In this subsection we briefly recall some of the structure of
\ell_{2q+1}(R)
, as discussed in

[
CS11
]
.
Let
x=((M,\psi);F,V)
be an
\varepsilon
-quadratic quasi-formation with symmetric form

\lambda=\psi+\varepsilon\psi^{*}
.
The
orthogonal complement
of
V
is the summand
V^{\perp}\subseteq M

of elements orthogonal to
V
:

V^{\perp}:=\{m\in M\,\mid\,\lambda(m,v)=0\text{ for all }v\in V\}.

Note that we take
V^{\perp}
with respect to the symmetrisation
\lambda
.
The
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
\psi
induces
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms on the summands
V

and
V^{\perp}
, which we denote by
v=(V,\theta)
and
v^{\perp}=(V^{\perp},\theta^{\perp})
respectively.
Since stabilisation with trivial quasi-formations has the effect of
adding a zero form on a free module to the induced form, we shall consider quadratic
forms up to
0
-stabilisation
, where two
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms
(N,\chi)
and

(N^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})
are called
0
-stably equivalent
if there is an isometry

(N,\chi)\oplus(P,0)\cong(N^{\prime},\chi^{\prime})\oplus(Q,0)

for some free modules
P
and
Q
.
The
0
-stable equivalence class of a form
(N,\chi)
is denoted
[N,\chi]_{0}

and such an equivalence class is called a
0-stabilised
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
.
Often we abbreviate
(N,\chi)
by
n
and
[N,\chi]_{0}
by
[n]_{0}
.
The set of
0
-stabilised
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms,

\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R):=\big(\{[n]_{0}\},\oplus\big),

forms an abelian monoid under orthogonal sum, with zero the
0
-stable class of the
0
-form.
We write
-[n]=[-n]
.
There are monoid maps
[
CS11
, Definition 5.2]
, called
boundary maps
,

b_{-}\colon\ell_{2q+1}(R)\to\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R),\quad[(M,\psi);F,V]\mapsto[v]_{0}

and

b_{+}\colon\ell_{2q+1}(R)\to\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R),\quad[(M,\psi);F,V]\mapsto-[v^{\perp}]_{0}.

To characterise the image of the monoid map
b:=b_{-}\oplus b_{+}\colon\ell_{2q+1}(R)\to\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R)\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R)

we recall that two
0
-stabilised quadratic forms
[N_{0},\chi_{0}]_{0}
and
[N_{1},\chi_{1}]_{0}

are called
stably
equivalent
if they become equal after the addition of
0
-stabilised hyperbolic forms, so that

[N_{0},\chi_{0}]_{0}\oplus[H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{r})]_{0}=[N_{1},\chi_{1}]_{0}\oplus[H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{s})]_{0}

for some
r,s
.
In this case we write
[N_{0},\chi_{0}]_{0}=_{\rm st}[N_{1},\chi_{1}]_{0}
and we define the

stable diagonal

\Delta_{\rm st}:=\big\{\big([n_{0}]_{0},[n_{1}]_{0}\big)\,\mid\,[n_{0}]_{0}=_{\rm st}[n_{1}]_{0}\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R)\times\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R).

Proposition 2.5

(cf.
[
CS11
, Corollary 5.3]
)
.

(i)

b(\ell_{2q+1}(R))=\Delta_{\rm st}
;

(ii)

b_{-}|_{\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}}=-b_{+}|_{\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}}
and
b_{-}|_{\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}}\colon\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}\to\mathcal{F}^{\rm zs}_{\varepsilon}(R)
is an isomorphism. ∎

For future use we make the following definitions.
In light of Proposition
2.5
(
ii
), we let

[e]_{[n]_{0}}\in\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}(R)

denote the unique elementary element with
b_{-}([e]_{[n]_{0}})=[n]_{0}
.
For a fixed
0
-stabilised
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
[n]_{0}

we define

\ell_{2q+1}([n]_{0}):=b_{-}^{-1}([n]_{0})\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R)

to be the subset represented by quasi-formations
((M,\psi);L,V)

with
[v]_{0}=[n]_{0}
.
Similarly we define

\ell_{2q+1}([n]_{0},[m]_{0}):=b_{-}^{-1}([n]_{0})\cap b_{-}^{-1}([m]_{0})\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(R).

This appears as
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})
in the sequence (
1
), where the 0-stable equivalence class was dropped from the notation.

3.
Realising odd dimensional modified surgery obstructions

As discussed in the introduction, Wall showed that under suitable conditions, elements of the quadratic
L
-group
L_{2q+1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi])
can be realised as surgery obstructions of degree one normal maps
[
Wal99
, Theorem 6.5]
.
The goal of this section is to prove an analogue of Wall’s realisation result for Kreck’s odd-dimensional modified surgery obstructions. Unless otherwise stated, manifolds are assumed to be compact, connected, oriented, and smooth.
This section is organised as follows: in Section
3.1
we recall the definition of normal smoothings and normal bordism, in Section
3.2
we review the definition of the odd-dimensional modified surgery obstruction, and in Section
3.3
we prove our realisation result, Theorem
3.10
.

3.1.
Normal smoothings, normal bordisms and Wall forms

In this subsection, we briefly review the notions of normal smoothing and normal bordisms.
The main reference is
[
Kre99
]
.

We fix a fibration
\xi\colon B\to BO
.
An
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
consists of a pair

(M,\overline{\nu})
, where
M
is a compact, connected, oriented, smooth
n
-dimensional manifold
and
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is a lift of the stable normal bundle
\nu\colon M\to BO
of
M
,
meaning that
\nu=\xi\circ\overline{\nu}
.

A
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism
for a closed
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is an
(n{+}1)
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(W,\overline{\nu})
for which
\partial(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
A
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
between two
n
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-manifolds
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
consists of a diffeomorphism
f\colon\partial M_{0}\to\partial M_{1}

which is compatible with the
(B,\xi)
-structures and
an
(n{+}1)
-dimensional
(B,\xi)
-null bordism of
(M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})

where
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}|_{M_{0}}=\overline{\nu}_{0}
and
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}|_{M_{1}}=-\overline{\nu}_{1}
.
We write

\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1}).

Modified surgery theory is a flexible theory in that one can use any fibration

\xi\colon B\to BO
, however for classification results one needs the
maps
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B
to be roughly
n/2
-connected.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a fibration and let
(M,\overline{\nu})
be a
(B,\xi)
-manifold.

(i)

If
\overline{\nu}
is
(k{+}1)
-connected then
(M,\overline{\nu})
is called a
normal
k
-smoothing
.

(ii)

If
(M,\overline{\nu})
is a normal
k
-smoothing and
\xi\colon B\to BO
is

(k{+}1)
-coconnected, then by
[
Kre99
, p.711]

the fibre homotopy type of
\xi
is uniquely determined and called a
normal
k
-type
of
M
.
Moreover, the existence of the Moore-Postnikov factorisation
[
Bau77
]
of
\nu\colon M\to BO
ensures that such fibrations exist for all
k\geq 0
.

Remark 3.2
.

While the normal
k
-types provide an important class of targets
in modified surgery, the theory applies to any fibration
\xi\colon B\to BO
.
However, the universal properties of the Moore-Postnikov factorisation
entail that if
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is a normal
(k{+}1)
-smoothing, then

\xi\colon B\to BO
factors through the normal
k
-type of
M
,
\xi\colon B\to B^{q-1}(M)\to BO
.

Whenever
\overline{\nu}_{*}
is
2
-connected, we will use

\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\pi_{1}(B)
to identify
\pi_{1}(M)=\pi_{1}(B)
.
For concision, we let

\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]

denote the group ring of
\pi_{1}(B)
and we will use
\Lambda
and
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]

interchangeably, depending on whether we wish to emphasise topology or algebra.

Now suppose that
M
is a
2q
-dimensional manifold, with a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M,\overline{\nu})
.
We set

K\pi_{q}(M):=\ker\big(\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{q}(M)\to\pi_{q}(B)\big)

and
\varepsilon:=(-1)^{q}
.
As explained in
[
Kre99
, Section 5]
,
intersection and self-intersections of framed immersions of
q
-spheres in
M

equip
K\pi_{q}(M)
with a quadratic refinement.
For
q\neq 1,3,7
or for
q=3,7
and
w_{q+1}(\xi)(\pi_{q+1}(B))=0
,
the quadratic refinement is given by an
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
(K\pi_{q}(M),\theta)
over
\Lambda
.
For
q=3,7
and
w_{q+1}(\xi)(\pi_{q+1}(B)\neq 0
, the quadratic refinement
takes values in a quotient of
Q_{-}(\Lambda)
.
Since the main examples of this paper are
4k
-dimensional, so that
q=2k
is
even, we shall leave aside the subtleties of the case
q=3,7
and

w_{q+1}(\xi)(\pi_{q+1}(B))\neq 0

and make the following definition.

Definition 3.3
.

(Standard pair)
A pair
((B,\xi),q)
, consisting of a fibration over
BO
and a dimension, is

standard
if
q\neq 3,7
or
w_{q+1}(\xi)(\pi_{q+1}(B))=0
. In addition we require that
B
has finite
q
-skeleton.

Henceforth we restrict our attention to standard pairs
((B,\xi),q)
.
We call the
\varepsilon
-quadratic form

(K\pi_{q}(M),\theta)
the
Wall form
of
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
.
Standard surgery arguments ensure that one can perform surgery on
x\in K\pi_{q}(M)
so that the normal
(B,\xi)
-structure on
M

extends over the trace and effect of the surgery if and only if
\theta(x)=0

[
Kre99
, Lemma 2, Propositions 6 and 7]
.

It turns out that the
0
-stabilisation of the Wall form is the invariant which
appears in the algebra of modified surgery.
While
K\pi_{q}(M)
need not be a free
\Lambda
-module, it is finitely generated.

Lemma 3.4
.

The
\Lambda
-module
K\pi_{q}(M)
is finitely generated.

Proof.

Since
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is
q
-connected,
\pi_{q+1}(B,M)\cong H_{q+1}(B,M;\Lambda)
by the Hurewicz theorem. Then
K\pi_{q}(M)
is a quotient of
\pi_{q+1}(B,M)
, so it suffices to see that
H_{q+1}(B,M;\Lambda)
is finitely generated. The algebraic mapping cone of map
\overline{\nu}_{*}
between the cellular chain complexes gives a chain complex
D_{*}
computing
H_{*}(B,M;\Lambda)
. Since
M
is compact and
B
has finite
q
-skeleton,
in degrees
0\leq i\leq q
this chain complex consists of f.g. free
\Lambda
-modules. Since
H_{i}(D_{*})=0
for
0\leq i\leq q
, we have an exact sequence

0\to\ker d_{q+1}\to D_{q+1}\xrightarrow{d_{q+1}}D_{q}\to\cdots\to D_{0}\to 0.

It follows from Schanuel’s lemma
[
Lam99
, Corollary 5.5]
that
\ker(d_{q+1})
is f.g. projective. Then
H_{q+1}(B,M;\Lambda)
is a quotient of
\ker(d_{q+1})
, so is finitely generated.
∎

Thus there is a surjection
\pi\colon S\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(M)
from a f.g. free
\Lambda
-module
S
.
The pull-back of
(K\pi_{q}(M),\theta)
along
\pi
is denoted
(S,\mu)
and called
a
free Wall form
of
(M,\overline{\nu})
.
By
[
Kre99
, Proposition 8]
the
0
-stable class of
(S,\mu)
is well-defined.

Definition 3.5
.

The
0
-stabilised Wall form
of a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M^{2q},\overline{\nu})
is defined by

[v(\overline{\nu})]_{0}:=[S,\mu]_{0}.

3.2.
The modified surgery obstruction

We recall the definition of the odd-dimensional modified surgery obstruction
and give a unified presentation of foundational results of Kreck about this obstruction.
From now on, all normal smoothings will be to a fixed fibration
\xi\colon B\to BO
.
The main reference is
[
Kre99
, Section 6]
; see also
[
CS11
, Section 2]
.

Recall that a
(2q{+}1)
-dimensional modified surgery prolem

(W,\xi)
is a normal

(B,\xi)
-bordism where
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})=\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})

and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})=\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})
are
(q{-}1)
-smoothings.
We define

\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M,\overline{\nu}_{0}):=\{\,[W,\overline{\nu}]\,\mid\,\text{$\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})$,
$\partial_{+}(W,\overline{\nu})$ is a $(q{-}1)$-smoothing}\}

to be the set of rel. boundary bordism classes of

(2q{+}1)
-dimensional modified surgery problems over
(B,\xi)
with
incoming boundary
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and outgoing boundary a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.
An important problem in surgery is to determine when modified surgery problem
(W,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-bordant
rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism and more generally to give an algebraic
classification of
\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
. The following theorem combines

[
Kre99
, Theorem 4 & Proposition 8]
, two fundamental results of
Kreck on this problem.

Theorem 3.6

(The modified surgery obstruction in odd dimensions)
.

If
((B,\xi),q)
is standard, then the map

\displaystyle\Theta\colon\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})

\displaystyle\to\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})\subseteq\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle[W,\overline{\nu}]

\displaystyle\mapsto\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}),

is well-defined and is such that
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)

if and only if

(W,\overline{\nu})
is
(B,\xi)
-bordant rel. boundary to an
s
-cobordism.
Moreover the boundary maps on
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
satisfy the equations

b_{-}(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))=[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}

and
b_{+}(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))=-[v(\overline{\nu}_{1})]_{0}
.

Remark 3.7
.

This theorem has been used to prove a truly vast array of classification results,
often by showing that
\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})=\{[e]_{[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{E}\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)
,
using the notation introduced just after Proposition
2.5
.
However, to the best of our knowledge
the image of
\Theta
has not been systematically discussed in the literature,
nor have the sets
\Theta^{-1}([x])
, for
[x]\neq[e]_{[v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0}}
.
In this paper, we shall show that
\Theta
is surjective; see Theorem
3.10
below.
We plan to investigate the pre-images
\Theta^{-1}([x])
in future work.

Remark 3.8
.

The appropriate analogue of Theorem
3.6
holds in the non-standard
cases. The proof again follows from
[
Kre99
, Theorem 4 & Proposition 8]
,
but some care is needed to interpret
[
Kre99
, Proposition 8]

in non-standard cases.

We now recall the definition of the modified surgery obstruction

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
for a bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
.
By surgery below the middle dimension
[
Kre99
, Proposition 4]
, we assume that
\overline{\nu}\colon W\to B

is
q
-connected. It follows by the same argument as that in Lemma
3.4
that
K\pi_{q}(W)
is finitely generated.
Choose a set
\omega
of generators for
K\pi_{q}(W)

and disjoint embeddings
\varphi_{i}\colon S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\hookrightarrow W
compatible with the
(B,\xi)
-structure representing these generators, and set

U:=\bigcup_{i}\varphi_{i}(S^{q}\times D^{q+1}).

Let
\psi
denote the quadratic form on
H_{q}(\partial U;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)])
. Since
\partial U=\bigcup_{i}S^{q}\times S^{q}
, we deduce that
(H_{q}(\partial U;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]),\psi)
is a hyperbolic quadratic form.
For concision, we refer to the pair
(\omega,\varphi)
as a
set of embedded generators for
K\pi_{q}(W)
, we set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
, and consider the
\Lambda
-modules

F:=H_{q+1}(U,\partial U;\Lambda)\text{ and }V:=\penalty\ H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U;\Lambda).

Via the boundary maps in the long exact sequences of the appropriate pairs,
F
and
V
can be identified with submodules of
P:=\penalty\ H_{q}(\partial U;\Lambda)
. In addition,
F
is a lagrangian and
V
is a half rank direct summand
[
Kre99
, p. 734]
.
The
kernel quasi-formation
is then defined as

\Sigma(W,\overline{\nu},\omega,\varphi):=((P,\psi);F,V).

The class of this quasi-formation in
\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)
only depends on the
(B,\xi)
-bordism class rel. boundary of the normal
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})

[
Kre99
, Theorem 4]
.
This leads to the following definition, which is due to Kreck
[
Kre99
, p. 734]
; see also
[
CS11
, p. 494]
.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0}),(M_{1}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{1})
be two
(q{-}1)
-smoothings.
Let
f\colon\partial M_{0}^{2q}\to\partial M_{1}^{2q}
be a diffeomorphism that is compatible with the smoothings.
Assume that
(W^{2q+1},\overline{\nu})
is a normal
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism for
M_{0}\cup_{f}-M_{1}
.
The
modified surgery obstruction
of
(W,\overline{\nu})
is

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}):=[(\Sigma(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}))]\in\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda),

where
(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing over
(B,\xi)
, which is
(B,\xi)
-bordant
rel. boundary to
(W,\overline{\nu})

and
(\omega^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
is a system of embedded generators for
K\pi_{q}(W^{\prime})
.

As stated in Theorem
3.6
, the class
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})\in\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)
only depends on the
(B,\xi)
-bordism class rel. boundary of the
(B,\xi)
-null-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
: it is independent of the choice of the
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(W^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
and of the subsequent choice of a system of embedded generators for
K\pi_{q}(W^{\prime})
.

3.3.
Realising modified surgery obstructions

We recall that
((B,\xi),q)
is a standard pair consisting of a fibration
(B,\xi)
and a dimension
q
.
In this subsection, we state and prove our realisation theorem, Theorem
3.10
,
for the odd-dimensional modified surgery obstruction.
For a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
,
this theorem implies that the surgery obstruction map

\Theta\colon\mathcal{W}_{2q+1,q}(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})\to\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})

of Theorem
3.6
is onto. In fact we prove a realisation result for
quasi-formations of sufficiently large rank.

Let
\pi\colon S\to K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
be a surjection from a free
\Lambda
-module
with
(S,\mu)
the associated free Wall form of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
Given
[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}]_{0})
we may assume that it is represented by
a quasi-formation
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
of large enough rank so that
for some
r
there is an isometry of quadratic forms

(5)

h\colon(S,\mu)\oplus(\Lambda^{r},0)\xrightarrow{\cong}(V,\theta).

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
q\geq 3
, let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be a
normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing and let
[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0}]_{0})

be represented by
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
,
where
(V,\theta)
is
0
-stably isomorphic to a free Wall form
(S,\mu)
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
as in (
5
).
Then there is a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
with
\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
such that

\Sigma(W,\overline{\nu},\omega,\varphi)=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
for certain choices of
\omega
and
\varphi
.
Hence

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=[x].

The same statement holds for
q=2
in the topological category if
\pi_{1}(B)
is a good group.

The proof of Theorem
3.10
occupies the remainder of the section.
First we use
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and the data
x,\pi
, and
h
to
construct a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
based on
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
Then we prove a lemma, then in the proof environment we show that
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=[x]
.

Note that since the
\Lambda
-modules
V
and
K
have the same rank, we deduce that
K
also has rank
s{+}r
, where
s
is the rank of
S
.
The first step is to perform trivial surgeries on the lagrangian
K
that appears in the quasi-formation
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
.
Namely, we let
W_{0}
be the cobordism obtained from
M_{0}\times[0,1]
by adding trivial
q
-handles along
M_{0}\times\{1\}
with trivial framing, one handle for each element of a basis for
K
.
In other words, we consider the following boundary connected sum:

W_{0}=\big(M_{0}\times[0,1]\big)\natural\big(\natural_{i=1}^{s+r}S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\big).

The boundary components of
W_{0}
are
\partial_{-}W_{0}=M_{0}
and
\partial_{+}W_{0}=-M_{0}\mathbin{\#}(\mathbin{\#}_{i=1}^{s+r}S^{q}\times S^{q})
.
We turn
W_{0}
into a
(B,\xi)
-manifold
(W_{0},\overline{\nu}_{W_{0}})
by taking the trivial extension of the normal structure
\overline{\nu}_{0}
to
W_{0}
(i.e. the
(B,\xi)
-structure obtained by restricting a null-homotopic map
D^{q+1}\times D^{q}\to B
on the handles to the
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}
).
This concludes the first step in the construction of
W
.

Next we wish to add
(q{+}1)
-handles to
W_{0}
along
\partial_{+}W_{0}
in order to complete the construction of the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
.
The attaching maps for these handles must lie in
K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})
, the domain of the Wall form of
\partial_{+}W_{0}
.
The identification
\partial_{+}W_{0}=-M_{0}\mathbin{\#}(\mathbin{\#}_{i=1}^{s+r}S^{q}\times S^{q})
gives

(K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0}),\theta_{\partial_{+}W_{0}})\cong(K\pi_{q}(M_{0}),-\theta_{M_{0}})\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(K).

Recall that the form
\mu
on
S
was obtained by pulling back the Wall form on
K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
via the surjection
\pi\colon S\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
.
By pre-composing with the projection
S\oplus\Lambda^{r}\twoheadrightarrow S
onto the first component,
we obtain a surjection
(\pi,0)\colon(S\oplus\Lambda^{r},\mu\oplus 0)\twoheadrightarrow(K\pi_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
of quadratic forms.
Recall furthermore that we fixed an isometry
h\colon(S,\mu)\oplus(\Lambda^{r},0)\to\penalty\ (V,\theta)
, where
(V,\theta)
is the restriction of the hyperbolic form on
H_{\varepsilon}(K)
to
V
; in particular
V\subseteq K\oplus K^{*}
.
We assert that we obtain a sublagrangian of
(K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0}),\theta_{\partial_{+}W_{0}})
by considering the image of
S\oplus\Lambda^{r}
under the map

(6)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}(\pi,0)\\
h\end{pmatrix}\colon S\oplus\Lambda^{r}

\displaystyle\to K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus V\subseteq K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus(K\oplus K^{*})=K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}(\pi(y_{1}),0)\\
h\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

The fact that
\operatorname{im}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a summand follows from the fact that
V\subseteq K\oplus K^{*}
is a summand.
Note that
\pi^{*}\theta_{M_{0}}\pi=\mu
(by definition of
\mu
) and
h^{*}\theta h=\mu\oplus 0
(since
h
is an isometry).
Using these facts, the following computation establishes the assertion that the image of
S\oplus\Lambda^{r}
is a sublagrangian:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{*}(-\theta_{M_{0}}\oplus H_{\varepsilon})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=-(\pi,0)^{*}\theta_{M_{0}}(\pi,0)+h^{*}H_{\varepsilon}h

\displaystyle=-(\mu\oplus 0)+(\mu\oplus 0)=0.

We now use the sublagrangian
\operatorname{im}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)
to provide data
with which to attach
(q{+}1)
-handles along
\partial_{+}W_{0}
.
Fix a basis
\{\overline{x}_{1},\dots,\overline{x}_{s+r}\}
for
S\oplus\Lambda^{r}
.
Since the elements
x_{i}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{smallmatrix}\right)(\overline{x}_{i})\in K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})
lie in a sublagrangian
of the Wall form of
(\partial_{+}W_{0},\overline{\nu}_{W_{0}}|_{\partial_{+}{W_{0}}})

for
q\geq 3
, it follows from
[
Kre99
, Proposition 6.7]
,
that these classes can be represented by disjoint framed embedded spheres.
We discuss the case
q=2
in Remark
3.11
below.

For
i=1,\dots,s+r
, we choose pairwise disjoint
framed embeddings
\phi_{i}\colon S^{q}\times D^{q}\hookrightarrow\partial_{+}W_{0}

representing
x_{i}
and also lifts
\omega_{i}\in\pi_{q+1}(B,\partial_{+}W_{0})
of the
x_{i}
.
We then attach
(q{+}1)
-handles (which we denote by
h_{i}^{q+1}
) along the set of disjoint, framed embeddings

\phi=(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{s+r})
to obtain the cobordism. Extend the
(B,\xi)

structure from
(W_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
using the classes
\omega=(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{r+s})
to obtain the
(B,\xi)
-bordism

(7)

W=W_{x,\pi,h,\phi,\omega}:=W_{0}\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{s+r}h_{i}^{q+1}.

Since we performed surgery along elements of
K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})
, there is a canonical
normal
(B,\xi)
-structure
(W,\overline{\nu})
which extends the
normal
(B,\xi)
-structure on
W_{0}
; see the end of Section
3.1
.
A key property of this construction is that the
x_{i}\in K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})\cong K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\oplus K^{*}
project to a basis for
V\subseteq K\oplus K^{*}
.
This concludes the construction of the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})

based on
\partial_{-}(W,\overline{\nu})=(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.

Remark 3.11
.

We discuss the additional requirements of the
4
-dimensional case
q=2
in this remark. In this case we pass to the topological category, and assume that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(B)
is good. In order to deduce that the homology classes
\{x_{i}\}
we wish to surger can be represented by
\pi_{1}
-negligible framed embedded 2-spheres, we need to use the
sphere embedding theorem
of Freedman-Quinn
[
FQ90
, Theorem 5.1B]
. Here
\pi_{1}
-negligible
means that removing the embedded spheres, and therefore the process of performing surgery on them, does not change the fundamental group. This is equivalent to the existence of a collection of geometrically dual 2-spheres
\{y_{i}\}
immersed in
\partial_{+}W_{0}
, intersecting the
\{x_{i}\}
transversely, and such that
x_{i}\cap y_{j}
is a single point if
i=j
and is empty for
i\neq j
. The existence of such a collection is guaranteed by
[
PRT20
]
.

The extra input needed in the 4-dimensional case, which is not needed in high dimensions, is that the classes
\{x_{i}\}
must be represented by an immersed collection of 2-spheres that admit a collection of framed algebraically dual spheres. Then the sphere embedding theorem applies to realise the
\{x_{i}\}
by
\pi_{1}
-negligible framed embedded 2-spheres as desired. So to extend our argument to the case
q=2
, we must show that such a dual collection exists. Since the hyperbolic form on
K\oplus K^{*}
is even, if we find the dual spheres their self-intersection numbers will be even. A cusp homotopy of an immersed 2-sphere in a 4-manifold adds a self-intersection point and changes the Euler number of the normal bundle by
\pm 2
. So apply these to trivialise the normal bundle, and choose a trivialisation, to obtain framed dual spheres.

It remains to find dual spheres for the spheres
\{x_{i}\}
. Let
f_{i}\in V
be the image of
x_{i}
under the projection
K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\oplus K^{*}\to K\oplus K^{*}
.
By the key property of the
\{x_{i}\}
, the
\{f_{i}\}
form a basis for the direct summand
V\subseteq K\oplus K^{*}
.
We will use that the hyperbolic form on
K\oplus K^{*}
is nonsingular, and we will find dual spheres
\{g_{i}\}
to the
\{f_{i}\}
in
K\oplus K^{*}
. Write
H:=K\oplus K^{*}
temporarily. Since
V\subseteq H
is a direct summand, then the dual restriction map
H^{*}\to V^{*}
is surjective. As
V^{*}
is free we may choose a splitting
s\colon V^{*}\to H^{*}
. Let
\lambda_{H}\colon H\to H^{*}
be the symmetrised hyperbolic form on
H
, which is an isomorphism, and consider
\lambda_{H}^{-1}(s(V^{*}))\subseteq H
. Choose a basis
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
for this submodule that maps to the duals
\lambda_{H}(g_{i})=s(f_{i}^{*})\in H^{*}
. The
\{g_{i}\}
are represented by immersed framed spheres in
\partial_{+}W_{0}
, algebraically dual to the
x_{i}
. As explained above we may therefore apply the sphere embedding theorem to represent the
\{x_{i}\}
by locally flat embedded spheres, to which we attach handles for the completion of the construction of the cobordism
W
.

The next lemma shows that
(W,\overline{\nu})
is a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing and describes its
q
-th homotopy group.
Recall that
x\in\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)
is a fixed element represented by a quasi-formation

(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)

such that there is a surjection
\pi\colon S\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
, where
S
is a free module of rank
s
.
Let
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\colon K\oplus K^{*}\to K
denote the canonical projection, and use
\widehat{h}
to denote the composition

\widehat{h}\colon S\oplus\Lambda^{r}\xrightarrow{h}V\hookrightarrow K\oplus K^{*}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{proj}_{1}}K.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing and let

[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}(\Lambda)
.
If the induced form of
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
is
0
-stably isomorphic to a free Wall form
(S,\mu)
of
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
as in (
5
), then the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
constructed in (
7
) satisfies the following.

(i)

The
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism

\varphi\colon\pi_{q}(W)\xrightarrow{\cong}(\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K)/\im\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
\widehat{h}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

(iii)

The isomorphism
\varphi
induces a surjection
K\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(W)\subseteq\pi_{q}(W)
.

Proof.

We start by proving the first assertion, but along the way we will prove the second.
First, we check that
\overline{\nu}\colon W\to B
induces an isomorphism on the first
q{-}1
homotopy groups.
Since we defined
W_{0}
as
W_{0}=\big(M_{0}\times[0,1]\big)\natural\big(\natural_{i=1}^{r+s}S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\big)
, we deduce that
\pi_{i}(W_{0})=\pi_{i}(M_{0})
for
i\leq q{-}1
.
Adding
(q{+}1)
-handles does not affect the first
q{-}1
homotopy groups, by the Hurewicz theorem applied to the universal covers, and therefore
\pi_{i}(W)=\pi_{i}(M_{0})
for
i\leq q{-}1
.
Since
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing, and since
\overline{\nu}\colon W\to B
restricts to
\overline{\nu}_{0}
on
M_{0}=\partial_{-}W_{0}
, we deduce that
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{i}(W)=\pi_{i}(M_{0})\to\pi_{i}(B)
is an isomorphism for
i\leq q{-}1
.

Next, we check that
\overline{\nu}

\colon\pi_{q}(W)\to\pi_{q}(B)
is surjective.
As
W_{0}=\big(M_{0}\times[0,1]\big)\natural\big(\natural_{i=1}^{r+s}S^{q}\times\penalty\ D^{q+1}\big)
, we deduce that
\pi_{q}(W_{0})=\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K
.
Since
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing, we know that
\pi_{q}(M_{0})\to\pi_{q}(B)
is surjective and therefore so is
\pi_{q}(W_{0})\to\pi_{q}(B)
.
Next, we compute
\pi_{q}(W)
.
Up to homotopy equivalence, adding
(q{+}1)
-handles is the same as adding
(q{+}1)
-cells and
in order to compute the effect on
\pi_{q}
of attaching
we must mod out by the image of the

(s{+}r)

(q{+}1)
-cells along the maps
S^{q}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow\partial_{+}W_{0}
.
The homotopy classes of these attaching maps were specified by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\pi\\
h\end{pmatrix}\colon S\oplus\Lambda^{r}

\displaystyle\to K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus V\subseteq K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus(K\oplus K^{*})=K\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\pi(x_{1})\\
\widehat{h}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\end{pmatrix}.

Since the induced map
\pi_{q}(\partial_{+}W_{0})\to\pi_{q}(W_{0})
is

\mathrm{Id}\oplus\operatorname{proj}_{1}\colon\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\oplus K^{*}\to\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K
, it follows that
\pi_{q}(W)\cong(\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K)/\im\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
\widehat{h}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
as claimed.
This concludes the computation of
\pi_{q}(W)
and thus the proof of the second assertion of the lemma. It remains to show that
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{q}(W)\to\pi_{q}(B)
is surjective.
The computation of
\pi_{q}(W)
shows that every element of
\pi_{q}(W)
is represented by a pair

(y_{1},y_{2})\in\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K
and that
\overline{\nu}_{*}([y_{1},y_{2}])=\overline{\nu}_{0*}(y_{1})
.
Since the
(B,\xi)
-structure on
W
was obtained by first extending the one on
M_{0}
and imposing the trivial
(B,\xi)
-structure on
S^{q}\times S^{q}
, it follows that for any element
[(y_{1},y_{2})]\in\pi_{q}(W)
we have

\overline{\nu}_{*}[(y_{1},y_{2})]=\overline{\nu}_{0*}(y_{1})\in\pi_{q}(B)
.
Since
\overline{\nu}_{0*}\colon\pi_{1}(M_{0})\to\pi_{q}(B)
is surjective, so is
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{q}(W)\to\pi_{q}(B)
, as desired.

It remains to prove the last assertion, namely that

\operatorname{proj}_{2}\colon K\to K\pi_{q}(W)\subseteq\pi_{q}(W)\cong(\pi_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K)/\im\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi\\
\widehat{h}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\,\,k\mapsto[(0,k)]

is a surjection.
Let
[(x_{1},x_{2})]\in K\pi_{q}(W)
with
x_{1}\in\pi_{q}(M_{0})
and
x_{2}\in K
.
We saw above that
\overline{\nu}_{*}([y_{1},y_{2}])=\overline{\nu}_{0*}(y_{1})
for all
[(y_{1},y_{2})]\in\pi_{q}(W)
.
This implies both that
\operatorname{proj}_{2}
is defined (i.e. its image lies in
K\pi_{q}(W)
as asserted) and that, for
[(x_{1},x_{2})]\in K\pi_{q}(W)
, we have
x_{1}\in K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
.
Next, since
\pi\colon S\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
is surjective, there exists
z\in S\oplus\Lambda^{r}
such that
x_{1}=\pi(z)
.
Using our computation of
\pi_{q}(W)
, we obtain
[(x_{1},0)]=[(\pi(z),0)]=[(0,\widehat{h}(w)]
for some

w\in S\oplus\Lambda^{r}
. We thus obtain that
[(x_{1},x_{2})]=[(0,x_{2}+\widehat{h}(w))]
, so that

\operatorname{proj}_{2}(x_{2}+\widehat{h}(w))=[(x_{1},x_{2})]
.
Since
[(x_{1},x_{2})]
was arbitrary, we are done.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

We must show that the modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
of the
(B,\xi)
-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})

of (
7
) lies in the equivalence class
[x]=[H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V]\in\ell_{2q+1}(\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)])
of the quasi-formation with which we started.

The definition of
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})
requires that we choose a set of generators for
K\pi_{q}(W)
and represent them by framed embedded
q
-spheres in
W
.
We will use
U\subseteq W
to denote the resulting handlebody, and
\psi
to denote the hyperbolic quadratic form on
H_{q}(\partial U;\Lambda)
.
Recall from Section
3.2
that the modified surgery obstruction of a
(q{-}1)
-normal smoothing
(W,\overline{\nu})
is given by

(8)

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=\big[\big((H_{q}(\partial U;\Lambda),\psi),H_{q+1}(U,\partial U;\Lambda),H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U};M_{0}\sqcup\partial U;\Lambda)\big)\big].

Lemma
3.12
established the existence of surjection
K\twoheadrightarrow K\pi_{q}(W)
.
Consequently, a basis for
K
provides a generating set for
K\pi_{q}(W)
.
After representing these generators by framed embedded
q
-spheres, we obtain
r{+}s

embeddings
S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\hookrightarrow W
whose images we join by thickened paths.
This leads to a solid handlebody
U
as in (
8
). We can and shall assume that
U\subseteq W_{0}
, since
U
represents geometrically a basis for
K
.

We will show that the surgery obstruction stemming from
U
is given by
x
.
For the rest of this proof, homology and cohomology groups are understood to have
\Lambda
-coefficients.
Since it is clear that
((H_{q}(\partial U),\psi);H_{q+1}(U,\partial U))\cong(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K)
, it remains to show that the map

H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U};M_{0}\sqcup\partial U;\Lambda)\to H_{q}(\partial U)\xrightarrow{\cong}K\oplus K^{*}

has image
V
.

Claim
.

The following assertions hold.

(i)

The inclusion induces an isomorphism
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q+1}(W_{0})
.

(ii)

There is an isomorphism
H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\cong K\oplus K^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{0})
.

(iii)

We have
H_{i}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
for
i=q,q{+}1
.

Proof of Claim.

In order to prove the first assertion, we will consider the long exact sequence of the pair
(W_{0},W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
.
By excision, we have
H_{q+2}(W_{0},W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})=H_{q+2}(U,\partial U)=0
.
We deduce that the inclusion induced map
\iota\colon H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q+1}(W_{0})
is injective.
Since
W_{0}=\bigl(M_{0}\times[0,1]\bigr)\natural\big(\natural_{i=1}^{r+s}S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\big)\simeq M_{0}\vee^{r+s}S^{q}
, we deduce that

H_{q+1}(M_{0})\cong H_{q+1}(W_{0})
, again induced by inclusion.
This factors as
H_{q+1}(M_{0})\to H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\xrightarrow{\iota}H_{q+1}(W_{0})
,
so
\iota
is also surjective.
This concludes the proof of the first assertion. We note for later that also
H_{q+1}(M_{0})\cong H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
.

In order to prove the second assertion, we consider the Mayer-Vietoris sequence associated to the decomposition
W=(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\cup U
.
The portion of interest is

(9)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\to H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\oplus H_{q+1}(U)\to H_{q+1}(W_{0})\xrightarrow{0}H_{q}(\partial U)

\displaystyle\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\oplus H_{q}(U)\to H_{q}(W_{0})\to H_{q-1}(\partial U)\to\cdots.

Since
the map
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q+1}(W_{0})
is an isomorphism (by the first assertion), we deduce that
H_{q+1}(W_{0})\to H_{q}(\partial U)
is the zero map as shown.
Next, we use that
H_{q-1}(\partial U)=0
as well as the fact that
H_{q}(\partial U)=K\oplus K^{*}
surjects onto
H_{q}(U)=K
and
H_{q}(W_{0})\cong H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K
to obtain that the sequence displayed in (
9
) determines the following short exact sequence:

(10)

0\to K^{*}\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\to 0.

The inclusion
M_{0}\sqcup\partial U\hookrightarrow W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U}
gives rise to a splitting of this short exact sequence.
This concludes the proof of the second assertion.
In order to prove the third assertion, we consider the long exact sequence of the pair
(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)
:

(11)

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\to H_{q+1}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\xrightarrow{j}H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)

\displaystyle\to H_{q}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to\cdots.

We show that
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
.
Note that
H_{q+1}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=H_{q+1}(M_{0})
and, as we showed in the first assertion, the inclusion induced map
H_{q+1}(M_{0})\to H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
is an isomorphism.
We deduce that
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q+1}(W_{0})
is an isomorphism.
It follows that the second map in (
11
) is surjective,
from which we deduce that
j\colon H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)
is the zero map.
We now show that
j
is surjective, from which it follows that
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
.
That
j
is surjective follows because
H_{q}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
is an isomorphism. To see this latter fact, apply the
5
-lemma to the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{incl}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}}
