\textstyle{H_{q}(W_{0})\oplus K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where we recall that
H_{q}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus K\oplus K^{*}
, and the bottom (split) exact sequence was described in (
10
).
We have therefore shown that
j
is both surjective and the zero map, proving that
H_{q+1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
, as desired.
Along the way, we have established that (
11
) gives the following exact sequence:

(12)

0\to H_{q}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\xrightarrow{0}H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to\cdots

It remains to prove that
H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
.
The isomorphism displayed in (
12
) implies that the map
H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)
is zero, as shown.
It therefore suffices to show that the next map is also zero.
Extending the exact sequence (
12
) to the right, this is equivalent to proving that
H_{q-1}(M_{0})\cong H_{q-1}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q-1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
is an injection.
A Mayer-Vietoris argument shows that the map
H_{q-1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})\to H_{q-1}(W_{0})
induced by the inclusion is an isomorphism.
Since we have
H_{q-1}(W_{0})\cong H_{q-1}(M_{0})
, we deduce that
H_{q-1}(M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q-1}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
is an isomorphism, as claimed.
We have therefore shown that
H_{i}(W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)=0
for
i=q,q+1
and
this concludes the proof of the Claim.

Using the Claim, we can now show that

H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)
is sent to
V\subseteq H_{q}(\partial U)\cong H_{\varepsilon}(K)
.
Consider the long exact sequence of the triple
(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)
.
The third assertion of the Claim implies that

H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\cong H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},W_{0}\smallsetminus\mathring{U})
.
By excision, this latter module is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r+s}H_{q+1}(D^{q+1},S^{q})
. Here, the
D^{q+1}
correspond to the cores of the handles
h^{q+1}_{i}
.
By definition of the attaching maps of the handles
h^{q+1}_{i}
, this module coincides with
V
under the composition

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r+s}H_{q+1}(D^{q+1},S^{q})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q+1}(W\smallsetminus\mathring{U},M_{0}\sqcup\partial U)\to H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus H_{q}(\partial U)\to H_{q}(\partial U)\cong K\oplus K^{*}.

It follows that the surgery obstruction
(W,\overline{\nu})
is represented by

x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)
, which concludes the proof of
Theorem
3.10
.
∎

4.
Boundary isomorphisms

This algebraic section recalls the notion of a boundary isomorphism from
[
CS11
]
.
Informally, these can be thought of as an algebraic analogue of the group of
diffeomorphisms of the
(2q{-}1)
-dimensional boundary of a compact
2q
-manifold modulo
those diffeomorphisms that are isotopic to the restriction of a diffeomorphism of the
2q
-manifold.

It is perhaps surprising that such an algebraic phenomenon should arise for closed
2q
-manifolds,
but in fact a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
of a
2q
-manifold
M
, satisfying certain algebraic conditions, induces a splitting of the intersection quadratic form as a union over two forms defined in terms of
B
and
\overline{\nu}
; details are given in Section
7
.

In Sections
4.1
and
4.2
, we review stable isomorphisms of split quadratic formations and homotopies between them, culminating in the definition of the boundary isomorphism set, while the gluing process for forms is reviewed in Section
4.3
.

We briefly recall our general strategy for the proof of Theorem
1.1
: elements of the boundary isomorphism sets will be the invariants we use to distinguish
4k
-manifolds. In Section
7
we will show, via the notion of primitive embeddings to appear in Section
6
, that the realisation results from Section
3
allow us to obtain many infinitely many nontrivial values of the boundary isomorphism invariant.

4.1.
Stable isomorphisms of split formations

In this subsection, we review the notion of a split quadratic formation.
References include
[
Ran81
, Section 1.6]
,
[
Ran02
, Section 12.5]
, as well as
[
CS11
, Section 4.1]
.

Definition 4.1
.

A
simple split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation

(F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma\\
\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G,\theta))
consists of a based
R
-module
F
, a
(-\varepsilon)
-quadratic form
(G,\theta)
, and morphisms
\gamma\colon G\to F
and
\delta\colon G\to F^{*}
such that

(i)

the
Hessian

\theta

satisfies

\gamma^{*}\delta=\theta-\varepsilon\theta^{*}\colon G\to G^{*};

(ii)

the map

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma\\
\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon(G,0)\to H_{\varepsilon}(F)
is the inclusion of a simple lagrangian.

The first examples of split quadratic formations are
the trivial split quadratic formations
:

(F,F^{*}):=\left(F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(F^{*},0)\right).

Next, observe that for a split quadratic formation
(F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma\\
\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G,\theta))
, the triple

(H_{\varepsilon}(F);F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma\\
\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)G)
is a simple quadratic formation.
In fact, quadratic formations also give rise to split quadratic formations: writing
\gamma
and
\delta
for the inclusions of the lagrangians, the map
\gamma^{*}\delta\colon G\to G^{*}
is a
(-\varepsilon)
-symmetric form and admits a quadratic refinement
[
Ran02
, Section 12.5]
.
The choice of this quadratic refinement is remembered in a split quadratic formation, but not in a formation.

Definition 4.2
.

An
isomorphism

(\alpha,\beta,\nu)\colon(F_{1},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\delta_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{1},\theta_{1}))\to(F_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{2}\\
\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{2},\theta_{2}))

between split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formations
consists of isomorphisms
\alpha\colon F_{1}\to F_{2}
and
\beta\colon G_{1}\to G_{2}
, as well as a
(-\varepsilon)
-quadratic form
(F_{1}^{*},\nu)
such that

(i)

the equality
\theta_{1}+\delta_{1}^{*}\nu\delta_{1}=\beta_{1}^{*}\theta_{2}\beta_{1}
holds in
Q_{-\varepsilon}(G_{1})
;

(ii)

the map
f:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\alpha(\nu-\varepsilon\nu^{*})\\
0&(\alpha^{*})^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon H_{\varepsilon}(F_{1})\to H_{\varepsilon}(F_{2})
defines an isomorphism of quadratic formations or, equivalently, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\delta_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{G_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{2}\\
\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{F_{1}\oplus F_{1}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f,\cong}
\textstyle{F_{2}\oplus{F_{2}}^{*}}

A
stable isomorphism
of split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formations is an isomorphism

(\alpha,\beta,\nu)\colon(F_{1},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\delta_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{1},\theta_{1}))\oplus(H_{1},H_{1}^{*})\xrightarrow{\cong}(F_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{2}\\
\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{2},\theta_{2}))\oplus(H_{2},{H_{2}}^{*})

for some f.g. free based
R
-modules
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.

We use
\cong
to denote the isomorphism relation and
\cong_{s}
to denote stable isomorphism relation.
A split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation is
trivial
if it is isomorphic to
(F,F^{*})=(F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(F^{*},0)).

The
composition
of stable isomorphisms
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
and
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
is defined as

(13)

(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\circ(\alpha,\beta,\nu):=(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha,\beta^{\prime}\beta,\nu+\alpha^{-1}\nu^{\prime}\alpha^{-*}).

The
inverse
of a stable isomorphism
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
is defined as
(\alpha^{-1},\beta^{-1},-\alpha\nu\alpha^{*})
.
The
identity
on a split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation
x
is
(1,1,0)
.
The next example shows that isometries of quadratic forms give rise to isomorphisms of their so-called boundary split formations.

Example 4.3
.

The
boundary
of a
(-\varepsilon)
-quadratic form
(P,\psi)
is the split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formation

\partial(P,\psi):=(P,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\psi-\varepsilon\psi^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(P,\psi)).

The
boundary of an isometry

h\colon(P,\psi)\to(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
of simple quadratic forms is the isomorphism

\partial h=(h,h,0)\colon\partial(P,\psi)\to\partial(P^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}).

4.2.
Boundary isomorphisms

In this subsection, we review homotopies between stable isomorphisms of formations.
References include
[
CS11
, Section 4.1]
and
[
Ran81
, Section 1.6]
.

Here is a short motivation for the definition.
Namely, given two isomorphisms of split quadratic formations

(\alpha,\beta,\nu),(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\colon(F_{1},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\delta_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{1},\theta_{1}))\to(F_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{2}\\
\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{2},\theta_{2})),

we will define a homotopy
\Delta\colon(\alpha,\beta,\nu)\to(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.
There is a one to one correspondence between stable isomorphism classes of split quadratic formations and chain equivalence classes of
1
-dimensional split quadratic complexes
[
Ran81
, Proposition 1.6.4]
.
Regardless of the definition of a quadratic complex, a chain complex corresponding to the split quadratic formation
(F,\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma\\
\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G,\theta))
is
0\to F\xrightarrow{\delta^{*}}G^{*}\to 0
.
The second condition of Definition
4.2
implies that the isomorphisms
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
of split quadratic formations fit into the following diagram:

(14)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G_{1}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\beta}^{-*}}
\scriptstyle{{{\beta^{\prime}}^{-*}}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}^{*}}
\textstyle{G_{2}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

In (
14
), the dashed map
\Delta
suggests the following definition for a homotopy between two isomorphisms of split formations.

Definition 4.4
.

A
homotopy
of isomorphisms
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
between split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formations
(F_{1},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{1}\\
\delta_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{1},\theta_{1}))
and
(F_{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\gamma_{2}\\
\delta_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(G_{2},\theta_{2}))
consists of an
R
-linear map
\Delta\colon G_{1}^{*}\to F_{2}
which satisfies

(i)

{\beta^{\prime}}^{-*}-\beta^{-*}={\delta_{2}}^{*}\Delta,

(ii)

\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha=\Delta\delta_{1}^{*},

(iii)

\alpha^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}{\alpha^{\prime}}^{*}-\alpha\nu\alpha^{*}=(\varepsilon\alpha^{\prime}\gamma_{1}+\Delta\theta)\Delta^{*}\in Q_{-\varepsilon}(F_{2}^{*}).

Next, we move on to stable homotopies.
Briefly, two stable isomorphisms are stably homotopic if they become homotopic in a common stabilization.
The formal definition reads as follows.

Definition 4.5
.

Given trivial split formations
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}
,
two isomorphisms
f_{1}\colon x\oplus u_{1}\to y\oplus v_{1}
and
f_{2}\colon x\oplus u_{2}\to y\oplus v_{2}

are
stably homotopic
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

there are based modules
R_{1},R_{2},P,Q
and isomorphisms

\displaystyle u_{1}\oplus(R_{1},R_{1}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{1},\cong}}{{\longleftarrow}}(P,P^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{2},\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}u_{2}\oplus(R_{2},R_{2}^{*}),

\displaystyle v_{1}\oplus(R_{1},R_{1}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{1},\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(Q,Q^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h_{2},\cong}}{{\longleftarrow}}v_{2}\oplus(R_{2},R_{2}^{*});

(ii)

there is a homotopy

\widetilde{f}_{1}\simeq\widetilde{f}_{2}\colon x\oplus(P,P^{*})\to y\oplus(Q,Q^{*}),

where the isomorphisms
\widetilde{f}_{1},\widetilde{f}_{2}
are

\tilde{f}_{i}:=(\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{y}\oplus h_{i})\circ(f_{i}\oplus\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{(R_{i},R_{i}^{*})})\circ(\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{x}\oplus g_{i})
for
i=1,2
:

\textstyle{x\oplus(P,P^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{x}\oplus g_{i}}
\textstyle{x\oplus u_{i}\oplus(R_{i},R_{i}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}\oplus\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{(R_{i},R_{i}^{*})}}
\textstyle{y\oplus(Q,Q^{*})}
\textstyle{y\oplus v_{i}\oplus(R_{i},R_{i}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{y}\oplus h_{i}}

Given simple split
\varepsilon
-quadratic formations
y
and
z
, and using
[f]
to denote the stable homotopy class of a stable isomorphism
f\colon y\cong_{s}z
, we set

\operatorname{Iso}(y,z):=\{[f]\ |\ f\text{ is a stable isomorphism from $y$ to $z$}\}.

For quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
, there are left actions of
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)
and
\operatorname{Aut}(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
on the set
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))
, respectively given by
g\cdot[f]:=f\circ\partial g^{-1}
and
h\cdot[f]:=\partial h\circ f
.
Here, recall from Example
4.3
that
\partial g^{-1}
and
\partial h
refer to the boundaries of the isometries
g^{-1}
and
h
.
Since these actions commute, we obtain an action of
\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\times\operatorname{Aut}(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
by setting
(g,h)\cdot f=\partial h\circ f\circ\partial g^{-1}
.
Modding out by these left actions leads to the main definition of this section.

Definition 4.6
.

Given quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
, the
one-sided boundary isomorphism sets
and
boundary isomorphism set
are respectively defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Iso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\,/\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta),

\displaystyle\operatorname{rIso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Iso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\,/\operatorname{Aut}(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bIso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{Iso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\,/\operatorname{Aut}(V,\theta)\times\operatorname{Aut}(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}).

When
(V,\theta)=(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
we
replace the word “isomorphism” with “automorphism” and set
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta)):=\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V,\theta))
.
For example, the
boundary automorphism set of
(V,\theta)

is the set
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(V,\theta):=\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(V,\theta)\times\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(V,\theta)
.

Informally, non-trivial elements of
\operatorname{bIso}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))
consist of stable isomorphisms of split boundary formations that are not induced by isometries of forms.
The boundary isomorphism set was introduced in
[
CS11
, Section 5]
.
We discuss the reasons why we consider
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits
.

Remark 4.7
.

As we saw before Example
4.3
, the composition of boundary isomorphisms gives rise to a composition

\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\times\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}))\to\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})).

It follows that
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))
is a group.
On the other hand, since the image of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(V,\theta)
in
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))

need not be a normal subgroup of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))
, the composition does
not
descend to
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))
in general.
Nevertheless, composition does induce maps

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\times\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}))\to\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))\times\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits(\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime}))\to\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})).

Another more topological motivation for the appearance of
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits
will feature in Section
7
below.
We conclude with a remark on some identifications we will often make in the sequel.

Remark 4.8
.

Generalising
[
CS11
, Remark 5.6]
, we observe that if
there are isometries
k\colon(V_{1},\theta_{1})\to(V_{2},\theta_{2})
and
h\colon(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime})\to(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})
,
then the three assignments
[f]\mapsto[f\circ\partial k^{-1}],[f]\mapsto[\partial h\circ f]
and
[f]\mapsto[\partial h\circ f\circ\partial k^{-1}]
respectively give rise to bijections

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}),\partial(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\approx}\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V_{2},\theta_{2}),\partial(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\operatorname{rIso}(\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}),\partial(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\approx}\operatorname{rIso}(\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}),\partial(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bIso}(\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}),\partial(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\approx}\operatorname{bIso}(\partial(V_{2},\theta_{2}),\partial(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})),

which are independent of the choices of the isometries
h
and
k
.
For instance, if
h^{\prime}\colon(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime})\to(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})
is another isometry, then
\partial h^{\prime}\circ f=\partial(h^{\prime}\circ h^{-1})\circ\partial h\circ f
agrees with
\partial h\circ f
in
\operatorname{rIso}(\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1}),\partial(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime}))
.

Thus in this situation, given
f\colon\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime})
, in order to compare boundary isomorphisms, we will sometimes write
[f]\in\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(\partial(V_{2},\theta_{2}),\partial(V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime}))
, in which case the class we are really referring to is
[\widetilde{f}]
, where
\widetilde{f}=\partial h\circ f\circ\partial k^{-1}
for some isometries
h\colon(V_{1}^{\prime},\theta_{1}^{\prime})\to\penalty\ (V_{2}^{\prime},\theta_{2}^{\prime})
and
k\colon(V_{1},\theta_{1})\to(V_{2},\theta_{2})
, and the choice of isometries is immaterial.

4.3.
Gluing of forms

In this subsection, we recall how two quadratic forms can be glued along a stable isomorphism of their boundary formations.
References for this construction include
[
Ran81
, Section 1.7]
and
[
CS11
, Section 4.2]
.

Fix
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
and let
\lambda=\theta+\varepsilon\theta^{*}
and
\lambda^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}+\varepsilon{\theta^{\prime}}^{*}
be the corresponding symmetric forms.
The following lemma provides a characterisation of stable isomorphisms
\partial(V,\theta)\cong_{s}\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
; a proof can be found in
[
CS11
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be based modules.
Let
\alpha\colon V\oplus P\to V^{\prime}\oplus P^{\prime}
and
\beta\colon V\oplus P^{*}\to V^{\prime}\oplus{P^{\prime}}^{*}
be simple isomorphisms and let
\nu\in Q_{\varepsilon}({V^{\prime}}^{*}\oplus{P^{\prime}}^{*})
.
The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

the triple
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
defines a stable isomorphism

(\alpha,\beta,\nu)\colon\partial(V,\theta)\oplus(P,P^{*})\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\oplus(P^{\prime},{P^{\prime}}^{*});

(2)

there are homomorphisms
a,b,s
and
a_{1},b_{1},a_{3}
such that
\alpha=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&a_{1}\\
\varepsilon b_{1}^{*}\lambda&a_{3}^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
\beta^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&b_{1}\\
a_{1}^{*}\lambda^{\prime}&a_{3}^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\alpha\nu\alpha^{*}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}s&-\varepsilon ab_{1}\\
0&-b_{1}^{*}\theta b_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in Q_{\varepsilon}({V^{\prime}}^{*}\oplus{P^{\prime}}^{*})
which satisfy

(i)

1=ab+(s^{*}+\varepsilon s)\lambda^{\prime},

(ii)

a^{*}\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda b,

(iii)

\theta^{\prime}=b^{*}\theta b+{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}s\lambda^{\prime}\in Q_{\varepsilon}(V^{\prime})
. ∎

From now on, we refer to the homomorphisms
a,b,s
as the
a
,
b
, and
s
components
of the stable isomorphism
f
.
We give some examples of these components.

Example 4.10
.

Given an isometry
h\colon(V,\theta)\to(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
, the components of
\partial h=(h,h,0)
are given by
a_{\partial h}=h,b_{\partial h}=h^{-1}
and
s_{\partial h}=0
.
Consequently, if
f
is a stable isomorphism with components
a,b,s
, and if
h_{1}\colon(V,\theta)\to(V,\theta),h_{2}\colon(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\to(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
are isometries, then the stable isomorphism
(h_{1},h_{2})\cdot f=\partial h_{2}\circ f\circ\partial h_{1}^{-1}
has components
h_{2}ah_{1}^{-1},h_{1}bh_{2}^{-1}
and
h_{2}sh_{2}^{*}
.

In the sequel, we will often use the following criterion for stable homotopy.

Remark 4.11
.

Two stable isomorphisms
(\alpha,\beta,\nu),(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\colon\partial(V_{1},\theta_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong_{s}}\partial(V_{2},\theta_{2})
are stably homotopic if and only if there is a
\Delta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(V^{*},V^{\prime})
such that
a^{\prime}-a=\Delta\lambda,b^{\prime}-b=\Delta^{*}{\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}
, and
s^{\prime}-s=(-\varepsilon a^{\prime}\Delta^{*}-\Delta\theta)\Delta^{*}

[
CS11
, Lemma 4.3 ii)]
.
In particular, if two stable isomorphisms have the same
a,b
and
s
components, then they are stably homotopic via
\Delta=0
.

The main definition of this subsection is the following.

Definition 4.12
.

The
union
of two quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
along a stable isomorphism
f\colon\partial(V,\theta)\oplus(P,P^{*})\to\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\oplus(P^{\prime},{P^{\prime}}^{*})
is defined to be the quadratic form

(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime}):=\left(V\oplus{V^{\prime}}^{*},\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
\varepsilon a&-s\end{pmatrix}\right),

where
a
and
s
denote the components of
f
as described in Lemma
4.9
.

Up to isometry, the union
(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
only depends on the stable homotopy class of the stable isomorphism
f

[
CS11
, Lemma 4.6 iii)]
.
In fact, more is known:
(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
only depends on the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}))

[
CS11
, Lemma 4.6 iv)]
.
The next lemma describes isometric inclusions of
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
into
(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
. These maps will be used frequently in the upcoming subsections.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
f\colon\partial(V,\theta)\oplus(P,P^{*})\to\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})\oplus(P,P^{*})
be an isomorphism of split quadratic formations, with components
a,b,s
.
The following maps are split isometric injections:

(i)

j_{f}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon(V,\theta)\to(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
,

(ii)

j_{f}^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})\to(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
.

Furthermore,
(V,\theta)
and
(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
are complementary inside the union:
(V,\theta)^{\perp}=j^{\prime}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
.

The gluing construction described in Definition
4.12
produces a nonsingular quadratic form starting from a boundary isomorphism.
Combining this with Lemma
4.13
, we conclude that two split isometric injections can be extracted from any boundary isomorphism.
The next proposition provides a converse of sorts: starting from a split isometric embedding, it constructs a boundary isomorphism. We refer to
[
CS11
, Proposition 4.8]
for a proof.

Proposition 4.14
.

Given a split isometric injection
j\colon(V,\theta)\hookrightarrow(M,\psi)
of quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
and
(M,\psi)
, one can construct

(i)

an isomorphism
f_{j}\colon\partial(V,\theta)\oplus(M^{\prime},{M^{\prime}}^{*})\to\partial(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})\oplus(M,M^{*})

that is well defined up to homotopy, where
M^{\prime}:=V^{\perp}\oplus{V^{\perp}}^{*};

(ii)

an isometry
r_{j}\colon(M,\psi)\xrightarrow{\cong}(V,\theta)\cup_{f_{j}}(V^{\perp},\theta^{\perp}).

Furthermore,
f_{j}
and
r_{j}
are natural with respect to isometries of such pairs of forms.

For later use, we recall the explicit formula for the isomorphism
f_{j}
of Proposition
4.14
.
Following
[
CS11
, proof of Proposition 4.8]
,
we write
(M^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}):=H_{\varepsilon}(V^{\perp})
, and let
\phi^{\prime}
be the
\varepsilon
-symmetrization of
\psi^{\prime}
.
The formula for the stable isomorphism
f_{j}=(\alpha_{f_{j}},\beta_{f_{j}},\nu_{f_{j}})
is given by

\displaystyle f_{j}\colon\partial(V,\theta)\oplus(M^{\prime},{M^{\prime}}^{*})\to\partial(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})\oplus(M,M^{*})

(15)

\displaystyle f_{j}:=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\phi\\
-\phi^{-*}\psi\phi^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right)\circ\partial h\circ\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\oplus\begin{pmatrix}1\\
{\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}\\
{\phi^{\prime}}^{-*}{\psi^{\prime}}^{*}{\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\right),

To decode this a little, recalling that
\partial h=(h,h,0)
and the composition law from (
13
), the notation implies that
f_{j}=(\alpha_{f_{j}},\beta_{f_{j}},\nu_{f_{j}})
is given by

\alpha_{f_{j}}=\left((1)\oplus(1)\right)\circ h\circ\left((1)\oplus(1)\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\circ h\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),

and similarly for the
\beta
and
\nu
components.
Here
the isometry
h
is given by

\displaystyle h\colon(V,\theta)\oplus H_{\varepsilon}(V^{\perp})\to(V^{\perp},-\theta^{\perp})\oplus(M,\psi)

(16)

\displaystyle h:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
j^{\perp}&\sigma&j\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j&1&-\sigma^{*}\psi^{*}\sigma\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

In this formula,
j^{\perp}\colon V^{\perp}\to M
is the inclusion of
V^{\perp}
, and
\sigma\colon(V^{\perp})^{*}\to M
is a splitting of the short exact sequence

(17)

0\to V\xrightarrow{j}M\xrightarrow{{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi}(V^{\perp})^{*}\to 0

with
({j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)\sigma=\Id.

This implies that
\widetilde{\sigma}:=j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)
is a splitting for
j
, since
{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi j=0
, and so
j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)j=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
. Here the notation
j^{-1}
is justified since the image of
(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)
is a subset of
\ker({j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)=\im(j)
.

The next proposition describes the
a,b,s
components of
f_{j}
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
j\colon(V,\theta)\hookrightarrow(M,\psi)
be a split isometric injection.
The
a,b,s
-components of the isomorphism
f_{j}
described in Proposition
4.14
are

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j,

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{\sigma}j^{\perp},

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\psi\sigma.

Proof.

By definition, the
b
component of a stable isomorphism
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
can be read off from the
(1,1)
-entry of
\beta^{-1}
.
Write
f_{j}
as
f_{j}:=J\circ\partial h\circ K
, where
J=(\alpha_{J},\beta_{J},\nu_{J})
and
K=(\alpha_{K},\beta_{K},\nu_{K})
are the stable isomorphisms that appear in (
4.3
).
Since
\beta_{J}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&\phi\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\beta_{K}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&{\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, the
b
component of
f_{j}
is equal to the
b
component of
\partial h
.
In order to understand the
b
component of
\partial h=(h,h,0)
, we compute
h^{-1}
.
To achieve this,
recall from (
17
) that we have the split exact sequence

(18)

0\to(V,\theta)\xrightarrow{j}(M,\psi)\xrightarrow{{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi}(V^{\perp},\theta^{\perp})^{*}\to 0,

that we used
\sigma
to denote a splitting of
{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi
, and that
\widetilde{\sigma}:=j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)
is a splitting for
j
.
Write the isometry
h
as
h=AB,
where
A
and
B
are the matrices that appear in (
4.3
).
A direct computation shows that both
A
and
B
are invertible, and

\displaystyle h^{-1}=B^{-1}A^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j&1&-\sigma^{*}\psi^{*}\sigma\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
j^{\perp}&\sigma&j\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&\sigma^{*}\psi^{*}j&\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\lambda^{\perp}&{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi\\
-j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)j^{\perp}&j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)\end{pmatrix}.

Although

A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
j^{\perp}&\sigma&j\end{pmatrix}\colon V^{\perp}\oplus(V^{\perp})^{*}\oplus V\to V^{\perp}\oplus M

is written as a non-square matrix, in fact
M\cong(V^{\perp})^{*}\oplus V
, so the domain and codomain of
A
coincide, and moreover
A
is an isomorphism.
Recall that the
b
component of
f_{j}
is equal to the
b
component of
\partial h
.
Since the
b
component of
\partial h=(h,h,0)
is equal to the
(1,1)
-entry
h^{-1}_{11}
of
h^{-1}
, we deduce that

(19)

b_{f_{j}}=b_{\partial h}=h^{-1}_{11}=-j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)j^{\perp}=-\widetilde{\sigma}j^{\perp},

where the last equality uses the definition of
\widetilde{\sigma}
.
Next, we compute the
a
component of
f_{j}
.
We write
f_{j}
as
f_{j}:=J\circ\partial h\circ K
, where the stable isomorphisms
J=(\alpha_{J},\beta_{J},\nu_{J})
and
K=(\alpha_{K},\beta_{K},\nu_{K})
are those which appear in (
4.3
).
Note that
\alpha_{J}
and
\alpha_{K}
are both identity matrices.
As
\partial h=(h,h,0)
, the definition of the composition of stable isomorphisms gives

\alpha_{f_{j}}=\alpha_{J}\circ\alpha_{\partial h}\circ\alpha_{K}=\alpha_{\partial h}=h.

Thus the
a
component of
f_{j}
is given by the
(1,1)
entry
h_{11}
of
h
.
The definition of
h
from (
4.3
) implies that
h_{11}=-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j
, and thus

a_{f_{j}}=h_{11}=-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j.

Finally, we compute the
s
component of
f_{j}
.
Recalling the composition law
\nu^{\prime}\circ\nu=\nu+\alpha^{-1}\nu^{\prime}\alpha^{-*}
from (
13
), we deduce that
\nu_{J\circ\partial h}=\nu_{\partial h}+\alpha_{\partial h}^{-1}\nu_{J}\alpha_{\partial h}^{-*}
.
Apply
\nu_{\partial h}=0
and
\alpha_{\partial h}=h
to obtain
\nu_{J\circ\partial h}=h^{-1}\nu_{J}h^{-*}
.
Use the composition law a second time, observing that
\alpha_{K}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
, to obtain

\displaystyle\nu_{f_{j}}=\nu_{J\circ\partial h\circ K}=\nu_{K}+\alpha_{K}^{-1}\nu_{J\circ\partial h}\alpha_{K}^{-*}=\nu_{K}+h^{-1}\nu_{J}h^{-*}.

By definition, the
s
component of
f_{j}
is obtained as the
(1,1)
-entry of

\displaystyle\alpha_{f_{j}}\nu_{f_{j}}\alpha_{f_{j}}^{*}=h(\nu_{K}+h^{-1}\nu_{J}h^{-*})h^{*}=h\nu_{K}h^{*}+\nu_{J}.

A glance at (
4.3
) shows that the
(1,1)
-entry of
\nu_{J}
is zero, so the
s
component of
f_{j}
coincides with the
(1,1)
-entry of
h\nu_{K}h^{*}
.
In order to compute
\nu_{K}
, we use that
\psi^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\phi^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
\varepsilon&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
to deduce that

{\phi^{\prime}}^{-*}\psi^{\prime}{\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
\varepsilon&0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

It follows from (
4.3
) and this computation that

(20)

\nu_{K}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&{\phi^{\prime}}^{-*}\psi^{\prime}{\phi^{\prime}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\\
=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&\varepsilon&0\end{pmatrix}.

Writing the entries of
h
as
h_{ij}
, a brief computation using (
4.3
) and (
20
) now implies that

s_{f_{j}}=(h\nu_{K}h^{*})_{11}=\varepsilon h_{13}h_{12}^{*}=-\varepsilon(\sigma^{*}\psi^{*}\sigma)=-\sigma^{*}\psi\sigma.

We have therefore obtained the announced values for the
a,b,s
components of
f_{j}
.
∎

From now on, we will often denote quadratic forms by
v
instead of
(V,\theta)
.
Next, we give a concrete example of Proposition
4.15
in the case where
m:=(M,\psi)
is a union.

Example 4.16
.

We apply Proposition
4.15
to the situation where
m=v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
for some stable isomorphism
f\colon\partial v\xrightarrow{\cong_{s}}\partial v^{\prime}
, and where
j=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon v\hookrightarrow v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
.
Using the notation of Proposition
4.15
, we have
\psi:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\theta&0\\
\varepsilon a&-s\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with symmetrisation
\phi:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&\varepsilon a^{*}\\
a&-(s+\varepsilon s^{*})\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and
j^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We deduce that
j^{\perp}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\lambda^{\perp}={j^{\perp}}^{*}\psi j^{\perp}=-\theta^{\prime}
.
Consequently, a splitting of
{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is
\sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and the corresponding splitting of
j
is

\widetilde{\sigma}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{f_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}j=-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&\varepsilon a^{*}\\
a&-(s+\varepsilon s^{*})\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-a,

\displaystyle b_{f_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{\sigma}j^{\perp}=-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-b,

\displaystyle s_{f_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\psi\sigma=-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\theta&0\\
\varepsilon a&-s\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=s.

Consider the isometries
(-1)\colon v\to v
and
(-1)^{\prime}\colon v^{\prime}\to v^{\prime}
that are induced by multiplication by
-1
.
The boundaries of these isometries are
(-1,-1,0)
.
The stable isomorphism
f_{j}
has the same components as
(\partial(-1)^{\prime}\oplus 1)\circ f
and
f\circ(\partial(-1)\oplus 1)^{-1}
.
By Remark
4.11
, these stable isomorphisms are thus pairwise stably homotopic.
In particular, while
f
and
f_{j}
might not agree in
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
, they do agree in
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.
In particular, they also agree in
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.

Given forms
v,v^{\prime}
, we are interested in the set of
[f]\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
such that the algebraic union
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
is a fixed isometry type
m
:

(21)

\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}):=\{[f]\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\,|\,v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\cong m\}

Here, observe that if
m\cong m^{\prime}
then, by definition, we have
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})=\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m^{\prime}}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
: postcomposing the isomorphism
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\cong m
with the isomorphism
m\cong m^{\prime}
, we deduce that
v\cup_{f}\penalty\ -v^{\prime}\cong\penalty\ m^{\prime}
.
The analogous definitions and remarks can be made for
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits,\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits
.
We conclude this subsection by recalling a concrete example over the integers.

Example 4.17
.

Given a product
q=p_{1}\cdots p_{k}
of
k
distinct odd primes,
[
CS11
, Section 6.3]
implies that
\operatorname{bAut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z},q))
is a group and
\operatorname{bAut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z},q))\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{k-1}
.
Since we are working over
\mathbb{Z}
, any twisted double
w\cup_{f}-w
is hyperbolic
[
Ran98
, Proposition 3.11 and Remark 29.18]
,
and we obtain

\operatorname{bAut}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}(\partial(\mathbb{Z},q))=\operatorname{bAut}(\partial(\mathbb{Z},q))\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{k-1}.

We will prove a generalisation of this result in Example
6.7
below.

5.
Infinite boundary automorphism sets

The aim of this section, which is again entirely algebraic, is to provide examples of infinite boundary automorphism sets.
For our topological applications, we must find infinitely many
(+1)
-quadratic forms
(V,\theta)
such that the set
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}])}(\partial(V,\theta))
is infinite.
Here recall that this set consists of those (equivalence classes of) boundary isomorphisms whose double is hyperbolic over
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]=\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}]
.
By
doubling
, we mean the map
\kappa(f)=(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)
that takes a boundary isomorphism to the corresponding algebraic union.

Throughout this section we make the following assumptions:

(i)

\pi
is an infinite abelian group (e.g.
\mathbb{Z}
);

(ii)

\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
is its group ring;

(iii)

our forms
(V,\theta)
are
(+1)
-quadratic (
\varepsilon=1
) over a free module of rank
1
, so that we will have
V=\Lambda,\lambda=\overline{\lambda}=\theta+\overline{\theta}\in\Lambda,\theta\in\Lambda
.

Note that under these assumptions, we have
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(V,\theta)=\mathop{\rm rAut}\nolimits(V,\theta)=\mathop{\rm\ell Aut}\nolimits(V,\theta)
.
Furthermore, given an integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, we will often use the following notation:

(i)

q=1-4p^{2}
,

(ii)

\theta=pq
,

(iii)

\lambda=2\theta=2pq.

Note that
(2p)(2p)+q=1
and therefore
2p
and
q
are coprime. We use this fact below when decomposing
\mathbb{Z}/\lambda
.
We first define the boundary automorphisms of interest.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\pi
be an abelian group and let
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Set
q:=1-4p^{2}
and consider the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=(\Lambda,pq)
.
Using the notation of Lemma
4.9
, for every
g\in\pi
there is a boundary isomorphism
t_{g}=(\alpha,\beta,\nu)\colon\partial(V,\theta)\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(V,\theta)
given by

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}s&=&-g+p&&b&=&2pg+q\\
b_{1}&=&-(s+\overline{s})&&a&=&\overline{b}\\
a_{1}&=&1&&a_{3}&=&b\\
\alpha&=&\begin{pmatrix}\overline{b}&1\\
-(s+\overline{s})\lambda&b\end{pmatrix}&&\beta^{-1}&=&\begin{pmatrix}b&-(s+\overline{s})\\
\lambda&\overline{b}\end{pmatrix}\\
\alpha\nu\alpha^{*}&=&\begin{pmatrix}s&\overline{b}(s+\overline{s})\\
0&-(s+\overline{s})\theta(s+\overline{s})\end{pmatrix}.\end{array}

Proof.

We need to go through the checklist of Lemma
4.9
.

(i)

Since
\lambda
is an integer, we have
a^{*}\lambda=\lambda b
.

(ii)

Next, we calculate:

\displaystyle ab

\displaystyle=\overline{b}b

\displaystyle=(2pg^{-1}+q)(2pg+q)=4p^{2}+2pq(g+g^{-1})+q^{2}=(1-q)+q^{2}+2pq(g+g^{-1})

\displaystyle=1+q(q{-}1)+\lambda(g+g^{-1})=1+q(-4p^{2})+\lambda(g+g^{-1})=1-2p\lambda+\lambda(g+g^{-1})

\displaystyle=1+\lambda(g+g^{-1}-2p)=1-(s+\overline{s})\lambda.

(iii)

The next calculation takes place in
Q_{+1}(\Lambda)=\Lambda/(x-\overline{x})
. Using the previous result we check

\displaystyle b^{*}\theta b

\displaystyle=\theta\overline{b}b=\theta(1-(s+\overline{s})\lambda)=\theta-(s+\overline{s})\theta\lambda

\displaystyle=\theta-(s+\overline{s})\theta\lambda+(\overline{s}\theta\lambda)-\overline{(\overline{s}\theta\lambda)}

\displaystyle=\theta-2s\theta\lambda=\theta-\lambda s\lambda=\theta-\overline{\lambda}s\lambda.

(iv)

We need to show that
\alpha
is a simple isomorphism. We compute the product of these two simple isomorphisms using the first calculation

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&b\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\overline{b}&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\overline{b}&1\\
-1+b\overline{b}&b\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{b}&1\\
-1+1-(s+\overline{s})\lambda&b\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{b}&1\\
-(s+\overline{s})\lambda&b\end{pmatrix}=\alpha.

(v)

At last we need to show that the matrix labelled
\beta^{-1}
is indeed an isomorphism:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}b&-(s+\overline{s})\\
\lambda&\overline{b}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{b}&(s+\overline{s})\\
-\lambda&b\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}b\overline{b}+(s+\overline{s})\lambda&b(s+\overline{s})-(s+\overline{s})b\\
\lambda\overline{b}-\overline{b}\lambda&\lambda(s+\overline{s})+\overline{b}b\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

This shows that
t_{g}=(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
is a boundary isomorphism and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Definition 5.2
.

Given a ring
R
with involution, the group of
unitary units
is defined as

U(R)=\{x\in R\ |\ x\overline{x}=1\}.

Lemma 5.3
.

For
\pi
an abelian group, we have
U(\Lambda)=\{\pm g|g\in\pi\}
.

Proof.

Let
x=\sum_{g\in\pi}n_{g}g\in U(\Lambda)
. Then

\displaystyle 1=x\overline{x}=\sum_{g\in\pi}n_{g}g\cdot\sum_{g\in\pi}n_{g}g^{-1}.

The component of the product for
1\in\pi
must fulfill
1=\sum n_{g}^{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
which means that exactly one of the
n_{g}
is
\pm 1
and the others are zero.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\pi
be an abelian group and let
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Set
q:=1-4p^{2}
and consider the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=(\Lambda,pq)
.
The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(V,\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{U(\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{U(\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\pi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits\partial(V,\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{U(\Lambda/\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{U(\Lambda/2p)\times U(\Lambda/q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(V,\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{U(\Lambda/\lambda)/U(\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(U(\Lambda/2p)\times U(\Lambda/q))/U(\Lambda),}

where the maps are defined as follows:

\displaystyle f\colon x

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle x;\quad\text{}

\displaystyle\partial\colon x

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle\partial x=(x,x,0)\quad\text{$($see Remark\penalty\ \ref{ex:BoundarySplitFormation}$)$;}

\displaystyle j\colon x

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[x];

\displaystyle j^{\prime}\colon x

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle([x],[x]);

\displaystyle t\colon g

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle t_{g}\quad\text{from Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:Deftg};}

\displaystyle h\colon(\alpha,\beta,\nu)

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle b\quad\text{using the notation of Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:CS11Lemma43};}

\displaystyle r\colon[x]

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle([x],[x])\quad\text{induced by canonical isomorphism $\mathbb{Z}/2pq\cong\mathbb{Z}/2p\times\mathbb{Z}/q$;}

\displaystyle k\colon t

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[t]\quad\text{the projection to the quotient;}

\displaystyle l\colon[x]

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[x]\quad\text{the projection to the quotient;}

\displaystyle l^{\prime}\colon([x],[y])

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[[x],[y]]\quad\text{the projection to the quotient;}

\displaystyle h^{\prime}\colon[(\alpha,\beta,\nu)]

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle[b]\quad\text{the map induced on the quotients by $h$;}

\displaystyle r^{\prime}\colon[x]

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle([x],[x])\quad\text{the map induced on the quotients by $r$.}

Proof.

By construction
2p
and
q
are coprime, so the definition of
r
makes sense.
The map
h
is well-defined by Remark
4.11
.
We verify it is a group homomorphism.
If
(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
are automorphisms of
\partial(V,\theta)
, then
h(\alpha,\beta,\nu)h(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})=bb^{\prime}
, by definition of
h
.
In order to understand
h((\alpha,\beta,\nu)(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}))
, we use Lemma
4.9
to compute the product

(\beta\beta^{\prime})^{-1}=\beta^{\prime-1}\beta^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}b^{\prime}&b_{1}^{\prime}\\
{a^{\prime}}_{1}^{*}\lambda&{a^{\prime}}_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}b&b_{1}\\
a_{1}^{*}\lambda&a_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}b^{\prime}b+b^{\prime}_{1}a_{1}^{*}\lambda&*\\
*&*\end{pmatrix}

and deduce that
h((\alpha,\beta,\nu)(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}))=b^{\prime}b\in\Lambda/\lambda
, proving that
h
is indeed a homomorphism.

The top left square of the diagram now commutes:
j(f(x))=j(x)=[x]=h(x,x,0)=h(\partial(x))
.
The remainder of the verifications are straightforward and are left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Using the same notation as in Lemma
5.4
,
the map
l^{\prime}\circ r\circ h\circ t
is injective.

Proof.

Given
g\in\pi
, observe that

\displaystyle r\circ h\circ t(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r(2pg+q)=([2pg+q],[2pg+q])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle([q],[2pg])=([1-4p^{2}],[2pg])=(1,[2pg])\in U(\Lambda/2p)\times U(\Lambda/q).

If there are
g,g^{\prime}\in\pi
such that
l^{\prime}\circ r\circ h\circ t(g)=l^{\prime}\circ r\circ h\circ t(g^{\prime})
, then there is an
x\in U(\Lambda)
with

(1,[2pg])=([x],[x])(1,[2pg^{\prime}])\in U(\Lambda/2p)\times U(\Lambda/q).

From Lemma
5.3
, we know that
x=s\gamma
for
s\in\{\pm 1\},\gamma\in\pi
, and therefore

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}/2p[\pi],

\displaystyle 2pg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s\gamma 2pg^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}/q[\pi].

The first equation shows that
\gamma
is the identity element of
\pi
and therefore the second equation implies that
g=g^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\pi
be an abelian group and
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Set
q:=1-4p^{2}
and consider the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=(\Lambda,pq)
.
The map

\displaystyle k\circ t\colon\pi

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(V,\theta)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\longmapsto[t_{g}]=[(\alpha,\beta,\nu)]

is injective, where
k
and
t
are defined in Lemma
5.4
and

(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
is defined as in Lemma
5.1
.
Hence if
\pi
is infinite, the subset
\{[t_{g}]\,|\,g\in\pi\}\subseteq\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(V,\theta)
is infinite.

Proof.

Lemma
5.5
showed that
l^{\prime}\circ r\circ h\circ t
is injective. According to the diagram in Lemma
5.4
, this is the same as
r^{\prime}\circ h^{\prime}\circ k\circ t
. This implies that
k\circ t
is already injective.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\pi
be an abelian group and
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Set
q:=1-4p^{2}
and consider the quadratic form
(V,\theta)=(\Lambda,pq)
.
Given
g\in\pi
, the quadratic form
(V,\theta)\cup_{\,t_{g}}(V,-\theta)
is hyperbolic.

Proof.

Recall from Definition
4.12
that the gluing construction gives

\kappa(t_{g}):=(V,\theta)\cup_{\,t_{g}}(V,-\theta)=\left(\Lambda^{2},\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
\overline{b}&-s\end{pmatrix}\right)=\left(\Lambda^{2},\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
2pg^{-1}+q&g-p\end{pmatrix}\right).

Consider the following simple isomorphism
u\in SL_{2}(\Lambda)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-p&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&g^{-1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
2p&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1+2pg^{-1}&g^{-1}\\
p-2p^{2}g^{-1}&1-pg^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

and then compute in
Q_{1}(\Lambda^{2})
:

\displaystyle u^{*}Hu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1+2pg^{-1}&g^{-1}\\
p-2p^{2}g^{-1}&1-pg^{-1}\end{pmatrix}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1+2pg^{-1}&g^{-1}\\
p-2p^{2}g^{-1}&1-pg^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1+2pg&p-2p^{2}g\\
g&1-pg\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1+2pg^{-1}&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}(p-2p^{2}g)(1+2pg^{-1})&(p-2p^{2}g)g^{-1}\\
(1-pg)(1+2pg^{-1})&(1-pg)g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p-4p^{3}+2p^{2}g^{-1}-2p^{2}g&pg^{-1}-2p^{2}\\
1-2p^{2}+2pg^{-1}-pg&g^{-1}-p\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p-4p^{3}+2p^{2}g^{-1}-2p^{2}g&pg^{-1}-2p^{2}\\
1-2p^{2}+2pg^{-1}-pg&g^{-1}-p\end{pmatrix}+(1-T)\begin{pmatrix}2p^{2}g&-(pg^{-1}-2p^{2})\\
0&-g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}p-4p^{3}&0\\
2pg^{-1}+(1-4p^{2})&g-p\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta&0\\
2pg^{-1}+q&g-p\end{pmatrix}.

This shows that
\kappa(t_{g})
is hyperbolic and concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Specialising to
\pi=\mathbb{Z}
, the following result will be used in our topological applications.

Theorem 5.8
.

For
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\theta=p(1-4p^{2})
, the set
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}])}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}],\theta))
is infinite.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.6
,
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}],\theta))
contains the infinite set
\{[t_{g}]\ |\ g\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where the
t_{g}
were constructed in Lemma
5.1
.
By Proposition
5.7
, each of the boundary automorphisms
[t_{g}]
belongs to

\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}])}(\partial(\mathbb{Z}[t^{\pm 1}],\theta))
, which establishes the result.
∎

6.
Primitive embeddings

This section, the last that develops algebraic machinery, gives a new formulation of the boundary automorphism set. Section
6.1
introduces primitive embeddings and Section
6.2

describes how primitive embeddings are related to boundary isomorphisms.

Not only are primitive embeddings simpler to define than boundary isomorphisms, they will also be used in Section
7
to describe the image
\delta(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))
of the modified surgery obstruction for the normal
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
constructed in Theorem
3.10
.

6.1.
2-sided primitive embeddings

The aim of this subsection is to introduce 2-sided primitive embeddings.
In what follows, we shall often write quadratic forms as
v=(V,\theta)
.

Motivated by Lemma
4.13
, the main definition of this section is the following.

Definition 6.1
.

A
primitive embedding
is a split isometric injection
i\colon v\hookrightarrow m
of
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms, where
m
is nonsingular.
A
2-sided primitive embedding
is a pair
v\xhookrightarrow{i}m\xhookleftarrow{i^{\prime}}v^{\prime}
of primitive embeddings such that
i(v)^{\perp}=i^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
.

We already encountered examples of 2-sided primitive embeddings in Section
4.3
.

Example 6.2
.

Given a boundary isomorphism
f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
, the inclusions described in Lemma
4.13
provide a 2-sided primitive embedding
v\hookrightarrow v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\hookleftarrow-v^{\prime}.

Two
2
-sided primitive embeddings
v\hookrightarrow m_{0}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
and
v\hookrightarrow m_{1}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
are
isomorphic
if there is an isometry
m_{0}\to m_{1}
which makes the following diagram commute:

(23)

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{m_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m_{1}}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We denote the isomorphism class of a 2-sided primitive embedding
(j,j^{\prime})
is by
[(j,j^{\prime})]
.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
v,v^{\prime}
and
m
be
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms such that there is a
2
-sided primitive embedding

v\hookrightarrow m\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
. Define the sets of isomorphism classes of
2
-sided
primitive embeddings

\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,v^{\prime}):=\{[(j,j^{\prime})]\ |\ v\xhookrightarrow{j}m\xhookleftarrow{j^{\prime}}v^{\prime}\text{ is a $2$-sided primitive embedding}\}

and

\operatorname{Prim}(v,v^{\prime}):=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,v^{\prime}),

where the latter disjoint union is taken over isomorphism classes of forms
m
which can be obtained by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together.

Next, we give explicit examples of primitive embeddings over the ring of integers.

Example 6.4
.

Given an integer
q=y_{1}y_{2}
with
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
coprime integers, the following maps define a 2-sided primitive embedding into the hyperbolic form
H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
:

(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}}):=\bigg((\mathbb{Z},q)\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},-q)\bigg).

One can also check that every 2-sided primitive embedding
(\mathbb{Z},q)\hookrightarrow H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\hookleftarrow(\mathbb{Z},-q)
is either of the form
(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
or of the form
(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},-j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
for some
y_{1},y_{2}
with
q=y_{1}y_{2}
.

For each factorisation
q=y_{1}y_{2}
(writing
q=(-y_{1})(-y_{2})
leads to the same observation), one can construct eight 2-sided primitive embedding
(\mathbb{Z},q)\hookrightarrow H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\hookleftarrow(\mathbb{Z},-q)
, namely
\pm(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
,
\pm(i_{y_{2},y_{1}},-j_{y_{2},y_{1}})
,
\pm(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},-j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
, and
\pm(i_{y_{2},y_{1}},j_{y_{2},y_{1}})
.
The first four (resp. last four) of these 2-sided primitive embeddings are isomorphic;
this is because isometries of
H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})
are of the form

\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\\
0&\varepsilon_{2}\end{pmatrix},\ \ \begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
\varepsilon_{2}&0\end{pmatrix},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}=1.

Summarising, for each factorisation
q=y_{1}y_{2}
, one can associate two isomorphism classes in
\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
, namely
[(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})]
and
[(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},-j_{y_{1},y_{2}})]
. These two classes are unchanged if the signs of both
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are reversed.

The groups
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v)
and
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v^{\prime})
both act on the left on
\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
: given a
2
-sided primitive embedding
(j,j^{\prime})
, and isometries
g\colon v\to v
and
h\colon v^{\prime}\to v^{\prime}
, one obtains a new 2-sided primitive embedding by setting

(g,h)\cdot[(j,j^{\prime})]:=[(j\circ g^{-1},j^{\prime}\circ h^{-1})]
.
Modding out by these actions leads to the second main definition of this section.

Definition 6.5
.

Given quadratic forms
v
and
v^{\prime}

the
one-sided boundary primitive embedding sets
and
boundary primitive embedding set
are respectively defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v)\times\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v^{\prime}).

Taking the union over all isomorphism classes of forms
m
obtained by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together,
we obtain
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits(v,v^{\prime}):=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime}),\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits(v,v^{\prime}):=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits(v,v^{\prime})

and
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits(v,v^{\prime}):=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
.

Thus, two
2
-sided primitive embeddings agree in
\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
) if and only if they fit in a commutative diagram as the one displayed in (
23
), but where instead one equality (resp. both equalities) are allowed to be self-isomorphisms.
For instance, two
2
-sided primitive embeddings
v\hookrightarrow m_{0}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
and
v\hookrightarrow m_{1}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
agree in
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits(v,v^{\prime})
if and only if there are isometries
m_{0}\to m_{1}
and
v\to v^{\prime}
that makes the following diagram commute:

(24)

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{m_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m_{1}}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

If
m\cong m^{\prime}
, then we saw in Section
4.3
that
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m^{\prime}}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.
The same property holds for 2-sided primitive embeddings: if
m\cong m^{\prime}
, then after postcomposing with the isomorphism
m\cong m^{\prime}
, a primitive embedding
v\hookrightarrow m
gives rise to a primitive embedding
v\hookrightarrow\penalty\ m^{\prime}
.
In Remark
4.8
, we also recalled that isometries
k\colon v\to w
and
h\colon v^{\prime}\to w^{\prime}
give rise to a canonical identification
\operatorname{bIso}_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\approx\operatorname{bIso}_{m}(\partial w,\partial w^{\prime})
via
[f]\mapsto[\partial h\circ f\circ\partial k^{-1}]
.
The analogous remark holds for 2-sided primitive embeddings.

Remark 6.6
.

Observe that isometries
k\colon v\xrightarrow{\cong}w
and
h\colon v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}w^{\prime}
give rise to a canonical identification
\operatorname{bPrim}_{m}(v,v^{\prime})\approx\operatorname{bPrim}_{m}(w,w^{\prime})
via
[(j,j^{\prime})]\mapsto[(j\circ h^{-1},j\circ k^{-1})]
.
Analogous remarks also hold for
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits
.
As a consequence, for instance, when comparing primitive embeddings it makes sense to say that
v_{0}\hookrightarrow m_{0}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
and
v_{1}\hookrightarrow m_{1}\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}

agree in
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m_{0}}(v_{0},v^{\prime})
: this means that these two
2
-sided primitive embeddings fit into a commutative diagram of the following type:

(25)

\textstyle{v_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{m_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{v_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m_{1}}
\textstyle{v^{\prime}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

In particular,
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m_{0}}(v_{0},v)
only depends on
v
and on the isometry type of
v_{0}
and
m
.
We emphasise that such identifications are not possible if one is merely working in
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits
.

Building on Example
6.4
, we now describe explicitly the elements of
\operatorname{Prim}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
.

Example 6.7
.

For a product
q=\pm p_{1}^{x_{1}}p_{2}^{x_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{x_{k}}\in\mathbb{Z}
of
k
distinct prime powers
we introduce some notation and define:

\mathcal{F}_{q}:=\operatorname{Prim}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
,

r\mathcal{F}_{q}:=\operatorname{rPrim}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
,

\ell\mathcal{F}_{q}:=\operatorname{rPrim}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
,

b\mathcal{F}_{q}:=\operatorname{bPrim}_{H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})}((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
.

We assert that
r\mathcal{F}_{q}=\ell\mathcal{F}_{q}=b\mathcal{F}_{q}
,
and

|b\mathcal{F}_{q}|=|r\mathcal{F}_{q}|=|\ell\mathcal{F}_{q}|=\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{F}_{q}|=2^{k-1}.

There are
2^{k-1}
ways to factorise
q
as a product
y_{1}y_{2}
with
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
coprime.
Indeed, since
\operatorname{gcd}(y_{1},y_{2})=1
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
, it suffices to choose whether or not the prime
p_{i}
appears in the prime decomposition of
y_{1}
.
Also note that Example
6.4
ensures that each of these factorisations determines two isomorphisms classes of 2-sided primitive embeddings;
[(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})]
and
[(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},-j_{y_{1},y_{2}})]
.
This establishes the claim about
|\mathcal{F}_{q}|
.

To conclude, notice that
(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
and
(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},-j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
agree in
r\mathcal{F}_{q}
(and therefore in
b\mathcal{F}_{q}
), as can be seen by glancing at the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},-q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{(-1)\cdot}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},-q).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
-y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

Replacing respectively the labels on the left, middle and right vertical arrows by
(-1)\cdot,-\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
=
shows that
(j_{y_{1},y_{2}},\iota_{y_{1},y_{2}})
and
(j_{y_{1},y_{2}},-\iota_{y_{1},y_{2}})
also agree in
\ell\mathcal{F}_{q}
.
We conclude that there are twice as many elements in
\mathcal{F}_{q}
as there are in
r\mathcal{F}_{q},\ell\mathcal{F}_{q}
and
b\mathcal{F}_{q}
.
This establishes the assertion.

The primitive embeddings in Example
6.7
being distinct is the algebra underlying the constructions from our paper
[
CCPS21
]
.
Next we give the primitive embeddings underlying the proof of
Theorem
1.1
.

Example 6.8
.

Let
(V,\theta)=(\Lambda,pq)
,
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
for
\pi
abelian,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q=1-4p^{2}

and recall from Proposition
5.7
that for each
g\in\pi
there is an
isometry

u_{g}\colon(V,\theta)\cup_{\,t_{g}}(V,-\theta)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{+}(\Lambda).

Here the module underlying
(V,\theta)\cup_{\,t_{g}}(V,-\theta)
is
V\oplus V^{*}=\Lambda\oplus\Lambda^{*}
,
which is also the module underlying
H_{+}(\Lambda)
,
and with these coordinates
u_{g}\in SL_{2}(\Lambda)
has matrix

\displaystyle u_{g}=\begin{pmatrix}1+2pg^{-1}&g^{-1}\\
p-2p^{2}g^{-1}&1-pg^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

It follows that there is a commutative diagram of
2
-sided primitive embeddings

\textstyle{(V,\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(V,\theta)\cup_{\,t_{g}}(V,-\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{g}}
\textstyle{(V,-\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}q+2pg\\
-2pq\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,pq)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1+2pg^{-1}\\
p-2p^{2}g^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{\left(\Lambda^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,-pq)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1+2pg\\
-p+2p^{2}g\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

and we denote the bottom
2
-sided sided primitive embedding by
(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g})
.
One checks that the top row is a
2
-sided primitive embedding using
Lemma
4.13
and the definition of
t_{g}
from Lemma
5.1
.

An interesting feature of the
2
-sided primitive embeddings
(j_{g},j_{g}^{\prime})
is that they are extended over
the inclusion of rings
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[\pi]=\Lambda
if and only if
g=e
is the identity.
Here we say that a
2
-sided primitive embedding
(j,j^{\prime})

is extended over
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\Lambda
if and only if there is an integral
2
-sided primitive embedding

(j_{\mathbb{Z}},j^{\prime}_{\mathbb{Z}})

such that
(j,j^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
(\Id_{\Lambda}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}j_{\mathbb{Z}},\Id_{\Lambda}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}j^{\prime})
.
For example,
(j_{e},j^{\prime}_{e})
is extended over
\iota
.

To see that
(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g})
is not extended over
\iota
when
g\neq e
,
we note that the augmentation homomorphism
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbb{Z},\Sigma_{g}n_{g}g\mapsto\Sigma_{g}n_{g}
,
is a right inverse to
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
It follows that a general
2
-sided primitive embedding
(j,j^{\prime})
over
\Lambda
is extended over
\iota

if and only if
(j,j^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
\iota(\varepsilon(j,j^{\prime}))
.
Now
\iota(\varepsilon(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g}))=(j_{e},j^{\prime}_{e})
.
In Theorem
6.12
below, we construct an isomorphism to a
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits
set, and in Proposition
5.6
we showed that the images of
(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g})
and
(j_{g^{\prime}},j^{\prime}_{g^{\prime}})
in this
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits
set coincide if and only if
g=g^{\prime}
. Therefore for
g\neq e
,
(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g})\neq(j_{e},j^{\prime}_{e})=\iota(\varepsilon(j_{g},j^{\prime}_{g}))
, and so
(j_{g},j_{g}^{\prime})
is not extended over
\iota
, as asserted.

6.2.
Primitive embeddings and boundary isomorphisms.

In this subsection we relate 2-sided primitive embeddings and boundary isomorphisms.

Recall that for
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms
v,v^{\prime}
and a nonsingular
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
m
,
in (
21
) we
defined the set

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\{[f]\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\,|\,v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\cong m\}.

Analogously to Definition
4.6
,
the sets
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}),\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})

are defined as quotients of
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
:
the groups acting preserve
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\subseteq\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.

Construction 6.9
.

We construct a map
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
.
Given a stable homotopy class of a stable isomorphism
f\colon\partial v\to\partial v^{\prime}
such that
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\cong m
, we choose an isometry
k\colon v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\to m
, use
\lambda^{\prime}
for the symmetrisation of the form
v^{\prime}
, and consider

(26)

\displaystyle\Pr\colon\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})

\displaystyle[f]

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(v\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{k,\cong}m\xleftarrow{k,\cong}v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}-v^{\prime}\right).

We verify that the definition of the map
\Pr
makes sense.
Set
j:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
j^{\prime}:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for concision.
It follows from Example
6.2
that
k\circ j(v)^{\perp}=k\circ j^{\prime}(-v^{\prime})
.
Also the isometry type of the algebraic union
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
only depends on the homotopy class of the stable isomorphism
f
, so for any choice of representative
f
, a choice of isometry
k
exists.
Thus
\Pr
can be defined, after making a choice of
k
. To see that
\Pr
is well-defined, we need to show that
\Pr
does not depend on the choice of the isometry
k\colon v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\to m
.
Assume that
h\colon v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\to m
is another isometry.
We must show that
(k\circ j,k\circ j^{\prime})
and
(h\circ j,h\circ j^{\prime})
define isomorphic 2-sided primitive embeddings.
This follows since
\varphi:=h\circ k^{-1}\colon m\to m
is an isometry that satisfies
\varphi\circ(k\circ j)=h\circ j
and
\varphi\circ(k\circ j^{\prime})=h\circ j^{\prime}
.
We conclude that
\Pr
is indeed well defined.

Since
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
and

\operatorname{pr}(v,v^{\prime})=\bigsqcup_{[m]}\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
are both disjoint unions as
[m]
ranges over the set of isomorphism classes
of forms obtain by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together, the union of the
\Pr
defines a map

\Pr\colon\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Prim}(v,-v^{\prime}).

We now construct an inverse for
\Pr
.

Construction 6.10
.

Let
v\xhookrightarrow{i}m\xhookleftarrow{i^{\prime}}-v^{\prime}
be a 2-sided primitive embedding.
According to Proposition
4.14
, the primitive embedding
i
gives rise to a stable isomorphism
[f_{i}]\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial(-v^{\perp}))
.
Since
(i,i^{\prime})
is a 2-sided primitive embedding, we know that
-v^{\perp}=i^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
; recall Example
6.2
.
We deduce the existence of the map
{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\colon-v^{\perp}=i^{\prime}(v^{\prime})\to v^{\prime}
.
Consider the assignment

\displaystyle\delta\colon\operatorname{Prim}_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\to\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})

\displaystyle[v\xhookrightarrow{i}m\xhookleftarrow{i^{\prime}}-v^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\mapsto[(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}].

We check that
\delta
is well defined.
Namely, we assume that
(j,j^{\prime})
is a 2-sided primitive embedding that is isomorphic to
(i,i^{\prime})
, and wish to show that
\delta(i,i^{\prime})=\delta(j,j^{\prime})
as boundary isomorphisms.
Since
(i,i^{\prime})
and
(j,j^{\prime})
are isomorphic, there is an isometry
g\colon m\to m
such that
g\circ j=i
and
g\circ j^{\prime}=i^{\prime}
.
Using the naturality statement of Proposition
4.14
,
we deduce that
f_{i}=(\partial g\oplus 1)\circ f_{j}
.
We can combine these facts to conclude the proof of the fact that
\delta
is well defined:

\displaystyle\delta(j,j^{\prime})

\displaystyle=[(\partial{j^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{j}]=[(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ(\partial g\oplus 1)\circ f_{j}]=[(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}]=\delta(i,i^{\prime}).

Taking the union of the maps
\delta
over all isomorphism classes of forms
m
obtained
by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together we obtain the map

\delta\colon\operatorname{Prim}(v,-v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}).

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
v
,
v^{\prime}
and
m
be
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms where the latter is nonsingular.
The map
\Pr
from Construction
6.9
descends to define maps

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}),

and the map
\delta
from Construction
6.10
descends to define maps

\displaystyle\delta\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\delta\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\delta\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime}).

Moreover, the same statements hold with the subscript
m
removed.

Proof.

Let
f\colon\partial v\cong_{s}\partial v^{\prime}
be a stable isomorphism, and let
h\colon v\to v
and
k\colon v^{\prime}\to v^{\prime}
be isometries.
To prove that the maps
\Pr
are well-defined on the quotient sets,
we must show that
\Pr(\partial k\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1})=\Pr(f)
.
We will use
[
CS11
, Lemma 4.6 iv)]
, which states that
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}h&0\\
0&k^{-*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\to v\cup_{\partial k\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1}}-v^{\prime}
is an isometry.
Let
a,b,s
be the components of the stable isomorphism
f=(\alpha,\beta,\nu)
.
Recall that for an isometry
h\penalty\ \colon\penalty\ v\penalty\ \to\penalty\ v
, we have
\partial h:=(h,h,0)
,
and recall from Example
4.10
that
\partial k\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1}
has
a
-component
k\circ a\circ h^{-1}
and
b
-component
h\circ b\circ k^{-1}
.
Next, we use
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
to denote the symmetrized forms on
v,v^{\prime}
and consider the following diagram in which the horizontal maps are the primitive embeddings described in Lemma
4.13
:

(27)

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}h&0\\
0&k^{-*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{k}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{v\cup_{\partial k\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1}}-v^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces-v^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}hbk^{-1}\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

The left hand square of (
27
) clearly commutes. Note that (
27
) also shows that
\Pr((h,k)\cdot f)=(h,k)\cdot\Pr(f)
so that
\Pr
intertwines the
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v)\times\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v^{\prime})
actions.
The right hand square of (
27
) commutes because
k
is an isometry.
As a consequence, we deduce that the 2-sided primitive embeddings
\Pr(\partial k\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1})
and
\Pr(f)
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
.
This shows that
\Pr
descends to
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
.
Taking
k=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
(resp.
h=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
) in this proof gives the analogous statement for
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
(resp.
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
).

We show that
\delta
descends to
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
.
Let
v\xhookrightarrow{j}m\xhookleftarrow{j^{\prime}}-v^{\prime}
be a 2-sided primitive embedding, and let
h\colon v\to v
and
k\colon v^{\prime}\to v^{\prime}
be isometries.
To show that
\delta
descends to the orbits sets, we prove
\delta(jh^{-1},j^{\prime}k^{-1})=\partial k\circ\delta(j,j^{\prime})\circ\partial h^{-1}
.
Recall that be definition
\delta(j,j^{\prime})=(\partial{j^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{j}
, where
f_{j}
denotes the stable isomorphism from Proposition
4.14
.
Use
a,b,s
to denote the components of the stable isomorphism
\delta(j,j^{\prime}):=(\partial{j^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{j}
.
By Example
4.10
,
\partial k\circ\delta(j,j^{\prime})\circ\partial h^{-1}
has components
kah^{-1},hbk^{-1}
and
ksk^{*}
.
By Remark
4.11
, it suffices to show that
\delta(jh^{-1},j^{\prime}k^{-1})
has the same components.
By definition, we have
\delta(jh^{-1},j^{\prime}k^{-1})=(\partial k\partial{j^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{jh^{-1}}
.
It therefore suffices to show that
(\partial{j^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)f_{jh^{-1}}
has components
ah^{-1},hb
and
s
.
Since
h
is an isometry, the orthogonal complement
jh^{-1}(v)^{\perp}
of
jh^{-1}(v)
in
m
coincides with the orthogonal complement
j(v)^{\perp}
of
v
in
m
.
In particular, the splitting
\sigma
of
{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi
can also be used in the computation of
f_{jh^{-1}}
. Here recall that
\phi
denotes the symmetrisation of the form on
v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, since
\widetilde{\sigma}:=j^{-1}(1-\sigma{j^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)
splits
j
as in (
17
), we deduce that
