h\widetilde{\sigma}
splits
jh^{-1}
.
It now follows from Proposition
4.15

that the components of
f_{jh^{-1}}
are indeed
ah^{-1},hb
and
s
.
This establishes that
\delta
descends to
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
as well as to
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
and

\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
(by respectively taking
k=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
and
h=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
),
which concludes the proof for a fixed form
m
.

The final statement follows since
m
was an arbitrary form obtained by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together.
∎

We can now prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
v,v^{\prime}
and
m
be
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms where the latter is nonsingular.
The map
\Pr
from Construction
6.9
descends to bijections

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\Pr\colon

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\to\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime}).

Its inverse is the map
\delta
induced by Construction
6.10
.
Moreover, the same statements hold with the subscript
m
removed.

Proof.

Given a
2
-sided primitive embedding
v\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\hookrightarrow}}m\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i^{\prime}}}{{\hookleftarrow}}v^{\prime}
, we first show that

\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})\cong(i,i^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
.
Recall that
\delta(i,i^{\prime})=[(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}]
, where
{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\colon-v^{\perp}=i^{\prime}(v^{\prime})\to v^{\prime}
.
Using
b:=b_{f_{i}}
to denote the
b
component of the stable isomorphism
f_{i}
, Example
4.10
shows that the
b
component of
(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}
is
bi^{\prime}
.
The 2-sided primitive embedding
\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})
is therefore

\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})=\left(v\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}v\cup_{(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}}-v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{k,\cong}m\xleftarrow{k,\cong}v\cup_{(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}}-v^{\prime}\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}bi^{\prime}\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}-v^{\prime}\right).

Proposition
4.14
provides an isometry
r_{i}\colon m\to v\cup_{f_{i}}v^{\perp}
.
We describe an explicit formula for this isometry following
[
CS11
, proof of Proposition 4.8]
:
r_{i}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i&-\sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, where
\sigma\colon{v^{\perp}}^{*}\to m
is a splitting of
{i^{\perp}}^{*}\phi\colon m\to{v^{\perp}}^{*}
, meaning that
{i^{\perp}}^{*}\phi\sigma=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits_{{v^{\perp}}^{*}}
; here
\phi=\psi+\varepsilon\psi^{*}
is the
\varepsilon
-symmetrization of the quadratic form
m=(M,\psi)
.
One can check that
r_{i}
is indeed an isometry by using Proposition
4.15
.
Consider the following diagram:

(28)

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{m}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{-i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{(-1)\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{v\cup_{f_{i}}v^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&{i^{\prime}}^{*}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}i&-\sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{v^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
\lambda^{\perp}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{v\cup_{(\partial{i^{\prime}}^{-1}\oplus 1)\circ f_{i}}-v^{\prime}}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}bi^{\prime}\\
-\lambda^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}

Observe that each horizontal line of the diagram in (
28
) consists of 2-sided primitive embeddings, and the third line is
\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})
.
The equation
\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})\cong(i,i^{\prime})
will therefore follow once we show that the diagram in (
28
) commutes.
The top and bottom left squares of (
28
) clearly commute.
The bottom right square of (
28
) commutes since
{i^{\prime}}^{*}\lambda^{\perp}i^{\prime}=-\lambda^{\prime}
.
In order to prove that the top right square of (
28
) commutes, we show that
-(ib-\sigma\lambda^{\perp})i^{\prime}=i^{\prime}.

Recall from Proposition
4.15
the
b
component of the stable isomorphism
f_{i}\colon\partial v\cong_{s}\partial(-v^{\perp})
is
b=-i^{-1}(1-\sigma{i^{\perp}}^{*}\phi)i^{\perp}.

By using the equation
{i^{\perp}}^{*}\phi i^{\perp}=\lambda^{\perp}
, note that
ib=\sigma\lambda^{\perp}-i^{\perp}
.
Using this formula and the fact that
i^{\perp}\colon v^{\perp}\hookrightarrow m
is the canonical inclusion (so that
v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}v^{\perp}\xhookrightarrow{i^{\perp}}m
is also denoted by
i^{\prime}
), we obtain the desired result:

-(ib-\sigma\lambda^{\perp})i^{\prime}=-(\sigma\lambda^{\perp}-i^{\perp}-\sigma\lambda^{\perp})i^{\prime}=i^{\perp}i^{\prime}=i^{\prime}.

This shows that the diagram in (
28
) commutes,
which proves that
\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})\cong(i,i^{\prime})
in the set
\mathop{\rm rPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
, and thus in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
.
To see that this isomorphism also holds in
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
, fix two
2
-sided primitive embeddings
(j,j^{\prime}),(k,k^{\prime})

and consider the two following diagrams:

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{(-1)\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{m}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k^{\prime}}

\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{(-1)\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{m}
\textstyle{-v^{\prime}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k^{\prime}}

The commutativity of one of the diagram is equivalent to the commutativity of the other.
Consequently (
28
) also proves that
\Pr\circ\delta(i,i^{\prime})\cong(i,i^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,-v^{\prime})
.

For
f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
, Example
4.16
proves that
\delta\circ\Pr(f)
and
f
agree in
the sets
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits_{m},\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}
and
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}
(we observed that the components of
\delta(\Pr(f))
coincide with the components of both
(\partial(-1)\oplus 1)\circ f
and
f\circ(\partial(-1)\oplus 1)^{-1}
and applied Remark
4.11
), so this concludes the proof of Theorem
6.12
for a fixed form
m
.

The final statement follows since
m
was an arbitrary form obtained by gluing
v
and
v^{\prime}
together.
∎

Example 6.13
.

As a concrete example of the calculations that appear in the proof of Theorem
6.12
, we describe a
2
-sided primitive embedding representing
\Pr(\delta([(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})])
.
Here, recall from Example
6.4
that for
q=y_{1}y_{2}
, we set

(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}}):=\left((\mathbb{Z},q)\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},-q)\right).

We compute the
a,b,s
components of
\delta(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}}):=f_{i_{y_{1},y_{2}}}
(the boundary automorphism
f_{i_{y_{1},y_{2}}}
was introduced in
(
4.3
)
), as this will be sufficient to calculate
\Pr(\delta([(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})])
.

We set
\phi:=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and
\psi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and note that
i_{y_{1},y_{2}}^{\perp}=j_{y_{1},y_{2}}
.
We also let
\sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
be a splitting of
i_{y_{1},y_{2}}^{\perp*}\phi=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{2}&-y_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
(so that
y_{2}z_{1}-y_{1}z_{2}=1
), and let
\widetilde{\sigma}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-z_{2}&z_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
be the associated splitting of
i_{y_{1},y_{2}}
.
By Example
4.16
, we deduce that the
a,b,s
-components
f_{i_{y_{1},y_{2}}}
are

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\phi^{*}i_{y_{1},y_{2}}=-\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&z_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=-(y_{1}z_{2}+y_{2}z_{1}),

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=-\widetilde{\sigma}j_{y_{1},y_{2}}=-\begin{pmatrix}-z_{2}&z_{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=-(y_{1}z_{2}+y_{2}z_{1}),

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=-\sigma^{*}\psi\sigma=-\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&z_{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}z_{1}\\
z_{2}\end{pmatrix}=-z_{1}z_{2}.

By definition of
\Pr
, we therefore deduce that
\Pr(\delta([(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})])
is represented by

\left((\mathbb{Z},q)\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}q&0\\
a&-s\end{pmatrix}}\right)\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-2q\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},-q)\right).

One can directly verify using the values of
a
,
b
,
s
and
q
above, together with
y_{2}z_{1}-y_{1}z_{2}=1
, that this is a primitive embedding.
As a reality check, we verify that that this
2
-sided primitive embeddings agrees with
(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})
in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits((\mathbb{Z},q),(\mathbb{Z},-q))
:

\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{\left({\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\right)}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},-q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{\left({\mathbb{Z}^{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}q&0\\
a&-s\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}&-z_{1}\\
y_{2}&-z_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{Z},-q).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-2q\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{(-1)}

Combining
b=-(y_{1}z_{2}+y_{2}z_{1})
,
q=y_{1}y_{2}
, and
y_{2}z_{1}-y_{1}z_{2}=1
one can check that the diagram commutes.

7.
Realising boundary isomorphisms and primitive embeddings

Now we start to apply the algebra of Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
.
We return to the setting of Section
3
. That is, we fix a fibration

\xi\colon B\to BO
and let

\overline{\nu}\colon M^{2q}\to B
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing over
(B,\xi)
.
Throughout this section, we work with closed, connected, oriented, smooth manifolds
M,M^{\prime}
or
M_{i}
, for
i=0,1,2
, and we assume that
q>3
is even, or that
q=2
, in which case we must switch to the topological category and assume that
\pi_{1}(B)
is a good group.
We set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
and consider the surgery kernel

K_{q}(M;\Lambda):=\ker\big(\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{q}(B;\Lambda)\big),

where we use
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\pi_{1}(B)
to identify fundamental groups.
Since
q
is even and
M
is oriented, if the
\Lambda
-valued intersection form of
M
,

\lambda_{M}\colon H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\times H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\to\Lambda

admits a quadratic refinement, then it is unique. In this case we denote it by
\theta_{M}
.

In Section
7.1
, under simplifying algebraic assumptions
we associate a 2-sided primitive embedding

K_{q}(M;\Lambda)\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\overline{\nu}}}H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\xhookleftarrow{j_{\overline{\nu}}}H^{q}(B;\Lambda)

to
(M,\overline{\nu})
(Definition
7.5
).
By Construction
6.10
, this gives rise to a boundary isomorphism

\delta_{\overline{\nu}}:=\delta(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{M}\iota_{\overline{\nu}}),\partial(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),-j_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{M}j_{\overline{\nu}})).

In Section
7.2
, we prove a realisation result for this boundary automorphism.
Roughly, given a boundary automorphism

f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{M}\iota_{\overline{\nu}}))
, Theorem
7.7
constructs another
normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
which is normally
(B,\xi)
-bordant (and thus stably diffeomorphic) to
(M,\overline{\nu})
and whose associated boundary isomorphism
\delta^{\prime}
agrees with
\delta f
in
\mathop{\rm rIso}\nolimits
.

Then for the quadratic form
v(\overline{\nu})=(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\iota^{*}_{\overline{\nu}}\theta_{M}\iota_{\overline{\nu}})

we relate this result to the realisation of elements of

\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu})]_{0})
from Section
3
.
Given an appropriate quasi-formation
x
representing
[x]\in\ell_{2q+1}([v(\overline{\nu}_{0})]_{0})
,
Theorem
3.10
constructed a
(B,\xi)
-bordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
with

\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=[x]
.
In Corollary
7.14
, under simplifying algebraic assumptions, we show that the two realisation theorems are related via the exact sequence (
1
) from
[
CS11
]
, as one would hope.

7.1.
The boundary isomorphism of an even split-free normal smoothing

Under simplifying algebraic assumptions,
we associate a 2-sided primitive embedding and a boundary isomorphism to a

(q{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M^{2q}\to B
.
In Section
9
, these objects will be used to distinguish
certain stably diffeomorphic
4k
-manifolds.

Consider a map
\overline{\nu}\colon M^{2q}\to B
,
with
B
homotopy equivalent to a
CW
-complex with finite
q
-skeleton
and
M
closed, connected, and oriented.
The inclusion
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)\hookrightarrow H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
and the composition of
\overline{\nu}^{*}\colon H^{q}(B;\Lambda)\to H^{q}(M;\Lambda)
with the Poincaré duality isomorphism
\operatorname{PD}\colon H^{q}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
produce the following
\Lambda
-homomorphisms:

(29)

\displaystyle\iota_{\overline{\nu}}\colon K_{q}(M;\Lambda)

\displaystyle\rightarrow H_{q}(M;\Lambda),

\displaystyle j_{\overline{\nu}}:=PD\circ\overline{\nu}^{*}\colon H^{q}(B;\Lambda)

\displaystyle\rightarrow H_{q}(M;\Lambda).

Recall that when
q
is even and
M
is oriented, if the symmetric form
(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\lambda_{M})

admits a quadratic refinement
\theta_{M}
, then the refinement is uniquely determined by
\lambda_{M}
.
The quadratic form
\theta_{M}
could be singular, because the evaluation homomorphism

\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}\colon H^{q}(M;\Lambda)\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}

need not be an isomorphism.
Next, use
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}
and
j_{\overline{\nu}}
to pull the quadratic form
\theta_{M}
back from
H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
to
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)
and
H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
respectively.
This leads to the two following quadratic forms:

\displaystyle(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})

\displaystyle:=(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{M}\iota_{\overline{\nu}}),

\displaystyle(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),\theta_{M})

\displaystyle:=(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),j_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{M}j_{\overline{\nu}}).

In order for
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
to define a 2-sided primitive embedding,
we impose some further restrictions on the map
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
.

Definition 7.1
.

Fix
B
, a space homotopy equivalent to a
CW
-complex with finite
q
-skeleton.
A map
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is called
q
-surjective
if it induces a
surjection
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
.
Let
M
be a closed, connected, and oriented
2q
-dimensional manifold with
q\geq 2
even.

(i)

We say that
M
is
even
if the
\Lambda
-valued intersection form on
H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
admits a quadratic refinement
\theta_{M}
, which since
M
is oriented and
q
is even is unique.

(ii)

We say that
(M,\overline{\nu})
is
split-free
if the evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}_{\!B}\colon H^{q}(B;\Lambda)\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{q}(B;\Lambda),\Lambda)}

is an isomorphism, and if the
\Lambda
-modules
K_{q}(M;\Lambda):=\ker(\overline{\nu}_{*})
and
H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
are f.g. free. If in addition
M
is even, we shall say that
(M,\overline{\nu})
is
an
even, split-free map.

We will describe the two main examples of
q
-surjective maps that we wish to keep in mind. For this we need a quick lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

For
q\geq 1
, if
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is
q
-connected, then it is
q
-surjective.

Proof.

Since
\overline{\nu}
induces an isomorphism on fundamental groups,
the pullback
\overline{\nu}^{*}(\widetilde{B})\to M
of the universal covering of
B
is homeomorphic to the universal cover
\widetilde{M}
, so

\overline{\nu}^{*}(\widetilde{B})
is simply-connected.
Apply the relative Hurewicz theorem to
(\widetilde{B},\overline{\nu}^{*}(\widetilde{B}))
to see that
\overline{\nu}
induces a surjection on
H_{q}(-;\Lambda)
as asserted.
∎

Example 7.3
.

Using Lemma
7.2
we note the two main examples of
q
-surjective maps.

(i)

Assume that
B=P_{q-1}(M)
is the
(q{-}1)
-st Postnikov stage of
M
, a model for which is obtained from
M
by adding cells of dimension of dimension
(q+1)
and higher in order to kill
\pi_{i}(M)
for
i\geq q

[
Hat02
, Chapter 4.3]
.
Since the inclusion map
\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)
is
q
-connected, it is
q
-surjective.

(ii)

Given a fibration,
\xi\colon B\to BO
, a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
is
q
-connected and therefore
q
-surjective.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
q\geq 2
be even.
If
\overline{\nu}\colon M^{2q}\to B
is an even, split-free
q
-surjective map and
(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})
is a nonsingular quadratic form, then
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
,

(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})\xhookrightarrow{\iota_{\overline{\nu}}}(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})\xhookleftarrow{j_{\overline{\nu}}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),\theta_{M}),

is a 2-sided primitive embedding.

Proof.

First,
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}
is a split injection, because
H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
free implies that the short exact sequence

0\to K_{q}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\iota_{\overline{\nu}}}H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\overline{\nu}_{*}}H_{q}(B;\Lambda)\to 0

splits. Next we prove that
j_{\overline{\nu}}=\operatorname{PD}\circ\overline{\nu}^{*}
is a split injection.
Since
\operatorname{PD}
is an isomorphism, it suffices to show that
\overline{\nu}^{*}
is a split injection.
The map
\overline{\nu}\penalty\
is
q
-surjective, so the induced map
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon H_{q}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
is onto.
It follows that its dual
\overline{\nu}^{*}_{d}
is injective.
Now consider the following commutative diagram:

(30)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{q}(B;\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.29477pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 125.04457pt\raise-10.84929pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.39738pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.39738pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{q}(B;\Lambda),\Lambda)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.37172pt\raise-14.97093pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22765pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}^{*}_{d}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.54457pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 119.54457pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 144.9397pt\raise-14.39316pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.03877pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 173.79437pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 173.79437pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 208.79437pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 208.79437pt\raise-21.69858pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We argue that the bottom line is split exact.
We already established that
\overline{\nu}_{d}^{*}
is injective. Since
H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
is free, there is no Ext term and so it follows that
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}
is surjective.
Next, since
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)
is finitely generated and free, so is
\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}
, and the exact sequence splits as claimed.
We can now show that
\overline{\nu}^{*}
is a split injection.
Since
(M,\overline{\nu})
is even and split-free, the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{\!B}
is an isomorphism, while
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}
is an isomorphism because the quadratic form
(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})
is nonsingular.
Since
\overline{\nu}^{*}_{d}
is a split injection, the commutativity of the diagram displayed in (
30
) together with these facts implies that
\overline{\nu}^{*}
must also be a split injection, as claimed.

It remains to show that
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}=j_{\overline{\nu}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda))
.
We first show that
j_{\overline{\nu}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda))
is contained in
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}
.
Use
\langle-,-\rangle_{B}
to denote the Kronecker pairing that evaluates a cohomology class on a homology class, so that
\langle\varphi,x\rangle_{B}=\operatorname{ev}_{\!B}(\varphi)(x)
.
For
x\in H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
and for
\varphi\in H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
, the definition of the
\Lambda
-valued intersection pairing
\lambda_{M}=\theta_{M}+\varepsilon\theta_{M}^{*}
(using Poincaré duality and the Kronecker evaluation map) gives

(31)

\lambda_{M}(j_{\overline{\nu}}(\varphi),x)=\langle\overline{\nu}^{*}\varphi,x\rangle_{B}=\langle\varphi,\overline{\nu}_{*}(x)\rangle_{B}.

The inclusion
j_{\overline{\nu}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda))\subseteq K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}

now follows readily: if
x\in K_{q}(M;\Lambda)=\ker(\overline{\nu}_{*})
, then

\lambda_{M}(j_{\overline{\nu}}(\varphi),x)=\langle\varphi,\overline{\nu}_{*}(x)\rangle_{B}=\langle\varphi,0\rangle_{B}=0,

so
j_{\overline{\nu}}(\varphi)\in K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}
as desired.
To prove the reverse inclusion,
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}\subseteq j_{\overline{\nu}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda))
, assume that
y\in H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
satisfies
\lambda_{M}(y,\iota_{\overline{\nu}}(x))=0
for all
x\in K_{q}(M;\Lambda)
.
Equivalently, we have
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}\operatorname{PD}_{M}^{-1}(y)\in\ker(\iota_{\overline{\nu}}^{*})=\im(\overline{\nu}_{d}^{*})
, by the exactness of the sequence in (
30
).
In particular,
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}\operatorname{PD}_{M}^{-1}(y)=\overline{\nu}_{d}^{*}(z)
for some
z\in\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{q}(B;\Lambda),\Lambda)}
.
Since
\operatorname{ev}_{\!B}
is an isomorphism,
z=\operatorname{ev}_{\!B}(\varphi)
for some
\varphi\in H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
.
By commutativity of (
30
), we obtain
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}\operatorname{PD}_{M}^{-1}(y)=\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}(\overline{\nu}^{*}(\varphi))
.
Since
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}
is an isomorphism, we deduce that
y=j_{\overline{\nu}}(\varphi)
.
We thus proved that
K_{q}(M;\Lambda)^{\perp}\subseteq j_{\overline{\nu}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda))
, and this concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Using Lemma
7.4
, we associate a 2-sided primitive embedding to an even, split-free
q
-surjective map
\overline{\nu}
.
We can also associate a boundary isomorphism to
\overline{\nu}\penalty\
by recalling from Construction
6.10
that a 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
determines a boundary isomorphism
\delta(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
(M^{2q},\overline{\nu})
be an even, split-free
q
-surjective map and assume that the quadratic form
(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})
is nonsingular.
The
2-sided primitive embedding associated with
\overline{\nu}
is the
2
-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
from (
29
),
the
boundary isomorphism associated to
\overline{\nu}\penalty\
is

\delta_{\overline{\nu}}:=\delta(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M}),\partial(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),-\theta_{M})).

Some remarks on this definition are in order.

Remark 7.6
.

Let
\overline{\nu}\colon M^{2q}\to B
be an even, split-free
q
-surjective map.

(i)

The invariant
\delta_{\overline{\nu}}
determines an element in the one-sided boundary automorphism set
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})}(\partial(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M}),\partial(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),-\theta_{M}))
.
This follows from Theorem
6.12
, in particular (
28
).
As a consequence, there is an isometry

(H_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})\cong(K_{q}(M;\Lambda),\theta_{M})\cup_{\delta_{\overline{\nu}}}(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),\theta_{M}).

(ii)

Recall from Remarks
4.8
and
6.6
that the sets
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{m}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})
and
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
only depend on
v^{\prime}
and on the isometry type of
m
and
v
.
As a consequence, we say that the 2-sided primitive embeddings associated with the
q
-surjective maps
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{1},\overline{\nu}_{1})
into
B

agree in
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits
if they fit into the following diagram:

\textstyle{(K_{q}(M_{0};\Lambda),\theta_{M_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(H_{q}(M_{0};\Lambda),\theta_{M_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),\theta_{M_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{(K_{q}(M_{1};\Lambda),\theta_{M_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\overline{\nu}_{1}}}
\textstyle{(H_{q}(M_{1};\Lambda),\theta_{M_{1}})}
\textstyle{(H^{q}(B;\Lambda),\theta_{M_{0}}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\overline{\nu}_{1}}}

If the right vertical arrow
in this diagram is merely an isomorphism, then we say that these two 2-sided primitive embeddings
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits
.
We also use the corresponding terminology for the boundary isomorphism associated to
\overline{\nu}
, replacing
\mathop{\rm Prim}\nolimits
by
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits
.

(iii)

If
H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
is free, then the data of the 2-sided primitive embedding associated with
\overline{\nu}
is determined by the map
\overline{\nu}^{*}\colon H^{q}(B;\Lambda)\to H^{q}(M;\Lambda)
: indeed, using that both
H_{q}(M;\Lambda)
and
H_{q}(B;\Lambda)
are free, the map
\overline{\nu}^{*}
determines
\overline{\nu}_{*}
.

7.2.
The realisation of boundary automorphisms

The goal of this section is to prove a realisation result for the boundary isomorphisms associated
to bordant normal smoothings.
Throughout this subsection, we fix a fibration
\xi\colon B\to BO
as above, and assume that all homology and cohomology groups have
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]
coefficients.

Given split quadratic formations
x,y
and
f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
, we write
[f]_{\ell}
for the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
and
[f]_{b}
for its class in
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(x,y)
.
Recall from Remark
4.7
that given quadratic forms
v
and
b
, the left action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)

on
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
does not descend to an action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)
on
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
.
However, if
g\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)
and
\delta\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
, it is possible to consider the class
[\delta\circ g]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
.
Keeping this in mind, the main result of this section is the following.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
q\geq 4
be even and let
\overline{\nu}_{0}\colon M_{0}^{2q}\to B
be an even, split-free normal

(q{-}1)
-smoothing.
Set
(V,\theta):=(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
, and
r:=\operatorname{rk}(V)
.
For every
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{r})}(\partial(V,\theta))
there is an even, split-free normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{2}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{2})
such that

(i)

(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
are stably diffeomorphic;

(ii)

there is an isometry
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})\cong(V,\theta)
, and in particular

\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}}),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))\cong\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}));

(iii)

[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}]_{\ell}
and
[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}f]_{\ell}
belong to
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
;

(iv)

[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}]_{\ell}
and
[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}f]_{\ell}
agree in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
.

The same holds in the topological category for
q=2
, assuming that
\pi_{1}(B)
is a good group.

Remark 7.8
.

The geometric construction in this proof is similar to that in the proof of Theorem
3.10
. We perform a set of trivial surgeries, and then a second set of surgeries on a “diagonal Lagrangian”. The difference is that Theorem
7.7
tracks the algebra differently. In order to obtain a bordism realising the desired change on boundary isomorphisms/primitive embeddings, it is much easier to return to the geometric construction than to track the change through the map
\delta
from the sequence (
1
). The reason is that the definition of
\delta
involves two different types of stabilisation which are tricky to control in this setting. We will discuss this further after Corollary
7.12
.

Proof.

First we will construct a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
W
based on
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
.
Since
\overline{\nu}_{0}
is split-free, the
\Lambda
-module
K_{q}(M_{0})\cong V
is finitely generated and free.
Using
r
to denote the rank of
V
, let
W_{0}
be the cobordism obtained from
M_{0}\times[0,1]
by adding trivial
q
-handles along
M_{0}\times\{1\}
with trivial framing, one handle for each element of a basis for
V
.
In other words, we consider the boundary connected sum:

\displaystyle W_{0}

\displaystyle:=(M_{0}\times[0,1])\natural(\natural_{i=1}^{r}S^{q}\times D^{q+1}),\text{ with}

\displaystyle M_{1}

\displaystyle:=M_{0}\mathbin{\#}(\mathbin{\#}_{i=1}^{r}S^{q}\times S^{q}).

Let
\overline{\nu}_{1}\colon M_{1}\to B
be the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing on
M_{1}
obtained from
\overline{\nu}_{0}
by imposing the trivial
(B,\xi)
-structure on the
S^{q}\times S^{q}
connected summands.
In order to obtain the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
W
, we will perform
r
additional
q
-surgeries on
M_{1}
.
To do so, we first prescribe a rank
r
sublagrangian of
K_{q}(M_{1})
; we then argue why it is possible to perform surgery along generators of this subspace.
Consider the following decomposition

(32)

(K_{q}(M_{1}),\theta_{M_{1}})\cong H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})\cong\big((V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\big)\oplus(V,\theta),

the primitive embedding
j_{f}^{\prime}\colon(V,-\theta)\hookrightarrow(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)
described in Lemma
4.13
, and the sublagrangian

(33)

D:=\{(j_{f}^{\prime}(x),-x)\mid x\in V\}\subseteq K_{q}(M_{1}).

Note that
(V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\cong H_{\varepsilon}(V)
because
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{r})}(\partial(V,\theta))
.
One way to see that
D
is a summand is to use item (
c
) of Lemma
7.9
below.

We assert that we can represent a basis of
D
by
r
framed embedded spheres, and perform
q
-surgeries on
M_{1}
along these spheres.
The relative Hurewicz theorem implies that the Hurewicz map
K\pi_{q}(M_{0})\to K_{q}(M_{0})
is surjective.
Consequently, every element of
K_{q}(M_{0})
comes from an element
x
in
K\pi_{q}(M_{0})
.
Since
D
is a sublagrangian of
K_{q}(M_{0})
, we deduce that
\lambda_{M_{0}}(x,x)=0
, where
\lambda_{M_{0}}
denotes the
\Lambda
-valued intersection form on
M_{0}
.
As
q
is even and
M_{0}
is orientable, this implies that the Wall form vanishes on
x
.
The
r
generators of
D
can therefore be represented by framed embedded
q
-spheres on which we can perform surgery.
For the
q=2
case, using the same proof as in Remark
3.11
, the generators of
D
can be represented by framed, topologically embedded, locally flat 2-spheres, on which we can perform surgery.

Taking the trace and effect of these surgeries, we obtain

\displaystyle W:=\bigl(M_{1}\times[0,1]\bigr)\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}h_{i}^{r+1}\text{ and }M_{2}:=\partial_{+}W.

Since we performed surgeries along elements of
K\pi_{q}(M_{1})
, the
(B,\xi)
-structure on
M_{1}
extends to
(B,\xi)
-structures
\overline{\nu}_{2}
on
M_{2}
and
\overline{\nu}
on
W
;
recall the end of Section
3.1
.
As
M_{0}
and
M_{2}
are
(B,\xi)
-bordant and have the same Euler characteristic, they are stably diffeomorphic
[
Kre99
, Theorem C]
, establishing (
i
) of the theorem.

Next we prove properties (
ii
) and (
iii
) of
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
, namely that
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})\cong(V,\theta)
, and that both

\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}}),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))\text{ and }\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))

define classes in

\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}})).

Note that if we show
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})\cong(V,\theta)
, it follows straight away that
\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}
determines a class in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
, so half of (
ii
) follows immediately from (
iii
).
From now on, we use the shorthand
\delta_{i}:=\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{i}}
for the boundary isomorphism associated to
(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i})
.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
(V,\theta),(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}),(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})
be
\varepsilon
-quadratic forms, and let
f\colon\partial(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cong_{s}\penalty\ \partial(V,\theta)
and
f^{\prime}\colon\partial(V,\theta)\cong_{s}\partial(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
be stable isomorphisms.
There is an isometry

(34)

\varphi\colon\big((V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\big)\oplus\big((V,\theta)\cup_{f^{\prime}}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cup_{f^{\prime}\circ f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})

that satisfies the following properties:

(a)

\varphi
takes the isometric copy of
(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})
to itself:

\varphi\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
j_{f^{\prime}}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
j_{f^{\prime}f}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right);

(b)

\varphi
restricts to an isometry
\varphi_{|}
on the first three summands; namely the following diagram of isometries and isometric injections commutes:

\textstyle{(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\oplus(V,\theta)\cup_{f^{\prime}}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cup_{f^{\prime}f}(V^{\prime},-\theta^{\prime})}
\textstyle{(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\oplus(V,\theta)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Id\oplus j_{f^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi|}
\textstyle{H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime});\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Id\oplus j_{f}}

(c)

\varphi
takes the
diagonal lagrangian
of
(V,-\theta)\oplus(V,\theta)
to the lagrangian
V\oplus 0
of
H_{\varepsilon}(V)
:

\varphi\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}j_{f}^{\prime}\\
-j_{f^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Proof.

We use the notation from Lemma
4.9
for the components of the stable isomorphisms
f
and
f^{\prime}
.
Namely, we write
a,b,s,a_{1},b_{1},a_{3}
(respectively
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},s^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime},a_{3}^{\prime}
) for the components of
f
(respectively
f^{\prime}
).
A fairly long but straightforward computation shows that

\varphi=\begin{pmatrix}a&-\varepsilon s&-s^{*}\lambda&-s^{*}{a^{\prime}}^{*}\\
0&1&-\lambda&-{a^{\prime}}^{*}\\
1&0&b&-b_{1}{a_{1}^{\prime}}^{*}\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\colon V^{\prime\prime}\oplus V^{*}\oplus V\oplus{V^{\prime}}^{*}\xrightarrow{\cong}V\oplus V^{*}\oplus V^{\prime\prime}\oplus{V^{\prime}}^{*}

provides the desired isometry.
The three properties of
\varphi
are also established by a direct computation.
∎

We continue with the proof of Theorem
7.7
.
In order to identify
(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
and
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
, we describe the corresponding quadratic forms, but for the intermediate manifold
M_{1}
.
Using (
32
) and Lemma
7.9
(
b
) (with
(V^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime})=(V,\theta),(V^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=(H^{q}(B);-\theta_{M_{0}})
and
f^{\prime}=\delta_{0}
), we obtain

\displaystyle(K_{q}(M_{1}),\theta_{M_{1}})

\displaystyle=H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})=\big((V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\big)\oplus(V,\theta)

(35)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varphi_{|},\cong}H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(V,\theta).

Next, we obtain a similar description for
(H_{q}(M_{1}),\theta_{M_{1}})
.
First, identifying
(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
with
(V,\theta)
, recall from Remark
7.6
that

(H_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})=(V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}}).

Using the definition of
M_{1}
, this decomposition, and the isomorphism from Lemma
7.9
, we deduce that

\displaystyle(H_{q}(M_{1}),\theta_{M_{1}})=

\displaystyle H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(H_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})=\big((V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\big)\oplus\left((V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}})\right)

(36)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varphi,\cong}

\displaystyle H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus(V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}f}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}}).

Items (

) and (
c
) of Lemma
7.9
imply that the sublagrangian
D
is mapped by
\varphi
to the lagrangian
V\oplus 0
of
H_{\varepsilon}(V)
.
By definition of
M_{2}
and looking at (
7.2
) and (
7.2
), the second set of surgeries kills the

-hyperbolic summands in
(H_{q}(M_{1}),\theta_{M_{1}})
and

and thus

(37)

\displaystyle(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})

\displaystyle=(V,\theta);

\displaystyle(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})

\displaystyle=(V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}\circ f}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}}).

This shows (
ii
) and that
\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}
determines a class in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
.
It also implies that
\iota_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}
is given by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Note that (
37
) also shows that the normal smoothing
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
is even and split-free: the target fibration is unchanged and
K_{q}(M_{0})\cong V
is finitely generated, and free since the normal smoothing
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is even and split-free.
Consequently, the 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{2},j_{2}):=(\iota_{\overline{\nu}_{2}},j_{\overline{\nu}_{2}})
is defined.
Remark
7.6
implies that
[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}
belongs to the set
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
.
This establishes the property (
iii
) of the normal smoothing
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
since (
37
) shows that
[\delta_{0}\circ f]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
.

Next we verify property (
iv
) of
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
.
We use (
37
) to identify
(V,\theta)
with
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
.
We must show that
[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}
coincides with
[\delta_{0}\circ f]_{\ell}
in the one-sided boundary automorphism set
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}))
.
We prove that the two following 2-sided primitive embeddings coincide:

\displaystyle(j_{\delta_{0}f},j_{\delta_{0}f}^{\prime}):=\Pr([\delta_{0}f]_{\ell})

\displaystyle\in\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}((V,\theta),(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}})),\text{ and}

\displaystyle([\iota_{2},j_{2}]_{\ell})

\displaystyle\in\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}((V,\theta),(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}})).

Since (
37
) established that
(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})=(V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}f}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}})
, the definition of
\Pr
from Construction
6.9
implies that
j_{\delta_{0}f}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Below (
37
), we already argued that
\iota_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and thus
\iota_{2}=j_{\delta_{0}f}
.
It remains to show that

.

Recall that the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
W_{1}
, originally obtained from
M_{1}
as the trace of the
q
-surgeries on the “diagonal sublagrangian”, could also be understood as the trace of trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries on
M_{2}
.
Similarly,
W_{0}
was defined as the trace of trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries on
M_{0}
.
For
i=0,1
, use
\overline{\nu}_{W_{i}}\colon W_{i}\to B
to denote the
(B,\xi)
-structure on
W_{i}
, and consider the composition

j_{W_{i}}\colon H^{q}(B)\xrightarrow{\overline{\nu}_{W_{i}}^{*}}H^{q}(W_{i})\xrightarrow{PD}H_{q+1}(W_{i},\partial W_{i}).

For
k=0,1
, we additionally consider the maps
p_{k}\colon H_{q+1}(W_{0},\partial W_{0})\to H_{q}(\partial W_{0})\to H_{q}(M_{k})
obtained by composing the connecting homomorphism from the long exact sequence of the pair
(W_{0},\partial W_{0})
with the projection
H_{q}(\partial W_{0})=H_{q}(M_{0})\oplus H_{q}(M_{1})\to H_{q}(M_{k})
.
The exact same process gives rise to maps
p_{k}^{\prime}\colon H_{q+1}(W_{1},\partial W_{1})\to H_{q}(\partial W_{1})\to H_{q}(M_{k})
for
k=1,2
.
Recall that for
i=0,1,2
, we defined
j_{i}:=j_{\overline{\nu}_{i}}
as
PD_{M_{i}}\circ\overline{\nu}_{i}^{*}
.
A short diagram chase involving Poincaré duality and the definition of the
j_{i}
yields the following commutative diagram:

(38)

\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{0})}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W_{0},\partial W_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H^{q}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{W_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{j_{W_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{2})}
\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W_{1},\partial W_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{1}).}

Since the cobordism
W_{1}
was obtained as the trace of trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries on
M_{2}
along
V=\varphi(D)
, we know that

W_{1}=\big(M_{2}\times[0,1]\big)\natural\big(\natural_{i=1}^{r}S^{q}\times D^{q+1}\big)\simeq M_{2}\vee\bigvee_{i=1}^{r}S^{q}\text{ and }M_{1}\cong M_{2}\mathbin{\#}(\mathbin{\#}_{i=1}^{r}S^{q}\times S^{q}).

We deduce that inclusion induces an isomorphism
H_{i}(M_{2})\cong H_{i}(W_{1})
for
i\neq q
. Also, we infer that the inclusion induced map
\iota\colon H_{q}(\partial W_{1})\to\penalty\ H_{q}(W_{1})
is surjective.
As
W_{1}
is a cobordism between
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, the inclusion induces an isomorphism
H_{i}(M_{1})\oplus H_{i}(M_{2})\cong H_{i}(\partial W_{1})
.
Combining these observations, the long exact sequence of the pair
(W_{1},\partial W_{1})
now looks as follows:

\textstyle{H_{q+1}(W_{1},\partial W_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}^{\prime}}

\textstyle{H_{q}(\partial W_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{q}(W_{1})}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{1})}
\textstyle{V\oplus V^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{V^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\left(V\oplus V^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\right)\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\textstyle{V\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

Since
M_{1}
is also obtained by trivial surgeries on
M_{0}
, the same reasoning can be applied on the trace cobordism
W_{0}
by replacing
M_{1},M_{2}
by
M_{1},M_{0}
.
Using the resulting identifications, we deduce that
j_{W_{0}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
j_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
(the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing on
M_{0}
was extended trivially over the cobordism
W_{0}
) and therefore the commutative diagram displayed in (
38
) takes the following form:

\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{0})}
\textstyle{V^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus H_{q}(M_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{H^{q}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{W_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
j_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\circ j_{1}}
\textstyle{H_{q}(M_{2})}
\textstyle{V^{*}\oplus H_{q}(M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{H_{\varepsilon}(V)\oplus H_{q}(M_{2}).}

The commutativity of the upper right triangle now gives
j_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&j_{0}\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{T}
.
Lemma
7.9
(
a
) then implies that
\varphi j_{1}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&j_{\delta_{0}f}\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{T}
.
The commutativity of the bottom right triangle yields
p_{1}^{\prime}j_{W_{1}}=\varphi j_{1}
.
Applying the splitting
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
of
p_{1}^{\prime}
to both sides of this equation, we deduce that
j_{W_{1}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
j_{\delta_{0}f}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
We conclude that
j_{2}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)j_{W_{1}}=j_{\delta_{0}f}
, as desired.

Summarising, we have shown that the 2-sided primitive embeddings
(j_{\delta_{0}f},j_{\delta_{0}f}^{\prime}):=\Pr([\delta_{0}f]_{\ell})
and
(\iota_{2},j_{2}):=\Pr([\delta_{2}]_{\ell})
agree in
\mathop{\rm\ell Prim}\nolimits
.
By Theorem
6.12
this implies that the boundary isomorphisms
[\delta_{0}\circ f]_{\ell}
and
[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}

agree in the set
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits_{(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})}(\partial(V,\theta),\partial(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}})).

This concludes the proof of (
iv
) and therefore of Theorem
7.7
.
∎

Example 7.10
.

We describe the diagonal sublagrangian
D\subseteq K_{q}(M_{1})
from (
33
) in a concrete example with
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}
.
Namely, we assume that
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})=(N_{y_{1},y_{2}},\overline{\nu}_{y_{1},y_{2}})
is an even, split-free normal
(2k-1)
-smoothing whose associated
2
-sided primitive embedding is the one from our running example (namely Example
6.4
): we set
q=y_{1}y_{2}
and consider

(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}}):=\left((\mathbb{Z},q)\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}}\right)\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-y_{1}\\
y_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},-q)\right).

Such smoothings exist, as described in Propositions
9.3
and
9.5
below.
In the notation of the proof of Theorem
7.7
, we have
(V,\theta)=K_{q}(M_{0})=(\mathbb{Z},q)
, so that
K_{q}(M_{1})=\mathbb{Z}^{3}\supset D
.
In this case, in order to describe
D
, we first rewrite the
2
-sided primitive embedding as a union, along
[f]:=[\delta(i_{y_{1},y_{2}},j_{y_{1},y_{2}})]
.
This was done in Example
6.13
and the outcome was

\left((\mathbb{Z},q)\xhookrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{2},{\begin{pmatrix}q&0\\
a&-s\end{pmatrix}}\right)\xhookleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-2q\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},-q)\right),

with
a=b=-(y_{1}z_{2}+y_{2}z_{1})
and
s=-z_{1}z_{2}
.
As a consequence, we deduce that in this case, we have
j_{f}^{\prime}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
-2q\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and therefore

\displaystyle D=\{(j_{f}^{\prime}(x),-x)\mid x\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\mathbb{Z}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-(y_{1}z_{2}+y_{2}z_{1})\\
-2y_{1}y_{2}\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{3}\cong K_{q}(M_{1}).

As a reality check, a brief calculation shows that
\lambda_{M_{1}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2q&a\\
a&-2s\end{smallmatrix}\right)\oplus(2q)
does indeed vanish on
D
(for this calculation, it is helpful once again to recall that
y_{2}z_{1}-y_{1}z_{2}=1
).
This diagonal lagrangian was used implicitly in the construction of the manifolds
N_{a,b}
from our previous article
[
CCPS21
]
.

It is worth noting that we do not claim that in general
M_{0}
and
M_{2}
have isometric intersection forms, even though their kernel forms are isometric. Corollary
7.12
will exhibit conditions under which this favourable situation occurs, but first a remark which introduces some notation.

Remark 7.11
.

Given split quadratic formations
x,y
and
f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
, we again write
[f]_{\ell}
for the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
and
[f]_{b}
for the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(x,y)
.
Given three isomorphic quadratic forms
b\cong v\cong v^{\prime}
, recall from Remark
6.6
that a boundary isomorphism
\delta\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
defines an element
[\delta]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
and an element
[\delta]_{b}\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)
, but does not determine an element in
\mathop{\rm\ell Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)
.

Keeping Remark
7.11
in mind, the next corollary provides a situation in which we can realise elements of
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits
sets as boundary isomorphisms associated to normal smoothings.

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
q\geq 4
be even, let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be an even, split-free normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
and
n
to denote the rank of
(V,\theta):=(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
.
If there is an isometry
(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}})\cong(V,\theta)
and
[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{0}}]_{b}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta))
, then for any boundary automorphism
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{n})}(\partial(V,\theta))
, there exists an even, split-free normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})

such that

(i)

(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
and
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
are stably diffeomorphic;

(ii)

M_{0}
and
M_{2}
have intersection forms isometric to
H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{n})
;

(iii)

the boundary isomorphism associated to
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
is given by

[\delta_{\overline{\nu}_{2}}]_{b}=[f]_{b}\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{n})}(\partial(V,\theta)).

The same holds in the topological category for
q=2
, assuming that
\pi_{1}(B)
is a good group.

Proof.

During this proof, we abbreviate
(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}),(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}}),(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
by
b,v,v^{\prime}
, and we write
\delta_{0}\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b),\delta_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
for the associated boundary isomorphisms.
The assumption that
[\delta_{0}]_{b}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
ensures that the intersection form of
M_{0}
is hyperbolic.
Apply Theorem
7.7
to
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{n})}(\partial(V,\theta))
in order to obtain a
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
that is stably diffeomorphic to
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
, whose associated boundary isomorphism is given by

(39)

[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}=[\delta_{0}f]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b).

We argue that
[\delta_{2}]_{b}=[f]_{b}
.
Recall from Remark
4.8
that (
39
) means that for some
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
, we have
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}=(\delta_{0}\circ f)\circ\partial h^{-1}
for some isometry
h\colon v\to v
.
Similarly,
[\delta_{0}]_{b}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
means that for some
\widetilde{\delta_{0}}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)
, we have
[\widetilde{\delta}_{0}]_{b}=\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
, where
\widetilde{\delta}_{0}=\partial h_{2}\circ\delta_{0}
for some isometry
h_{2}\colon b\to v
.
In turn this implies
\widetilde{\delta}_{0}=\partial h_{3}\circ\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits\circ\partial h_{4}^{-1}
for some isometries
h_{3},h_{4}\colon v\to v
.
In order to study
[\delta_{2}]_{b}
, we choose
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)
so that
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{\prime}=\partial h_{1}\circ\widetilde{\delta}_{2}
for some isometry
h_{1}\colon b\to v
.
Summing up, we obtain

\displaystyle[\delta_{2}]_{b}

\displaystyle:=[\widetilde{\delta}^{\prime}_{2}]_{b}=[\partial h_{1}\circ\widetilde{\delta}_{2}]_{b}=[\partial h_{1}\circ(\delta_{0}\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1})]_{b}=[\partial h_{1}\circ(\partial h_{2}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\delta}_{0})\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1}]_{b}

\displaystyle=[\partial h_{1}\circ\partial h_{2}^{-1}\circ(\partial h_{3}\circ\partial h_{4}^{-1})\circ f\circ\partial h^{-1}]_{b}=[f]_{b}.

Finally, we argue that
M_{0}
and
M_{2}
both have hyperbolic intersection forms.
For
M_{0}
, this follows from Remark
7.6
and from our assumption that
\delta_{0}=[\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits]_{b}
, and so we focus on
M_{2}
.
Recall from (
37
) that
(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})\cong(V,\theta)\cup_{\delta_{0}f}(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}}).

Since we have
(H^{q}(B),\theta_{M_{0}})\cong(V,-\theta)
(by assumption), this implies that
(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})\cong(V,\theta)\cup_{[\delta_{0}f]_{b}}(V,-\theta)
.
Since we have just established that
[\delta_{0}f]_{b}=[f]_{b}
and since
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda^{n})}(\partial(V,\theta))
, we deduce that
(H_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
is indeed hyperbolic.
This completes the proof of the corollary.
∎

We conclude this section by describing the application of Theorem
7.7
discussed in Remark
7.8
.
We recall the exact sequence of
[
CS11
, Theorem 5.11]
that was displayed in (
1
).
Given a quadratic form
v=(V,\theta)
over
R
whose symmetrisation
\lambda\colon V\to V^{*}
is injective (we call such a form
non-degenerate
),
[
CS11
, Theorem 5.11 and Lemma 6.2]
establish the existence of a sequence

(40)

L_{2q+1}^{s}(R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\delta}\operatorname{bIso}(\partial v,\partial v^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\kappa}L_{2q}^{s}(R).

Here,
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})
consists of quasi-formations whose induced form and its orthogonal complement respectively coincide
0
-stably with
v
and
v^{\prime}
, the dashed arrow
\rho
denotes the action of
L_{2q+1}^{s}(R)
on
\ell_{2q+1}(v,v^{\prime})
by direct sum,
\kappa
is the map induced by the assignment
f\mapsto v\cup_{f}-v^{\prime}
, and the map
\delta
is (roughly) described as follows.
For a quasi-formation
x=((M,\psi);F,V)
, use
j\colon(V,\theta)\hookrightarrow\penalty\ (M,\psi)
to denote the split isometric inclusion of the induced form into
(M,\psi)
.
The map
\delta
assigns to
x
the boundary isomorphism
f_{j}
constructed in Proposition
4.14
.

Remark 7.13
.

Given split quadratic formations
x,y
and
f\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
, we again write
[f]_{\ell}
for the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
and
[f]_{b}
for the class of
f
in
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(x,y)
.
Even though
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,y)
is not a group, given a third split quadratic formation
z
, we can consider the map

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,y)\times\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(x,z)

\displaystyle\to\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits(y,z)

\displaystyle([f]_{\ell},[g]_{\ell})

\displaystyle\mapsto[g^{-1}\circ f]_{b}.

This map does not depend on the choice of representatives for
[f]_{\ell}
and
[g]_{\ell}
: indeed for
h_{1}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(y)
and
h_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(z)
, we can use the definitions of the actions to obtain the following equalities:

[(g\circ\partial h_{2}^{-1})^{-1}\circ(f\circ\partial h_{1}^{-1})]_{b}=[\partial h_{2}\circ(g^{-1}\circ f)\circ\partial h_{1}^{-1}]_{b}=[(h_{1},h_{2})\cdot(g\circ f^{-1})]_{b}=[g\circ f^{-1}]_{b}.

Now let
b,v,v^{\prime}
be quadratic forms with an isometry
v\xrightarrow{\cong}v^{\prime}
.
As explained in Section
4.2
, even though we cannot identify
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
with
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
, we can identify
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
with
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
.
In particular, if
g\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
, then there is an isometry
h\colon v^{\prime}\to v
and a
\widetilde{g}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
such that
\widetilde{g}
and
g\circ\partial h^{-1}
agree in
\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
.
In particular, there is a map

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)\times\mathop{\rm\ell Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)

\displaystyle\to\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)

\displaystyle([f]_{\ell},[g]_{\ell})

\displaystyle\mapsto[\widetilde{g}^{-1}\circ f]_{b}=:[g]_{\ell}^{-1}\circ[f]_{\ell}.

From the discussion above, we already know that this assignment does not depend on the choice of representatives for
[f]_{\ell}
and
[g]_{\ell}
.
This map also does not depend on the choice of the isometry
h
: indeed if
h_{1},h_{2}\colon v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}v
are two isometries, then

\displaystyle[\partial h_{1}\circ g^{-1}\circ f]_{b}=[(\partial h_{2}\circ\partial h_{1}^{-1})\circ\partial h_{1}\circ g^{-1}\circ f]_{b}=[\partial h_{2}\circ g^{-1}\circ f]_{b}.

Using the notation of Theorem
7.7
, we apply this construction to the case where the quadratic forms
b,v
and
v^{\prime}
are
(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}})
,
(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
, and
(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
respectively.
In this case, the boundary isomorphism
\delta_{0}
associated to
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
belongs to
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
, while the associated normal smoothing
\delta_{2}
associated to
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
belongs to
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v^{\prime},\partial b)
.
The remarks above nevertheless allow us to make sense of the composition
[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}^{-1}\circ[\delta_{0}]_{\ell}
as an element of
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)
.

The next result describes the image under the map
\delta\colon\ell_{2q+1}(v,v)\to\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial v)
of the modified surgery obstruction for the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
constructed in Theorems
3.10
and
7.7
.

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
q\geq 4
be even and let
(M_{0}^{2q},\overline{\nu}_{0})
be an even, split-free normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing with non-degenerate kernel form
(V,\theta):=(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
.
Fix a quasi-formation
x=(H_{\varepsilon}(K);K,V)

whose induced form is
(V,\theta)
and satisfies
(V,\theta)\cong(V^{\perp},\theta^{\perp})
.
Assume either that
q
is even or that
(K\pi_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})\cong(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
.
Then there exists a normal
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
,
with modified surgery obstruction
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=[x]
,
from
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
to another even, split-free normal smoothing
(M_{2},\overline{\nu}_{2})
such that

\delta(\Theta(W,\overline{\nu}))=\delta(x)=[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}^{-1}\circ[\delta_{0}]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(V,\theta)).

The same holds in the topological category for
q=2
, assuming that
\pi_{1}(B)
is a good group.

Proof.

During this proof, we abbreviate
(H^{q}(B),-\theta_{M_{0}}),(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}}),(K_{q}(M_{2}),\theta_{M_{2}})
by
b,v,v^{\prime}
.
Since we assumed that
v\cong v^{\perp}
, we know that
[f]_{b}:=\delta(x)
belongs to
\operatorname{bAut}(\partial v)
.
As
v
is non-degenerate, the exact sequence displayed in (
1
) ensures that the union
v\cup_{f}-v
is hyperbolic.
We apply Theorem
7.7
to
a representative
f^{-1}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\partial v)
of
\delta(x)^{-1}
to obtain a
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,\overline{\nu})
with

[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}=[\delta_{0}\circ f^{-1}]_{\ell}\in\mathop{\rm\ell Aut}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
.
Now pick any
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}\in\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits(\partial v,\partial b)
as in Remark
7.13
.
In particular, there is an
h\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(v)
such that
\widetilde{\delta}_{2}=h\cdot(\delta_{0}\circ f^{-1})=\delta_{0}\circ f^{-1}\circ\partial h^{-1}
. It follows that

\delta(x)=[f]_{b}=[\partial h\circ f]_{b}=[(\partial h\circ f\circ\delta_{0}^{-1})\circ\delta_{0}]_{b}=[\widetilde{\delta}_{2}^{-1}\circ\delta_{0}]_{b}=[\delta_{2}]_{\ell}^{-1}\circ[\delta_{0}]_{\ell}.

It remains to show that
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=x
.
Recall that
(W,\overline{\nu})
was constructed by first performing
\operatorname{rk}(V)
trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries on
M_{0}
(yielding
M_{1}
), and then performing additional
q
-surgeries on the diagonal sublagrangian of
K_{q}(M_{1})=(V,\theta)\oplus\left((V,\theta)\cup_{f}(V,-\theta)\right)
.
We assert that this produces the
(B,\xi)
-cobordism of Theorem
3.10
which was shown to have surgery obstruction
x
.
As
(M_{0},\overline{\nu}_{0})
is even and split-free,
(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
is
a free Wall form.
Since we assumed that
(K_{q}(M_{0}),\theta_{M_{0}})
is isometric to the induced form
(V,\theta)
, the assertion follows from the construction in Theorem
3.10
; see in particular (
6
).
This proves the equality
\Theta(W,\overline{\nu})=x
, thus establishing the corollary.
∎

8.
Homotopy invariance of certain
2
-sided primitive embeddings

Let
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
be a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.
Due to the extra data afforded by
\overline{\nu}
, it is in general not clear that the
2
-sided primitive embedding associated to
(M,\overline{\nu})
is a homeomorphism invariant, let alone invariant under homotopy equivalences. On the other hand, this hurdle is not present when considering the
2
-sided primitive embedding associated to the functorial map
M\to P_{q-1}(M)
.
In this section, we therefore develop the notion of the Postnikov
2
-sided primitive embedding of a manifold, show that this object is invariant under orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences, and then relate it to the
2
-sided primitive embedding of a particular normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing.
We believe that the ideas in this section are of independent interest as they provide a general formalism for
defining homotopy invariants of manifolds, especially in certain modified surgery settings.

8.1.
Normal
(q{-}1)
-types

Given
m\geq q
, this subsection introduces a specific kind of normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle
that we will use in Section
9
.

Recall that the
j
-connected cover
X\langle j\rangle
of a
CW
-complex
X
is a
CW
-complex which, up to homotopy equivalence, is characterized by the property that
X\langle j\rangle
is
j
-connected and there is a fibration
p_{j}\colon X\langle j\rangle\to X
inducing isomorphisms on
\pi_{i}
for
i>j
. In our case, we take
X=BO
leading to a fibration

\vartheta_{k}\colon BO\langle k\rangle\to BO.

In this subsection, we are concerned with a closed, connected, oriented manifold
M^{2q}

such that for some
m\geq q
, the stable normal bundle
\nu\colon M\to BO
lifts to a map

\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle.

Such manifolds are called
m
-parallelisable
.
The next lemma describes the normal
(q{-}1)
-type of
m
-parallelisable
2q
-manifolds.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold whose stable normal bundle lifts to a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
for some
m\geq q
.
The following map is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle.

Furthermore, the normal
(q{-}1)
-type of
M
is represented by the fibration

\vartheta_{q}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle q\rangle\to BO,

where
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is the projection onto the second factor.

Proof.

Note that
\vartheta_{q}\colon BO\langle{q}\rangle\to BO
is
q
-coconnected, and
\pi_{i}(P_{q-1}(M))=0
for
i\geq q
. It follows that
\vartheta_{q}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is
q
-coconnected.
Also
p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)
lifts the stable normal bundle of
M
:

\vartheta_{m}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}\circ(p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M))=\vartheta_{m}\circ\nu_{m}(M)=\nu\colon M\to BO

Finally
p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)
is
q
-connected since
m\geq q
and
p_{q-1}(M)
is
q
-connected.
∎

8.2.
The Postnikov
2
-sided primitive embedding

We modify Definition
7.5
in order to obtain an invariant of manifolds, instead of an invariant of
q
-surjective maps.
More precisely, under some technical conditions, we use the Postnikov
(q{-}1)
-type to associate a
2
-sided
primitive embedding
\textup{PPE}(M)
to an oriented manifold
M^{2q}
.
After proving the invariance of
\textup{PPE}(M)
under orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences, we then relate
\textup{PPE}(M)
to the
2
-sided primitive embedding associated to the normal smoothing from Section
8.1
.

Construction 8.2
.

Let
\pi
be a group and set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Given an automorphism
\varphi\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
and an
\varepsilon
-quadratic form
(V,\theta)
, we write
\overline{(V,\theta)}^{\varphi}=(\overline{V}^{\varphi},\overline{\theta}^{\varphi})
for the quadratic form where

(i)

the underlying module
\overline{V}^{\varphi}
agrees with
V
as an abelian group but has left
\Lambda
-module structure
x\cdot v:=\varphi(x)v
for
x\in\Lambda
and
v\in V
;

(ii)

the quadratic form is defined as
\overline{\theta}^{\varphi}(x,y)=\varphi^{-1}(\theta(x,y))\in Q_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda)
.
Here it is understood that
\varphi
extends to a ring automorphism
\varphi\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda
which itself descends to
Q_{\varepsilon}(\Lambda)
.

If
i\colon(V,\theta)\to(M,\psi)
is
\Lambda
-linear and preserves quadratic forms, then so does
i\colon\overline{(V,\theta)}^{\varphi}\to\overline{(M,\psi)}^{\varphi}
.
The
action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
on a
2
-sided primitive
(i,j):=v\hookrightarrow m\hookleftarrow v^{\prime}
is defined as

\overline{(i,j)}^{\varphi}:=\overline{v}^{\varphi}\xhookrightarrow{i}\overline{m}^{\varphi}\xhookleftarrow{j}\overline{v^{\prime}}^{\varphi}.\penalty\

Since
\overline{v}^{\varphi}
need not be isomorphic to
v
, this construction in general does not provide an action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
on
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
.
It does however provide an action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
on the universal collection

\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi}:=\bigcup_{[m,v,v^{\prime}]}\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime}),

where
[m,v,v^{\prime}]
denotes isometry classes of triples of forms.
We then say that two
2
-sided primitive embeddings
(i_{0},j_{0}),(i_{1},j_{1})

agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi}/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
if
(i_{0},j_{0})
and
\overline{(i_{1},j_{1})}^{\varphi}
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits
for some automorphism
\varphi\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
, meaning that there is a commutative diagram with vertical maps isomorphisms:

\textstyle{v_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{m_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces v_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\overline{v_{1}}^{\varphi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{1}}
\textstyle{\overline{m_{1}}^{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\overline{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\varphi}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}

For a ring homomorphism
u\colon\Gamma\to\Lambda\penalty\
, we write
\Lambda_{u}
for the
(\Lambda,\Gamma)
-bimodule with underlying abelian group
\Lambda
, where the left
\Lambda
-module structure is given by multiplication and the right
\Gamma
-module structure is given by
p\cdot\gamma=pu(\gamma)
, for
p\in\Lambda
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Remark 8.3
.

Fix a group
\pi
and set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold.
Given an isomorphism
\psi\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi
, we have that
\Lambda_{\psi}
is a
(\Lambda,\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])
-bimodule, and the
\Lambda
-module
H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi})
is endowed with a
\Lambda
-valued intersection form
\lambda_{M,\psi}
, obtained by composing the Poincaré duality isomorphism with evaluation.
Since
\Lambda_{\psi}
admits a length zero free resolution as a right
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
-module, we have
H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi})\cong\Lambda_{\psi}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]}H_{q}(M;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])
, and similarly in cohomology.
Under this identification, it follows that
\lambda_{M,\psi}
is isometric to
(p\otimes x,q\otimes y)\mapsto p\psi(\lambda_{M}(x,y))\overline{q}
.

Convention 8.4
.

Given
k>0
, we always identify
\pi_{1}(P_{k}(M))
with
\pi_{1}(M)
.
This is possible because
P_{k}(M)
is obtained from
M
by adding cells of dimension
\geq k{+}2>2
.
In particular,
p_{k}(M)\colon M\to P_{k}(M)
induces maps
p_{q-1}(M)_{*}
and
p_{q-1}(M)^{*}
on twisted homology and cohomology respectively.

Definition 8.5
.

Fix a group
\pi
, and set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented, even
2q
-manifold
where
p_{q-1}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)
is split-free.
Given an isomorphism
\psi\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi
,
the Postnikov 2-sided primitive embedding of
M
is defined as

\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)=\big(\ker(p_{q-1}(M)_{*})\hookrightarrow H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi})\hookleftarrow H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M;\Lambda_{\psi})\big)\in\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi}/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi).

When no identification is fixed, we will write
\textup{PPE}(M)\in\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}
for the analogous
2
-sided primitive embedding, but where the homology groups have coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
. In this case we omit the superscript
\pi
.
Note that
\textup{PPE}(M)
and
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)
are indeed
2
-sided primitive embeddings by Example
7.3
and Lemma
7.4
.

We show that
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)
does not depend on the choice of the identification
\psi
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Fix a group
\pi
, and set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.
Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold with
p_{q-1}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)
even and split-free.
The Postnikov 2-sided primitive embedding
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)
of
M
does not depend on the identification
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\psi_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi
be isomorphisms for
i=1,2
.
For concision, throughout this proof, we set
\overline{p}:=p_{q-1}(M)
as well as
K_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{i}}):=\ker(\overline{p}_{*}\colon H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{i}})\to H_{q}(P_{q-1}(M_{i});\Lambda_{\psi_{i}}))
.
It can be verified that
\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
induces
\Lambda
-linear maps on twisted homology and cohomology that make the following diagram commute:

(41)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M);\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{K_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H^{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H^{2k}(P_{q-1}(M);\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\overline{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\overline{p}}}

The change of coefficients map
(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}
induces an isometry
\lambda_{M,\psi_{1}}\cong\overline{\lambda_{M,\psi_{2}}}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}
.
Indeed, the twisted intersection forms on
H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})
and
\overline{H_{q}(M;\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1}}
are both defined by composing duality with the evaluation map, and changing coefficients commutes with these maps.
Since the
2
-sided primitive embeddings are even, this implies that
(\psi_{2}\psi_{1}^{-1})_{*}
preserves the quadratic forms. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Note that
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)
is not in general an invariant of
M
: changing the orientation on
M
changes its equivariant intersection form and may therefore change
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M)
.
On the other hand the next proposition shows that
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}
is invariant under orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
M_{1}^{2q}
and
