M_{2}^{2q}
be closed, connected, oriented manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
, such that
p_{q-1}(M_{i})
is even and split-free for
i=1,2
.
If
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
is an orientation preserving homotopy equivalence, then
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M_{1})
and
\textup{PPE}^{\pi}(M_{2})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi}/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
.

Proof.

Choose arbitrary isomorphisms
\psi_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong\pi
for
i=1,2
.
Write
\overline{p}_{i}:=p_{q-1}(M_{i})
as well as
K_{q}(M_{i};\Lambda_{\psi_{i}}):=\ker((\overline{p}_{i})_{*})
for
i=1,2
, and
\varphi:=\psi_{2}f_{*}\psi_{1}^{-1}\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi)
.
The homotopy equivalence
f
and the automorphism
\varphi
induce
\Lambda
-linear isomorphism on twisted homology that fit into the following diagram:

(42)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(M_{1};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(M_{1};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(M_{1};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M_{1});\Lambda_{\psi_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}f_{*}^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}f_{*}^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{1}f_{*}^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M_{2});\Lambda_{\psi_{1}f_{*}^{-1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{K_{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H_{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H^{q}(M_{2};\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{H^{2k}(P_{q-1}(M_{2});\Lambda_{\psi_{2}})}^{\varphi}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{p}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\overline{p}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{p}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{p}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\overline{p}_{2}}}

This diagram commutes (for the top central square, we use that
f
is an orientation-preserving homotopy equivalence) and, in the second column, both
f_{*}
and
\varphi_{*}
induce isometries.
This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Construction 8.8
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
such that
p_{q-1}(M)
is even and split-free.
We wish to compare
\textup{PPE}(M)\in\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}
with the
2
-sided primitive embedding associated to the normal smoothing from Section
8.1
.

Set
B:=P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle
and consider the fibration
\xi\colon B\xrightarrow{\pr_{2}}BO\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{\vartheta_{m}}BO
.
Assume that the stable normal bundle
M\to BO
lifts to a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
.
For
m\geq q
, Lemma
8.1
showed that the following map is a normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle=:B.

Since
BO\langle m\rangle
is simply-connected, we have
\pi_{1}(B)=\pi_{1}(P_{q-1}(M))
, which we can canonically identify with
\pi_{1}(M)
as we have been doing up to now; recall Convention
8.4
.
Assuming that
\overline{\nu}
is even and split-free, we saw in Definition
7.5
how to associate a
2
-sided primitive embedding to
(M,\overline{\nu})
and we obtain

\textup{PE}(M,\overline{\nu})\in\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{H_{q}(M;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)])}(K_{q}(M;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]),H^{q}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]))\subseteq\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}.

Next we show that
\textup{PPE}(M)
and
\textup{PE}(M,\overline{\nu})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}
.

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold such that
p_{q-1}(M)
is even and split-free.
Assume that
M
admits a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
with
m\geq q
, which
lifts the stable normal bundle of
M
and consider the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle=:B.

If
\overline{\nu}
is also even and split-free, then
\textup{PPE}(M)
and
\textup{PE}(M,\overline{\nu})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}.

Proof.

Set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
.
Since
\overline{\nu}=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)
, it suffices to prove that the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon B\to P_{q-1}(M)
induces an isomorphism
(\operatorname{pr}_{1})_{*}\colon H_{q}(B;\Lambda)\cong H_{q}(P_{q-1}(M);\Lambda)
,
as the isomorphism
(\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}\colon H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M);\Lambda)\cong H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
then follows because
\overline{\nu}
and
p_{q-1}(M)
are split-free.
To see that
(\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}
is an isomorphism, consider the fibration
BO\langle m\rangle\to BO\langle m\rangle\times P_{q-1}(M)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{pr}_{1}}P_{q-1}(M)
and its homotopy long exact sequence.
We see that
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon\pi_{i}(BO\langle m\rangle\times P_{q-1}(M))\to\pi_{i}(P_{q-1}(M))
is an isomorphism for
i\leq q
.
Applying the relative Hurewicz theorem to the universal covers of these spaces then implies the assertion.
∎

Construction 8.10
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold such that
p_{q-1}(M)
is even and split-free.
Assume that
M
admits a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
with
m\geq q
,
which lifts the stable normal bundle of
M
, set
\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)
, and consider the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle=:B.

We let
\xi=\vartheta_{m}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{BO\langle{m}\rangle}\colon B\to BO

and perform a compatible
r
-surgery on
(M,\overline{\nu})
leading to a trace
(B,\xi)
-cobordism
(W,M,M^{\prime})
,
and therefore to a normal smoothing
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B
.
Assume that either (i)
r={q}
or (ii)
r=q{-}1
but with a surgery on unknotted framed embeddings.
Note that
(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is again even and split-free.
Also, we can identify
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
with
\pi_{1}(M)
.

The following proposition generalises Proposition
8.9
.

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold such that
p_{q-1}(M)
is even and split-free.
Assume that
M
admits a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
with
m\geq q
, which
lifts the stable normal bundle of
M
.
Assume further that the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle=:B

is split-free.
If
(W,M,M^{\prime})
is a trace
(B,\xi)
-cobordism as in Construction
8.10
, then
\textup{PPE}(M^{\prime})

and
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}.

Proof.

Set
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B)]=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})]=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
.
Our goal is to define a map
\Phi\colon B\to P_{q-1}(M^{\prime})
such that there is a homotopy
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\circ\Phi\simeq p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})
.
Indeed, if we had such a map, then the following diagram would commute and, after showing that
\Phi^{*}\colon H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M^{\prime});\Lambda)\to H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
is an isomorphism, the proposition would be proved:

(43)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M^{\prime});\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{q}(B;\Lambda).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\overline{\nu}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{PD}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\nu}^{\prime*}}

Use
\iota\colon M\to W
and
\iota^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to W
to denote the inclusions, and let
\overline{\nu}_{W}
and
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}=\overline{\nu}_{W}\circ\penalty\ \iota^{\prime}
be the extensions of
\overline{\nu}
.
By construction, the trace cobordism
W
is obtained by attaching an
(r+1)
-handle to
M\times[0,1]
.
The extension
\overline{\nu}_{W}
is null-homotopic on the
(r+1)
-handle and coincides with
\overline{\nu}
on
M
.
Composing
\overline{\nu}_{W}
and
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}
with the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle{m}\rangle\to BO\langle{m}\rangle
to the second coordinate gives maps
W\to BO\langle{m}\rangle
and
M^{\prime}\to BO\langle{m}\rangle
that fit into a commutative diagram:

(44)

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{m}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}_{W}}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle{m}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{BO\langle{m}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{BO\langle{m}\rangle}
\textstyle{BO\langle{m}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{=}

Next, composing
\overline{\nu}_{W}
and
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}
with the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle{m}\rangle\to P_{q-1}(M)
to the first coordinate gives maps
W\to P_{q-1}(M)
and
M^{\prime}\to P_{q-1}(M)
which fit into the following diagram:

(45)

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(M)}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(W)}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle{m}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(\iota),\simeq}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(W)}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(\iota^{\prime}),\simeq}

The two triangles and the right square commutes.
The bottom maps are homotopy equivalences because
M^{\prime}
is obtained from
M
by trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries or by
{q}
-surgeries, both of which do not affect the first
q{-}1
homotopy groups.
We claim that the left square commutes up to homotopy.
As recalled above,
\overline{\nu}_{W}
restricts to
\overline{\nu}
on
M
and is a null-homotopic map on the handle.
As a consequence,
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}_{W}
restricts to
p_{q-1}(M)
on
M
and is a null-homotopic map on the handle.
It follows that
p_{q-1}(\iota)\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}_{W}
restricts to
p_{q-1}(\iota)\circ p_{q-1}(M)=p_{q-1}(W)\circ\iota

on
M
and is a null-homotopic map on the handle.
Up to homotopy, this is precisely the effect of
p_{q-1}(W)
. The claim is established.

Via the diagrams in (
44
) and (
45
), we obtain the following homotopy-commutative diagram:

\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p_{q-1}(W)\\
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}_{W}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})\\
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(\iota)\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits,\simeq}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(W)\times BO\langle m\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M^{\prime})\times BO\langle m\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{q-1}(\iota^{\prime})\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{proj}_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{q-1}(M^{\prime}).}

Here, the maps
g
and
g^{\prime}
respectively denote the homotopy inverses of the homotopy equivalences
p_{q-1}(\iota)\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
and
p_{q-1}(\iota^{\prime})\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits
.
Note that up to homotopy,
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}=\overline{\nu}_{W}\circ\iota
can be expressed as

(46)

\overline{\nu}^{\prime}=\overline{\nu}_{W}\circ\iota^{\prime}\simeq g\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p_{q-1}(W)\\
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}_{W}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\circ\iota^{\prime}=g\circ(p_{q-1}(\iota^{\prime})\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits)\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})\\
\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Finally, we set

\Phi:=\operatorname{proj}_{1}\circ g^{\prime}\circ(p_{q-1}(\iota)\times\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits)\colon B\to P_{q-1}(M^{\prime}).

Using (
46
), one verifies that
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\circ\Phi\simeq p_{q-1}(M^{\prime})
.
Thanks to our identification of fundamental groups, we may assume that
\Phi
induces a map on twisted homology.
Taking a look at the definition of
\Phi
and using the same reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
8.9
, one then notes that
\Phi^{*}\colon H^{q}(P_{q-1}(M);\Lambda)\to H^{q}(B;\Lambda)
is an isomorphism.
This concludes the explanation of the diagram in (
43
) and therefore concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Finally, we prove that the
2
-sided primitive embeddings associated to the normal smoothings from Section
8.1
are invariant under orientation-preserving homotopy equivalences.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
M
be a closed, connected, oriented
2q
-manifold such that
p_{q-1}(M)
is even and split-free.
Assume that
M
admits a map
\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to BO\langle m\rangle
with
m\geq q
, which
lifts the stable normal bundle of
M
.
Assume further that the normal
(q{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}:=p_{q-1}(M)\times\nu_{m}(M)\colon M\to P_{q-1}(M)\times BO\langle m\rangle=:B

is split-free.
Assume that
(W,M,M^{\prime})
is a bordism over
B
obtained by performing a sequence of trivial
(q{-}1)
-surgeries followed by a sequence of compatible
q
-surgeries.
If
M
and
M^{\prime}
are orientation-preserving homotopy equivalent, then
\textup{PE}(M,\overline{\nu})
and
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi_{1}(M))
.

Proof.

Since
M
and
M^{\prime}
are orientation-preserving homotopy equivalent, Proposition
8.9
ensures that
\textup{PPE}(M)
and
\textup{PPE}(M^{\prime})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}/\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi_{1}(M))
.
But now by Proposition
8.11
, we know that
\textup{PPE}(M)
and
\textup{PE}(M,\overline{\nu})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\pi_{1}(M)}
, and similarly for
\textup{PPE}(M^{\prime})
and
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
.
This concludes the proof of the corollary.
∎

9.
Manifolds with infinite homotopy stable class

In this section we prove
Theorem
1.1
from the introduction.
For the readers’ convenience, we recall the necessary terminology.
Firstly, two closed
2q
-manifolds
M_{0}
and
M_{1}

with the same Euler characteristic
are
stably diffeomorphic
if there exists a positive integer
g
and a diffeomorphism

M_{0}\mathbin{\#}W_{g}\cong M_{1}\mathbin{\#}W_{g},

where
W_{g}=\mathbin{\#}_{g}(S^{q}\times S^{q})
.
Secondly, recall that the
homotopy stable class
of a
2q
-manifold
M_{0}
is defined
to be the set of homotopy classes of manifolds stably diffeomorphic to
M
:

\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h}(M_{0})=\{M_{1}^{2q}\mid M_{1}\text{ is stably diffeomorphic to }M_{0}\}/\text{homotopy equivalence.}

In this section we show that there are manifolds with infinite homotopy stable class.
A manifold
M
is
stably parallelisable
if its tangent bundle becomes trivial after
Whitney sum with a trivial bundle.
We assume that
q=2k
, so that we consider
4k
-dimensional manifolds.
We also exclude dimension four. This is because we rely on the structure of the cohomology ring of the manifolds we construct, in a way that cannot occur for closed 4-manifolds.

The main result of this section is the following theorem, which restates Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
k\geq 2
.
There exist infinitely many closed, oriented, connected
4k
-manifolds
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}
that are stably parallelisable, have hyperbolic equivariant intersection form, equal Euler characteristic, are pairwise not stably diffeomorphic, and moreover each of the
M_{i}
has infinite homotopy stable class:

|\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{h}(M_{i})|=\infty.

In fact, for each
i
, there are infinitely many
4k
-manifolds
M_{i}^{1},M_{i}^{2},\ldots
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i}^{j})\cong\mathbb{Z}

that have hyperbolic intersection form and hyperbolic equivariant intersection form,
and are all stably diffeomorphic to
M_{i}
, but as
j

varies the
M_{i}^{j}
are pairwise not homotopy equivalent.

Moreover,
M_{i}^{j}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}\cong M_{i}^{j^{\prime}}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}
for all pairs
\{j,j^{\prime}\}
,
but
M_{i}^{j}
is homotopy equivalent to a connected sum
N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
with
N
a simply-connected

4k
-manifold if and only if
j=0
.

We briefly describe the strategy of the proof of the theorem.
In Proposition
9.3
we recall the simply-connected
4k
-manifolds
N_{a,b}
that we constructed in
[
CCPS21
]
, for a pair of positive, coprime integers
\{a,b\}
.
The cohomology rings depend on
a
and
b
.
We define

M_{a,b}:=N_{a,b}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}).

These manifolds satisfy
\pi_{1}(M_{a,b})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and will serve as base manifolds.
We apply Corollary
7.7
to realise the infinitely many boundary automorphisms
\{f_{i}\}
that we constructed in Section
5
by bordisms
W_{f_{i}}
. The
4k
-manifolds
M^{f_{i}}_{a,b}
arising as the new boundary components of these bordisms
\partial_{+}W^{f_{i}}
will be the infinite family of pairwise stably diffeomorphic manifolds promised by the theorem. We apply Corollary
8.12
to show that they are pairwise not homotopy equivalent.

In order to rule out orientation-reversing homotopy equivalences, we shall appeal to the following observation, which also appeared in
[
CCPS21
]
. We recall it here for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
N
and
N^{\prime}
be closed, connected, oriented
4k
-manifolds.
Suppose that a class
z
freely generates
H^{2}(N;\mathbb{Z})
and
z^{2k}=n
for some
nonzero
n\in\mathbb{Z}=H^{4k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
, and similarly for
(N^{\prime},z^{\prime})
then any homotopy equivalence
f\colon N\to N^{\prime}
must be orientation preserving.
Moreover, if
N^{\prime\prime}
is stably diffeomorphic to such an
N
and
k\geq 2
,
then
H^{2}(N^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{Z})
is freely generated by a class
z^{\prime\prime}
with
(z^{\prime\prime})^{2k}=n
and so
any homotopy equivalence
N^{\prime\prime}\to N^{\prime}
is orientation preserving.

In order to apply this lemma, we shall orient our manifolds
N
,
N^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime\prime}
in such a way that e.g.
z^{2k}\cap[N]\in H_{0}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
is positive, and similarly for
N^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime\prime}
. If we do this then we will be able to deduce that any homotopy equivalence between two of them is orientation preserving.

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Assume that
f
is of degree
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.
Since
f
is a homotopy equivalence,
N
and
N^{\prime}
have isomorphic cohomology rings.
In particular
H^{2}(N^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is generated by
z^{\prime}=\pm f^{-*}(z)
.
Since
{z^{\prime}}^{2k}=n
in
H^{4k}(N^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and
f^{*}({z^{\prime}}^{2k})=z^{2k}
, properties of the cap and cup products show that

n=f_{*}(z^{2k}\cap[N])=f_{*}(f^{*}({z^{\prime}}^{2k})\cap[N])={z^{\prime}}^{2k}\cap f_{*}([N])={z^{\prime}}^{2k}\cap\varepsilon[N^{\prime}]=\varepsilon n.

Since
n\neq 0
, this implies that
f
must be orientation-preserving.

To prove the last assertion, use the naturality of the cup product together with the degree one
map
N\mathbin{\#}(S^{2k}\times S^{2k})\to N
that collapses
S^{2k}\times S^{2k}
to a point, and induces isomorphisms on the second and

4k
-th cohomology. This shows that
N
has an element
z\in H^{2}(N;\mathbb{Z})
as in the statement of the lemma, if and only if
H^{2}(N\mathbin{\#}(S^{2k}\times S^{2k});\mathbb{Z})
does, and similarly for
N^{\prime\prime}
. The last sentence of the lemma then follows from the argument of the previous paragraph.
∎

9.1.
The simply-connected
4k
-manifolds
N_{a,b}

Let us recall the next proposition from
[
CCPS21
]
, which builds the simply-connected
4k
-manifolds
N_{a,b}
. We will then proceed to discuss some of their properties.

Proposition 9.3
.

Fix
k\geq 2
. Given an unordered pair
\{a,b\}
of positive coprime integers such that
(2k)!
divides
2ab
, there exists a closed, connected, oriented, smooth
4k
-manifolds
N^{4k}_{a,b}
with the following properties.

(i)

The manifold
N_{a,b}
is simply-connected and stably parallelisable.

(ii)

The ring
H^{*}(N_{a,b};\mathbb{Z})
has generators
w,x,y,z
and
1
of degrees
2k{+}2
,
2k
,

2k
,
2
and
0
respectively,
with
z^{k}=ax+by,x^{2}=0=y^{2},2abw=z^{k+1},xz=bw,yz=aw
and
xy

generates
H^{4k}(N_{a,b};\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

There is a
2k
-connected map
\beta\colon N_{a,b}\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}

In particular, the intersection form of
N_{a,b}
is hyperbolic and
z^{2k}=2abxy
is
2ab

times a fundamental class of
N_{a,b}
.
If
\{a,b\}\neq\{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\}
then
N_{a,b}
and
N_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
have non-isomorphic integral cohomology rings
and so are not homotopy equivalent. Moreover if
ab\neq a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
, then
N_{a,b}
and
N_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
are
not even stably diffeomorphic.

As mentioned at the start of the section, we will use
N_{a,b}
to construct our base manifolds

M_{a,b}:=N_{a,b}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
. We will prove some facts purely about
N_{a,b}
, and then later obtain their analogues for
M_{a,b}
.

Next we describe a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing for
N_{a,b}
.
Since
N_{a,b}
is stably parallelisable, the stable normal bundle
\nu\colon N_{a,b}\to BO
factors through a map
\nu_{2k}\colon N_{a,b}\to BO\langle 4k\rangle
followed by
\vartheta_{2k}\colon BO\langle{4k}\rangle\to BO
.
This is a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing but it is not the normal
(2k{-}1)
-type.
We also use
p_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})\colon N_{a,b}\to P_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})
to denote the
(2k{-}1)
st Postnikov stage of
N_{a,b}
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
a
and
b
be positive, coprime integers such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
.
The
(2k{-}1)
st Postnikov stage
P_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
, and a normal
(2k{-}1)
-type of
N_{a,b}
is given by the fibration

\vartheta_{2k}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\times BO\langle 4k\rangle\to BO,

where
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is the projection onto the second factor.
The map

\overline{\nu}_{a,b}:=p_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})\times\nu_{2k}\colon N_{a,b}\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\times BO\langle 4k\rangle=:B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})

is a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing.
Write
\left(\overline{\nu}\colon N\to B_{2k-1}(N)\right):=\left(\overline{\nu}_{a,b}\colon N_{a,b}\to B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})\right)
to ease notation.
It satisfies the following properties.

(i)

The evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{N}\colon H^{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

The following evaluation maps are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{P_{2k-1}(N)}\colon H^{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{B_{2k-1}(N)}\colon H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}).

(iii)

The abelian groups
K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z}),\ker(p_{2k-1}(N)_{*})
,
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})
, and
H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})
are finitely generated and free.

In particular, the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon N\to B_{2k-1}(N)
is split-free.

Proof.

Since there is a
2k
-connected map
\beta\colon N_{a,b}\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
(by item (
iii
) of Proposition
9.3
) and since
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\simeq K(\mathbb{Z},2)
, we see that
P_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})\simeq\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
.
The first part of the lemma then follows from Lemma
8.1
.

Now we check the claimed properties.
For (
i
), since
N\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
is
2k
-connected, we have
H_{2k-1}(N;\mathbb{Z})=0
and thus the universal coefficient theorem ensures that
\operatorname{ev}_{N}
is an isomorphism.
For (
ii
), as
B_{2k-1}(N)\simeq\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle
, we deduce from the Künneth theorem that
H_{2k-1}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=H_{2k-1}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=0
, and thus we also obtain that
\operatorname{ev}_{B_{2k-1}(N)}
and
\operatorname{ev}_{P_{2k-1}(N)}
are isomorphisms.
Since
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
is free and
\mathbb{Z}
is a PID, we deduce that
K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\ker({\overline{\nu}}_{*})
and
\ker(p_{2k-1}(N)_{*})
are also free.
Also
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, so this is f.g. and free, proving (
iii
).
The second and third assertions imply, by definition, that
p_{2k-1}(N)
and the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}
are split-free.
∎

As in the statement of Lemma
9.4
, when the integers
a,b
are fixed or clear from the context, we write
N
,
B_{2k-1}(N)
and
\overline{\nu}
instead of
N_{a,b},B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})
and
\overline{\nu}_{a,b}
.
By Proposition
9.3
, the intersection form on
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
is hyperbolic and, in particular nonsingular. Since
4k=2q
implies
q
is even, and the intersection form on
N
is hyperbolic, it admits a unique quadratic refinement, so the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon N\to B_{2k-1}(N)
is even. We showed in Lemma
9.4
that
\overline{\nu}\colon N\to B_{2k-1}(N)
is a split-free normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing. Therefore the 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
associated with the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}
is defined.
The next lemma describes it in detail.

Proposition 9.5
.

Given positive, coprime integers
a
and
b
such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
, the 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
associated to

\overline{\nu}_{a,b}\colon N_{a,b}\to B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})
is isomorphic to

\left((\mathbb{Z},-ab)\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)}H_{+}(\mathbb{Z})\xleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\mathbb{Z},ab)\right).

Proof.

For ease of notation, we set

\left(\overline{\nu}\colon N\to B_{2k-1}(N)\right):=\left(\overline{\nu}_{a,b}\colon N_{a,b}\to B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b})\right)
.
In order to describe the primitive embedding
j_{\overline{\nu}}\colon H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
, we first choose bases for the
\mathbb{Z}
-modules
H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
and
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
Recall from Proposition
9.3
that
H^{2}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle z\rangle
and
H^{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle x\rangle\oplus\mathbb{Z}\langle y\rangle
, where
z^{2k}=ax+by
.
Passing to homology, we endow
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
with the basis
(\bar{x},\bar{y})
Poincaré dual to
(x,y)
.
In other words,
we set
\bar{x}:=\operatorname{PD}(x)
and
\bar{y}:=\penalty\ \operatorname{PD}(y)
.
Since
BO\langle{4k}\rangle
is
4k
-connected, it follows that
H^{2}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=H^{2}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
\overline{\nu}
is
{2k}
-connected, it induces an isomorphism between the second cohomology groups.
We set
v:=\penalty\ \overline{\nu}^{-*}(z)
, so that
H^{2}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle v\rangle
.
It follows that
H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle v^{k}\rangle
and therefore the primitive embedding
j_{\overline{\nu}}
is determined by its value on
v^{k}
.
Since, by definition, we have
j_{\overline{\nu}}=\operatorname{PD}\circ\overline{\nu}^{*}
, we deduce that

j_{\overline{\nu}}(v^{k})=\operatorname{PD}\circ\overline{\nu}^{*}(v^{k})=\operatorname{PD}(z^{k})=\operatorname{PD}(ax+by)=a\overline{x}+b\overline{y}.\penalty\

In other words, with respect to the bases
v^{k}
and
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
, the map
j_{\overline{\nu}}
is represented by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The quadratic form
j_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{N}j_{\overline{\nu}}
on
H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})
is obtained by pulling back the (hyperbolic) form
\theta_{N}
on
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
.
The description of
j_{\overline{\nu}}
is now concluded by the following computation:

j_{\overline{\nu}}^{*}\theta_{N}j_{\overline{\nu}}(v^{k},v^{k})=\theta_{N}(j_{\overline{\nu}}(v^{k}),j_{\overline{\nu}}(v^{k}))=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a\\
b\end{pmatrix}=ab.

In order to describe
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}\colon K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\hookrightarrow H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
, we first determine the isometry type of the quadratic form
(K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z}),\theta_{N})
.
To start with, we base
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
.
Using Lemma
9.4
(
ii
), we know that

\mathbb{Z}\langle v^{k}\rangle=H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z}).\penalty\

We base
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
with the image of
v^{k}
under this isomorphism and base the group
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\cong\penalty\ \mathbb{Z}
with the dual basis
v^{k}_{*}
.
Using Lemma
9.4
(
i
), we can apply the same procedure to
N
, yielding basis elements
x_{*},y_{*}\in H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that are hom-dual to
x,y
.
Observe that
x_{*}=\overline{y}
and
y_{*}=\overline{x}
.
With respect to the bases
(x_{*},y_{*})
and
v^{k}_{*}
, the map
{\overline{\nu}}_{*}
is given by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)^{T}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Passing to the basis
(\overline{x},\overline{y})
, we see that
{\overline{\nu}}_{*}
is represented by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Taking the kernel of this map, we deduce that

K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\ker(\overline{\nu}_{*})=\mathbb{Z}\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle.

Pulling back the hyperbolic form from
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
, we infer that the quadratic form on
K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})
is represented by
(-ab)
.
This implies that
(K_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z}),\theta_{N})=(\mathbb{Z},-ab)
, and the computation of
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}
now follows readily.
This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

9.2.
The
4k
-manifolds
M_{a,b}
and their associated primitive embeddings

Given two positive, coprime integers
a
and
b
such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
, as above let
N_{a,b}
be the simply-connected
{4k}
-manifold built in the proof of Proposition
9.3
and studied in the previou subsection, and let

(47)

M_{a,b}:=N_{a,b}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}).

We need the analogous results for
M_{a,b}
to those that we proved in Lemma
9.4
and Proposition
9.5
for
N_{a,b}
. We will use these two results to deduce their analogues.

Since both
N_{a,b}
and
S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}
are stably parallelisable, their stable normal bundles lift to
BO\langle{4k}\rangle
.
The same assertion therefore holds for
M_{a,b}
.
In particular, its stable normal bundle lifts to a map
\nu_{2k}\colon M_{a,b}\to BO\langle{4k}\rangle
.
Using
p_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})\colon M_{a,b}\to P_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})
to denote the
(2k{-}1)
st Postnikov stage of
M_{a,b}
, Lemma
9.4
and Lemma
8.1
yields the following result.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
a
and
b
be positive, coprime integers such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
.
A normal
(2k{-}1)
-type for the stably parallelisable
{4k}
-manifold
M_{a,b}
is given by the fibration

\vartheta_{2k}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2}\colon P_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle\to BO,

where
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
is the projection onto the second factor.
The following map is a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing:

\overline{\nu}_{a,b}:=p_{{2k-1}}(M_{a,b})\times\nu_{2k}\colon M_{a,b}\to P_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle=:B_{2k-1}(M_{a,b}).

When the integers
a,b
are clear from the context, as before we write
M,N,B_{2k-1}(M),B_{2k-1}(N),\overline{\nu}
instead of
M_{a,b},N_{a,b},B_{2k-1}(M_{a,b}),B_{2k-1}(N_{a,b}),\overline{\nu}_{a,b}
.
Our aim is to describe the
2
-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
associated with the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}
.
From now on, we set

\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(B_{2k-1}(M))]=\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]

(recall Convention
8.4
) and note that the intersection form on the
\Lambda
-module
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
is hyperbolic, and therefore admits a quadratic refinement. In particular, the form is nonsingular and the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing is even.
To ensure that
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
is defined, we must check that
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B_{2k-1}(M)
is a split-free normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
a
and
b
be positive, coprime integers such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
.
Writing
M:=M_{a,b}
as well as
N:=N_{a,b}
, the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B_{2k-1}(M)
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

The evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{\!M}\colon H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

The following evaluation maps are isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{P_{2k-1}(M)}\colon H^{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda),\Lambda)},

\displaystyle\operatorname{ev}_{B_{2k-1}(M)}\colon H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda),\Lambda)}.

(iii)

The inclusion
\mathring{N}\to M
induces an isomorphism
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
.

(iv)

The inclusion
\mathring{N}\to M
induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda),

\displaystyle H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda).

In particular,
H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
and
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
are finitely generated and free.

(v)

The kernels
K_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
and
\ker(p_{2k-1}(M))
are finitely generated free
\Lambda
-modules.

In particular,
p_{2k-1}(M)
and the normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B_{2k-1}(M)
are split-free.

Proof.

The first three assertions are proved using the universal coefficient spectral sequence, or UCSS for short
[
Lev77
, Theorem 2.3]
.
For a space
Y
, this spectral sequence is given by

E_{2}^{p,q}=\overline{\operatorname{Ext}^{q}_{\Lambda}(H_{p}(Y;\Lambda),\Lambda)}\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(Y;\Lambda).

To describe the second page of the UCSS for
Y=M
, we compute
H_{i}(M;\Lambda)
for
0<i\leq{2k}
.
As
M=N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
, the universal cover is homeomorphic to an infinite connected sum of copies of
N
, so we obtain
H_{i}(M;\Lambda)\cong H_{i}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
for
0<i\leq 2k
.
We deduce the
\Lambda
-homology of
M
, establishing in particular the third assertion (
iii
):

(48)

H_{i}(M;\Lambda)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&\quad\text{if }i=0,\\
0&\quad\text{if }0<i\leq 2k,i\text{ odd},\\
H_{i}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda&\quad\text{if }0<i\leq 2k,i\text{ even}.\end{cases}

We infer that the odd columns of the second page of the UCSS vanish.
Then since the homology of
N
is f.g. free over
\mathbb{Z}
, the homology groups
H_{i}(M;\Lambda)
for
0<i\leq 2k
and
i
even are also f.g. free over
\Lambda
.
It follows that for
p\geq 1
and
0<i\leq 2k
we have

\overline{\operatorname{Ext}^{p}_{\Lambda}(H_{i}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}=0.

A spectral sequence computation then shows that
\operatorname{ev}_{N}\colon H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)\to\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}
is an isomorphism.
This establishes (
i
).

We prove the second assertion (
ii
), namely the analogous result for
Y=B_{2k-1}(M)
and
Y=P_{2k-1}(M)
.
It suffices to show that for
i\leq 2k-1
, we have
H_{i}(Y;\Lambda)=H_{i}(M;\Lambda)
. The computation of
H^{2k}(Y;\Lambda)
then follows the exact same steps as for
Y=M
.
Using Lemma
9.6
we have
B_{2k-1}(M)\simeq P_{2k-1}(M)\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle
.
Since
BO\langle{4k}\rangle
is
{4k}
-connected and
P_{2k-1}(M)
is obtained from
M
by adding cells of dimension
2k+1
or greater, we obtain
H_{i}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)=H_{i}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)=H_{i}(M;\Lambda)
for
i\leq{2k-1}
, as desired.
This establishes (
ii
).

We move on to the fourth assertion (
iv
).
First note that

\displaystyle H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda

\displaystyle\cong H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N)\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\cong H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda

\displaystyle\cong H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\cong\Lambda

by the Künneth theorem and since
BO\langle{4k}\rangle
is
4k
-connected.
Similarly, we claim that

H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)\cong H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M)\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle;\Lambda)\cong H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda).

To see this, first note that the fibration
BO\langle 4k\rangle\to BO\langle 4k\rangle\times P_{2k-1}(M)\to P_{2k-1}(M)
implies that
\pi_{i}(BO\langle 4k\rangle\times P_{2k-1}(M))=\pi_{i}(P_{2k-1}(M))
for
i\leq 4k
.
Applying the relative Hurewicz theorem to the universal covers of these spaces then implies the claim.

The proof of (
iv
) therefore reduces to showing that the inclusion induced map
P_{2k-1}(N)=P_{2k-1}(\mathring{N})\to P_{2k-1}(M)
gives rise to an isomorphism

H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda).

For later use, note that
H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\Lambda)=\Lambda
is generated by the
{2k}
-cells of
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
.

Since
P_{2k-1}(M)
only depends on the homotopy type of the
{2k}
-skeleton of
M=N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
, which is homotopy equivalent to
N^{(2k)}\vee S^{1}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle P_{2k-1}(M)

\displaystyle\simeq P_{2k-1}(N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}))\simeq P_{2k-1}((N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1}))^{({2k})})

(49)

\displaystyle\simeq P_{2k-1}(N^{({2k})}\vee S^{1})\simeq P_{2k-1}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1}).

Here we use that since
N\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
is
2k
-connected, so is the composition
N^{(2k)}\to N\to\penalty\ \mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
, from which it follows that
N^{(2k)}\vee S^{1}\to\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1}
is
2k
-connected and therefore we have
P_{2k-1}(N^{(2k)}\vee S^{1})\simeq P_{2k-1}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1})
as asserted for the last equivalence of (
9.2
).

In the Claim below, we prove that
H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1});\Lambda)=\Lambda=H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\Lambda)
, generated by the
{2k}
-cells of
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
.
Since this is also the case for
P_{2k-1}(N)
, combining the Claim below with the equalities from (
9.2
) will prove (
iv
), that the inclusion
\mathring{N}\to M
induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda),

\displaystyle H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda).

Claim
.

H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1});\Lambda)\cong\Lambda
, generated by the
{2k}
-cell of
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
.

Proof of claim:

Let
X:=\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}\vee S^{1}
and consider the map
p_{2k-1}\colon X\to P_{2k-1}(X)
.
By definition,
p_{2k-1}
is a
{2k}
-connected and hence

p_{2k-1*}\colon H_{2k}(X;\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda)

is surjective.
The universal cover
\widetilde{X}
of
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
with a copy of
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
wedged on
at each integer, and so

(50)

H_{2k}(X;\Lambda)=H_{2k}(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2k}(X;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda\cong\Lambda,

generated by the
2k
cell of
\mathbb{C}P^{\infty}
. Therefore to prove the claim it suffices to show that
p_{2k-1*}
is injective, which we now do.

It follows from
H_{2k}(X;\Lambda)\cong H_{2k}(X;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
that for every nonzero
u\in H_{2k}(X;\Lambda)
there is a class

\alpha\in H^{2k}(X;\Lambda)
such that
\langle{\alpha,u}\rangle\neq 0
.
For every
\ell>0
we have

\phi\colon H^{2\ell}(X;\Lambda)\cong H^{2\ell}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\Lambda)\cong H^{2\ell}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda,

which is isomorphic to
\Lambda
and generated by
(\phi^{-1}(z\otimes 1))^{\ell}
for
z\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})
a generator.
Here, to make sense of this cup product, we think of
H^{2}(X;\Lambda)
as the compactly support cohomology group
H^{2}_{c}(\widetilde{X};\mathbb{Z})
.
Now

p_{2k-1}^{*}\colon H^{2}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda)\to H^{2}(X;\Lambda)
is an isomorphism,
since
p_{2k-1}
is
2k
-connected.
We may therefore invert
p_{2k-1}^{*}
to define the element

d:=p_{2k-1}^{-*}(\phi^{-1}(z\otimes 1))\in H^{2}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda).

We consider its
k
th power
d^{k}\in H^{2k}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda)
.
Note that

p_{2k-1}^{*}(d^{k})=(p_{2k-1}^{*}(d))^{k}=(\phi^{-1}(z\otimes 1))^{k}=\phi^{-1}(z^{k}\otimes 1).

This shows that
d^{k}
maps under
p_{2k-1}^{*}
to the generator of
H^{2k}(X;\Lambda)
.
The map
p_{2k-1}^{*}
is therefore surjective and hence
\alpha=p_{2k-1}^{*}(\beta)\in H^{2k}(X;\Lambda)
for some
\beta\in H^{2k}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda)
.
It follows that

\langle{\beta,p_{{2k-1}*}(u)}\rangle=\langle{p_{2k-1}^{*}(\beta),u}\rangle=\langle{\alpha,u}\rangle\neq 0,

so in particular
p_{{2k-1}*}(u)\neq 0
.
Thus
p_{{2k-1}*}\colon H_{2k}(X;\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(P_{2k-1}(X);\Lambda)
is
injective, and so is an isomorphism, which completes the proof of the claim.

As explained above, the claim completes the proof of (
iv
) of Lemma
9.7
.

Having completed the proof of the first four assertions of Proposition
9.7
, we prove (
v
).
Since
\overline{\nu}
is
{2k}
-connected, the induced map
\overline{\nu}_{*}\colon H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
is surjective.
The fourth assertion implies that
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
is free.
We deduce that the following short exact sequence splits:

0\to K_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\overline{\nu}_{*}}H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)\to 0.

The third item ensures that
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
is free.
We therefore deduce that
K_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
is finitely generated and projective.
Since finitely generated, projective
\Lambda
-modules are free
[
Lam06
, Chapter V, Corollary 4.12]
,
K_{2k}(M;\Lambda)=\ker(\overline{\nu}_{*})
is finitely generated and free as desired.
The proof for
\ker(p_{2k-1}(M)_{*})
is identical.
This concludes the proof (
v
) and therefore of Lemma
9.7
.
∎

Since Lemma
9.7
implies that
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B_{2k-1}(M)
is an even, split-free normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing, the 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
is defined.
The next lemma describes it in more detail.

Proposition 9.8
.

Given positive, coprime integers
a
and
b
such that
(2k)!
divides
ab
, the 2-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
associated to
\overline{\nu}_{a,b}\colon M_{a,b}\to B_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})

is isomorphic to

\left((\Lambda,-ab)\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)}H_{+}(\Lambda)\xleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\Lambda,ab)\right).

Proof.

For ease of notation, set

\left(\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B_{2k-1}(M)\right):=\left(\overline{\nu}_{a,b}\colon M_{a,b}\to B_{2k-1}(M_{a,b})\right)
.
In Proposition
9.5
, an analogous statement was proven for the simply-connected
N:=\penalty\ N_{a,b}
by performing an explicit computation using the bases
H^{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\langle x,y\rangle
and
H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\langle\overline{x},\overline{y}\rangle
and
H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle v^{2}\rangle
, where
v:=\overline{\nu}^{-*}(z)
with
z
the generator of
H^{2}(N;\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
.
These bases led to the computation of
j_{\overline{\nu}}
, while taking the hom-dual bases led to the computation of
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}
.
Our aim is to show that these statements can be “tensored over
\Lambda
”.

We start with the computation of
j_{\overline{\nu}}
.
Applying the third and fourth assertions of Lemma
9.7
, we know that
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)=H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
and
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)=H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
.
Additionally applying the first and second isomorphisms of Lemma
9.7
leads to the identifications

\displaystyle H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)

\displaystyle\cong\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda),\Lambda)}=\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda}

\displaystyle=H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda;

\displaystyle H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)

\displaystyle\cong\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(M;\Lambda),\Lambda)}=\overline{\operatorname{Hom}_{\Lambda}(H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda}

\displaystyle=H^{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda.

Base the
\Lambda
-modules
H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)=\Lambda^{2}
and
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)=\Lambda^{2}
as well as
H^{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)=\penalty\ \Lambda
with the tensored up bases
(x\otimes 1,y\otimes 1),(\overline{x}\otimes 1,\overline{y}\otimes 1)
and
v^{k}\otimes 1
respectively, so that
z^{k}\otimes 1=a(x\otimes 1)+b(y\otimes 1)
.
The computation of
j_{\overline{\nu}}
now proceeds as in the simply-connected case:

j_{\overline{\nu}}(v^{k}\otimes 1)=\operatorname{PD}\circ\overline{\nu}^{*}(v^{k}\otimes 1)=\operatorname{PD}(z^{k}\otimes 1)=\operatorname{PD}(ax\otimes 1+by\otimes 1)=a\overline{x}\otimes 1+b\overline{y}\otimes 1.

Note that in the second equality, we use that
\overline{\nu}(M)^{*}=\overline{\nu}(N)^{*}\otimes 1
: we have
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)=H_{2k}(N;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
as well as
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)=H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(N);\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda=H_{2k}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
, and in both cases, the normal smoothings come from the Postnikov-tower induced map.

The simply-connected procedure can also be applied to compute
\iota_{\overline{\nu}}
.
Indeed, we can use Lemma
9.7
to base
H_{2k}(B_{2k-1}(M);\Lambda)
and
H_{2k}(M;\Lambda)
with the bases
v^{k}_{*}\otimes 1
and
(x_{*}\otimes 1,y_{*}\otimes 1)
that are hom-dual to the aforementioned ones on the cohomology groups.
With respect to these bases,
\overline{\nu}_{*}
is represented by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b\\
a\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Taking the kernel, we deduce that

K_{2k}(M;\Lambda)=\Lambda\langle\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\rangle
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

Let
\overline{\nu}\colon M\to B
be as in Proposition
9.8
and let
\overline{\nu}^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B
be another normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing with the same homology and intersection form as
M
.
For instance,
M^{\prime}
could be a manifold obtained by the realisation process of Corollary
7.12
.
Thinking of
H^{*}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)
as the compactly supported cohomology group
H^{*}_{c}(\widetilde{M}^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
,
we argue that the data of
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is determined by the cup product

\displaystyle\cup^{k}\colon H^{2}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)

\displaystyle\to H^{2k}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto x^{k}.

To see this, note that item (
iii
) of Remark
7.6
asserts that the data of
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is determined by the map
\overline{\nu}^{\prime*}\colon H^{2k}(B;\Lambda)\to H^{2k}(M^{\prime};\Lambda)
and use the naturality of the cup product to obtain the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{2k}(B;\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)}
\textstyle{H^{2}(B;\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\nu}^{*},\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{k}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(M^{\prime};\Lambda).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup^{k}}

The left hand side
\cup^{k}
is determined by
B
, while the bottom isomorphism is determined by choosing once and for all a generator of
H^{2}(B;\Lambda)=H^{2}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty};\Lambda)\cong H^{2}(\mathbb{C}P^{\infty})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda=\Lambda
and basing
H^{2}(M;\Lambda)=\Lambda
by the image
z
of that generator.
This shows that the data of
\textup{PE}(M^{\prime},\overline{\nu}^{\prime})
is determined by
\cup^{k}
and thus by
z^{k}\in H^{2k}(M;\Lambda)=\Lambda^{2}
.

For
b=1
we can explicitly compute the 2-sided primitive embedding and boundary automorphism associated to
\overline{\nu}_{a,1}
.

Corollary 9.10
.

If
(2k)!
divides
a
, then the
2
-sided primitive embedding
(\iota_{\overline{\nu}},j_{\overline{\nu}})
associated to the
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
\overline{\nu}_{a,1}\colon M_{a,1}\to B_{2k-1}(M_{a,1})
is isomorphic to

(\Lambda,-a)\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\Lambda,-a)\cup_{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}(\Lambda,a)\xleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
2a\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\Lambda,a).

In particular, the boundary automorphism
\delta_{\overline{\nu}}
is trivial in
\mathop{\rm\ell Aut}\nolimits(\partial(\Lambda,-a))
.

Proof.

For
b=1
, Proposition
9.8
implies that the 2-sided primitive embedding associated to
\overline{\nu}_{a,1}
is

(j_{a,1},\iota_{a,1})=\left((\Lambda,-a)\xrightarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\Lambda^{2},\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right))\xleftarrow{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}(\Lambda,a)\right).\penalty\

The quadratic form on the union
(\Lambda,-a)\cup_{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}(\Lambda,a)
is represented by
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a&0\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
A quick verification shows that the isometry
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&a\end{smallmatrix}\right)\colon H_{+}(\Lambda)\to(\Lambda,-a)\cup_{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}(\Lambda,a)
provides the desired isomorphism of 2-sided primitive embeddings:

\textstyle{(\Lambda,-a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,-a)\cup_{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}(\Lambda,a)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
2a\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,-a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-a\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{H_{+}(\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&-a\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(\Lambda,a).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{=}

The assertion on boundary isomorphisms now follows from the correspondence between 2-sided primitive embeddings and boundary isomorphisms (Theorem
6.12
).
∎

9.3.
Proof of Theorem
9.1

We can now prove Theorem
9.1
, which states the existence of infinitely many stably parallelisable

{4k}
-manifolds
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i})=\mathbb{Z}
that are stably parallelisable, have hyperbolic intersection form, equal Euler characteristic, are pairwise not stably diffeomorphic, and satisfy the following: for each
i
, there are infinitely many
{4k}
-manifolds
M_{i}^{1},M_{i}^{2},\ldots
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i}^{j})=\mathbb{Z}
that are stably parallelisable, have hyperbolic intersection form, are all stably diffeomorphic to
M_{i}
, but are pairwise not homotopy equivalent.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Fix
k\geq 2
.
Given an integer
i>0
, define integers
p=p(i):=-i(2k)!
and
q=q(i):=(1-4p^{2})>0
.
For each
i
, the closed smooth
{4k}
-manifold

M_{i}=M_{pq,1}:=N_{pq,1}\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})

is stably parallelisable and has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}
.
The description of the cohomology ring of
N_{pq,1}
from Proposition
9.3
implies that the
M_{pq,1}
are pairwise not stably diffeomorphic.
Next, consider the
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing

\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}\colon M_{pq,1}\to B_{2k-1}(M_{pq,1})=P_{2k-1}(M_{pq,1})\times BO\langle{4k}\rangle

constructed in Lemma
9.6
.
Corollary
9.10
shows that the boundary automorphism associated to
(M_{pq,1},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1})
is trivial in
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(\Lambda,-q))
.
As
pq=p(1-4p^{2})
, Theorem
5.8
shows that the boundary automorphism group
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq))
is infinite.
For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we constructed a boundary automorphism
[t_{n}]
, and we proved that the map

\mathbb{Z}\to\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq));\;\;n\mapsto[t_{n}]

is injective.
Recall from Construction
8.2
that there is an action of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi_{1}(M_{pq,1}))=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

on
\operatorname{bPrim}^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\bigcup_{[m,v,v^{\prime}]}\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
.
Use the bijection from Theorem
6.12
to pull this action back to
\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}:=\bigcup_{[m,v,v^{\prime}]}\mathop{\rm bIso}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
.
Under the bijection from Theorem
6.12
,
[t_{n}]
is sent to the class of the primitive embedding
(j_{n},j^{\prime}_{n})
from Example
6.8
. Using Construction
8.2
, we see that the action of the generator of
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\mathbb{Z})
sends
(j_{n},j^{\prime}_{n})
to
(j_{-n},j^{\prime}_{-n})
. It follows that the action of the generator
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\mathbb{Z})
on
[t_{n}]
yields
[t_{-n}]
.

Using that
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,-q))
is infinite and applying our realisation result, we will construct infinitely many homotopically inequivalent manifolds.

Corollary
7.12
implies the following: for each
[f]\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq))
, there is a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing
(M_{pq,1}^{f},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f})
such that the
{4k}
-manifold
M_{pq,1}^{f}
has hyperbolic intersection form, is stably diffeomorphic to
M_{pq,1}
, and the boundary isomorphism associated to
(M_{pq,1}^{f},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f})
is

\delta_{pq,1}^{f}=[f]\in\operatorname{bAut}_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq)).

The action by
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\pi_{1}(M_{pq,1}))=\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
will lead to some of these boundary automorphisms being identified in
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
For our infinite family of elements
\{[t_{n}]\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, we saw that the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
identifies
[t_{n}]
with
[t_{-n}]
. Therefore
\mathcal{F}:=\{[t_{n}]\mid n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
is an infinite family of elements of
\operatorname{bAut}_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq))
that are pairwise distinct in
\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
In particular, for any
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
, the
2
-sided primitive embeddings
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f_{1}},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f_{1}})
and
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f_{2}},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f_{2}})
do not agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

For a contradiction, assume that some pair of manifolds among the
M_{pq,1}^{f}
is homotopy equivalent, say
M_{pq,1}^{f_{1}}
and
M_{pq,1}^{f_{2}}
.
By Lemma
9.2
, the homotopy equivalence must be orientation preserving:
indeed each manifold
M_{pq,1}^{f}
is stably diffeomorphic to
M_{pq,1}
, a manifold with
H^{2}(M_{pq,1};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\langle z\rangle
,
z^{2k}\neq 0
, and
z^{2k}\cap[M_{pq,1}]>0
.
For an orientation preserving homotopy equivalence, Corollary
8.12
implies that
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f_{1}},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f_{1}})
and
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f_{2}},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f_{2}})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
This is a contradiction,
concluding the proof of the first part of Theorem
9.1
.

To see that
M_{i}^{j}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}
is diffeomorphic to
M_{i}^{j}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}
for any pair
\{j,j^{\prime}\}

we note that each
M_{i}^{j}
is produced from
M_{i}=M_{i}^{0}
by applying
Theorem
7.7
to some
f\in\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits_{H_{+}(\Lambda)}(\partial(\Lambda,pq))
.
The proof of Theorem
7.7
then shows that

M_{i}^{j}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}\cong M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{1}
.

To see that
M_{i}^{j}
is homotopy equivalent to
N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
for some simply-connected

4k
-manifold
N
if and only if
j=0
, we consider
the primitive embedding
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f})
,
where
[f]=[t_{n}]\in\mathop{\rm bAut}\nolimits(\partial(\Lambda,pq))
.
If there is a homotopy equivalence
M_{i}^{j}\simeq N\mathbin{\#}(S^{1}\times S^{4k-1})
then

\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f})
will be extended over the inclusion of
rings
\iota\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\Lambda
.
But by Example
6.8
,
\textup{PE}(M_{pq,1}^{f},\overline{\nu}_{pq,1}^{f})
is extended over

\iota
if and only if
n=0
; i.e. if and only if
j=0
.
∎

10.
Extended symmetric forms and the homotopy stable class

In this section we prove Theorem
1.7
. For the convenience of the reader we recall the statement.
To do this we need the following algebraic map.

Proposition 10.1
.

Consider the set
\operatorname{bES}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of isomorphism classes of extended symmetric forms
(H,\lambda,\nu\colon H\to Q)
with

H
and
Q
f.g. free,
\mathrm{rank}(H)=2\mathrm{rank}(Q)
,
\lambda
even, and
\nu
surjective.
The assignment

\big(H,\lambda,\nu\colon H\to Q\big)\mapsto\big(\ker(\nu)\xhookrightarrow{i_{\nu}}H\xhookleftarrow{j_{\nu}}Q^{*}\big)

determines a map

t\colon\operatorname{bES}^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}

from
\operatorname{bES}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to the set of isomorphism classes of 2-sided primitive embeddings
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The latter set can be thought of as the union of
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits_{m}(v,v^{\prime})
over all isomorphism classes of
m
,
v
, and
v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We consider extended symmetric forms
(H,\lambda,\nu\colon H\to Q)
where
H
and
Q
are f.g. free,
\mathrm{rank}(H)=2\mathrm{rank}(Q)
,

\lambda
even, and
\nu
surjective.
The inclusion
i_{\nu}\colon\ker(\nu)\subseteq H
and the composition
of
\nu^{*}\colon Q^{*}\to H^{*}
with
\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}\colon H^{*}\to H
(the inverse of the adjoint of
\lambda
) produce
a pair
(i_{\nu},j_{\nu}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1})
of
\Lambda
-homomorphisms that we claim fit into a
2
-sided primitive embedding

\ker(\nu)\xhookrightarrow{i_{\nu}}H\xhookleftarrow{j_{\nu}}Q^{*}.

Here we use the assumption that
\lambda
is an even symmetric form, and that the orientation character is trivial to see that
(H,\lambda)
admits a uniquely determined quadratic refinement
\theta
.
Use
i_{\nu}
and
j_{\nu}
to pull the quadratic form
\theta
back from
H
to
\ker(\nu)
and
Q^{*}
respectively.
This leads to the quadratic forms:

(\ker(\nu),\theta):=(\ker(\nu),i_{\nu}^{*}\theta i_{\nu})\text{ and }(Q^{*},\theta):=(Q^{*},j_{\nu}^{*}\theta j_{\nu}).

Observe that both
i_{\nu}
and
j_{\nu}
are injective:
i_{\nu}
is an inclusion, while for
j_{\nu}
use that the dual of a surjective map is injective.
The same verification as the one made in the proof of Lemma
7.4
implies that we have a 2-sided primitive embedding

t(H,\lambda,\nu):=\left((\ker(\nu),\theta)\xhookrightarrow{i_{\nu}}(H,\theta)\xhookleftarrow{j_{\nu}}(Q^{*},\theta)\right)

as claimed.

Next we claim that
t
is well defined on isomorphism classes of extended symmetric forms with
H
and
Q
f.g. free,

\mathrm{rank}(H)=2\mathrm{rank}(Q)
,
\lambda
even, and
\nu
surjective.
To prove this, assume that
(f,g)
is an isometry between extended symmetric forms
(H_{1},\lambda_{1},\nu_{1})
and
(H_{2},\lambda_{2},\nu_{2})
in
\operatorname{bES}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We must show that
t(H_{1},\lambda_{1},\nu_{1})
and
t(H_{2},\lambda_{2},\nu_{2})
agree in
\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By assumption, we have an isometry
f\colon(H_{1},\lambda_{1})\to(H_{2},\lambda_{2})
and an isomorphism
g\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
such that
\nu_{2}f=g\nu_{1}
.
Since
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are determined by
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
respectively, it follows that
f_{*}
also determines an isometry of the quadratic refinements.
In particular,
f
restricts to an isometry
f|\colon(\ker(\nu_{1}),\theta_{1})\to(\ker(\nu_{2}),\theta_{2})
.
Additionally, the isomorphism
g\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
induces an isomorphism
g^{*}\colon Q_{2}^{*}\to Q_{1}^{*}
and consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{(\ker(\nu_{1}),\theta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\nu_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{|}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(H_{1},\theta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(Q_{1}^{*},\theta_{1}),}
\scriptstyle{j_{\nu_{1}}}
\textstyle{(\ker(\nu_{2}),\theta_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\nu_{2}}}
\textstyle{(H_{2},\theta_{2})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(Q_{2}^{*},\theta_{2}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\nu_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}

The left square commutes because
i_{\nu_{1}}
and
i_{\nu_{2}}
are inclusion maps.
The right square is seen to commute by using successively the definition of
j_{\nu_{1}}
, the fact that
\nu_{2}f=g\nu_{1}
, the fact that
f
is an isometry, and the definition of
j_{\nu_{2}}
:

fj_{\nu_{1}}g^{*}=f(\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}\nu_{1}^{*})g^{*}=f\widehat{\lambda}_{1}^{-1}f^{*}\nu_{2}^{*}=\widehat{\lambda}_{2}^{-1}\nu_{2}^{*}=j_{\nu_{2}}.

One can then check that
g^{*}
is in fact an isometry of the quadratic forms.
This completes the proof that the map
t
is well-defined on isomorphism classes, and therefore completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

We use this map in the proof of Theorem
1.7
, whose statement we recall for the convenience of the reader.

Theorem 10.2
.

For the
4k
-dimensional manifolds
M_{i}
with
\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}
,
k\geq 2
, constructed in Section
9
for the proof of Theorem
1.1
,

\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M_{i})/L_{4k+1}(\Lambda)\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))

is surjective and has infinite image. Moreover
\mathop{\rm Aut}\nolimits(\mathbb{Z})=\{\pm 1\}
is finite, so after further identifying two extended symmetric forms related by changing the identification of the fundamental group with
\mathbb{Z}
, the image remains infinite.

Proof.

In this proof
\pi=\mathbb{Z}
and
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
.
We start by showing that the image is infinite. We will use the map
t
from Proposition
10.1
.
By inspection of the construction, if
(H,\lambda,\nu\colon H\to Q)=\overline{\lambda}(M,\overline{\nu})
then
t(H,\lambda,\nu)
coincides with the 2-sided primitive embedding corresponding to
(M,\overline{\nu})
; recall Definition
7.5
.
By Theorem
1.1
, we know that there are infinitely many manifolds
M_{i}^{1},M_{i}^{2},\ldots
each of which is stably diffeomorphic to
M_{i}
but that are pairwise not homotopy equivalent.
We detected that the
M_{i}^{j}
are not homotopy equivalent by using the Postnikov primitive embedding of
M_{i}^{j}
,
which coincides with
t(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i}^{j}))\in\mathop{\rm bPrim}\nolimits^{\mathbb{Z}}
; see Section
8.2
.
Since the
t(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i}^{j}))
are pairwise distinct, the
\overline{\lambda}(M_{i}^{j})
must be pairwise distinct in
\operatorname{bES}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This concludes the proof of the fact that
\overline{\lambda}
has infinite image.
The action of the automorphisms
\pi=\mathbb{Z}
can at most identify these elements in pairs, so indeed we have infinitely many pairwise not homotopy equivalent manifolds.

Now we show that
\overline{\lambda}
is surjective.
The proof is analogous to the proof of
[
HK88a
, Lemma 4.1]
.
Let
(H,\lambda,\nu)
be an extended symmetric form representing an element of
\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))
,
where
\overline{\nu}_{i}\colon M_{i}\to B_{i}=P_{2k-1}(M_{i})\times BO\langle 4k\rangle
is a normal
(2k{-}1)
-smoothing.
Since
(H,\lambda,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))
, there is an isometry
\alpha
and an isomorphism
\beta
that fit into the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{(H_{q}(M_{i};\Lambda),\lambda_{M_{i}})\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)^{\oplus r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{\nu}_{i}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{q}(B_{i};\Lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(H,\lambda)\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)^{\oplus r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\nu&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{Q.}

Perform
r
trivial
(2k{-}1)
surgeries on
(M_{i}
to obtain
M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{r}),\overline{\nu}_{i}\mathbin{\#}\nu_{f})
,
where
\overline{\nu}_{f}
is the trivial
B_{i}
-structure on
W_{r}

and a cobordism over
B_{i}
to
M_{i}
.
The intersection form of
M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{r}
is
(H_{q}(M_{i};\Lambda),\lambda_{M_{i}})\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)^{\oplus r}
.
Then consider

G:=\alpha^{-1}(H^{+}(\Lambda)^{\oplus r})\subseteq\lambda_{M_{i}}\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)^{\oplus r}.

This is a hyperbolic summand, since
\alpha
is an isometry.
Surgery on a collection of framed embedded
2k
-spheres in
M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{r}

representing a lagrangian for
G
produces a
4k
-manifold
M^{\prime}_{i}
with intersection pairing isomorphic to
G^{\perp}\cong(H,\lambda)
, via
\alpha|_{G^{\perp}}
. We may find such framed embedded spheres since we have a lagrangian that maps trivially to
H_{2k}(B_{i};\Lambda)
, under the normal smoothing
\overline{\nu}_{i}
. Also note that the vanishing of the intersection form implies the vanishing of the quadratic enhancement.
We have that
\beta\circ(\overline{\nu}_{i}\oplus 0)(G)=\{0\}
by commutativity of the diagram. Hence

\textstyle{G^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha|_{G^{\perp}}}
\scriptstyle{\beta\circ\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\overline{\nu}_{i}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\textstyle{(H,\lambda)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{Q}

commutes and the extended symmetric form of
M^{\prime}_{i}
is isomorphic to
(H,\lambda,\nu)
as desired.
Since the second set of surgeries is performed on spheres which lie in the kernel of the map

\overline{\nu}_{i}\mathbin{\#}\overline{\nu}_{r}\colon H_{2k}(M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{r});\Lambda)\to H_{2k}(B_{i};\Lambda)
, the trace of the surgeries is a cobordism over
B_{i}
. The map to
B_{i}
factors through the normal
(2k{-}1)
-type
P_{2k-1}(M_{i})\times BO\langle 2k\rangle
, so we deduce that
M_{i}
and
M^{\prime}_{i}
are bordant over their
(2k{-}1)
type and have the same Euler characteristic. It then follows from
[
Kre99
, Theorem C]
that
M_{i}
and
M^{\prime}_{i}
are stably diffeomorphic,
so we have
M^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M_{i})
and therefore
\overline{\lambda}(M_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))
. Alternatively one can directly see from the construction of
M^{\prime}_{i}
that it stabilises to
M_{i}\mathbin{\#}W_{r}
.
This completes the proof that
\overline{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}_{+}(M_{i})\to\mathcal{S}^{\rm st}(\overline{\lambda}(M_{i},\overline{\nu}_{i}))
is surjective.
∎
